# The Comprehensive Irad Wiki Encyclopedia (Volume 3: Start and Stub Articles)

The **Irad Wiki** is a collaboratively edited encyclopedia of the Republic of Irad (Iradistan). This volume collects start-class and stub articles: shorter entries on settlements, landforms, institutions, people, wildlife and vocabulary, many of which await expansion by editors.

---

## 5. Start-Class Articles (254 Entries)

### 5.Category: Biographies & Leadership (36 articles)

#### 1. Mar Timotheos Alimbekov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Mar Timotheos Alimbekov** (1869–1938) was a bishop of the Iradi church who negotiated its union with Rome. Under the bull *Orientalis Stella*, issued by [[wp:Pope Pius XI]] on September 14, 1931, the church was received into full communion as the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], an Eastern Catholic church *sui iuris* that kept its East Syriac rite, and Iyık Janyl became its metropolitan see. Timotheos became its first archbishop. He was arrested during [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges|the purges of 1937]] and, according to church records, died in custody the following year.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Early life and episcopate
Timotheos was born in [[Iyık Janyl]] and studied [[wp:Syriac language|Classical Syriac]] and scripture with the clergy of the [[Church of St. Janyl]]. Church biographies state that as a young man he spent several years among the East Syriac communities of Urmia in Persia, where he met Catholic missionaries. He was consecrated bishop of Iyık Janyl before the [[wp:World War I|First World War]] and led the church through the 1916 revolt and the [[wp:Russian Civil War]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Union with Rome
Soviet rule made open contact with Rome impossible. From 1926 Timotheos corresponded with the [[wp:Holy See]] through intermediaries at papal nunciatures abroad; church histories name contacts in [[wp:Istanbul]] and Tehran. His surviving letters, held in the diocesan archives, ask for recognition of the church's Syriac liturgy, its synodal government and its own calendar, including the feast of St. Janyl. The negotiations lasted five years and ended with the bull of 1931. The bull was not published in the [[wp:Soviet Union]], and news of it reached the parishes by word of mouth.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Soviet response and death
Soviet authorities treated the union as evidence that the church answered to a foreign power. Officials linked the Vatican, which had concluded the [[wp:Lateran Treaty]] with [[wp:Italy]] in 1929, with "fascist" influence, and historians connect this suspicion with the severity of anti-religious repression in the Iradi SSR during the 1930s. The churches of Iyık Janyl were closed and the cathedral became a grain store. Timotheos was arrested in 1937 together with his senior clergy, most of whom were executed. The security service file on his case has not been published, and his place of burial is unknown.<sup>[2]</sup>

The Church of St. Janyl was reconsecrated on September 14, 1992, the anniversary of the bull, and Timotheos is commemorated in Iyık Janyl on that date. The historian and politician [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] was a member of the same family.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### 2. Tilek Kurmanov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Tilek Kurmanov** (born 1953) is an Iradi diplomat who represented the government of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) at the United Nations from 2008 to 2017. Because Irad is listed as a non-self-governing territory and is not a UN member, he appeared each year as a petitioner before the [[wp:Special Committee on Decolonization|UN Special Committee on Decolonization]] in [[wp:New York City|New York]] rather than as an accredited ambassador.<sup>[1]</sup>

A graduate of the [[wp:Moscow State Institute of International Relations]] (1976), Kurmanov served in the Soviet foreign service before returning to [[Talas]] in 1992. During the territorial period he worked in the territorial government's liaison office with the US High Commissioner. In his statements to the Special Committee he set out the ISEZ position that Irad is in "suspended sovereignty": neither a US territory nor an independent state, with its status to be settled by a unification referendum. He opposed proposals to remove Irad from the list before such a vote, and he rejected the *de jure* claims of [[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Uzbekistan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]]. Petitioners from the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] presented a competing view at the same sessions (see [[Political status of Irad]]).<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 3. Gulnara Sadykova
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Gulnara Sadykova** (born 1974) is an Iradi human rights lawyer based in [[Talas]]. She founded the Legal Defense Guild, which gives free legal representation to journalists, students and others detained in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], and her reports on political detentions are widely cited by international monitors.<sup>[1]</sup>

Sadykova studied law at [[Talas State University]] and took a master's degree in human rights law at Central European University in Budapest in 2001. She founded the Legal Defense Guild in Talas in 2009. After the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]], the guild represented more than sixty detained students; most were released without charge by the end of November 2023. Its reports on pre-trial detention have been cited by the human rights unit of the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] and by [[wp:Human Rights Watch]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> In 2021 the Ministry of Internal Security audited the guild's foreign grants. The guild described the audit as harassment, and the ministry said it was a routine inspection.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### 4. Muratbek Tanıp-Biy
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

{{Hatnote|Not to be confused with [[Muratbek Biy]], an 18th-century clan leader.}}

**Muratbek Tanıp-Biy** (c. 1805 – 1874) was a chieftain (*biy*) of the Tanıp clan in the [[Talas Alatau]]. He led the northern Iradi clans during the mid-19th-century frontier conflicts between the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]] and the [[wp:Russian Empire]], and he is remembered for negotiating pasture settlements between rival clans. According to the Tanıp genealogy (*[[Sanjyra (Genealogy)|sanjyra]]*), the present chieftain, [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], descends from him in the male line.<sup>[1]</sup>

Most of what is known about him comes from the *sanjyra* and from a few Russian frontier reports of the 1860s that mention "Muratbek, biy of the Tanyp". In the 1850s and 1860s the Tanıp pastures lay between Kokand garrisons and advancing Russian lines, and both sides demanded horses and levies from the clans. Tradition credits Muratbek with the Kara-Suu settlement, agreed at an assembly of elders at the passes between the Kara-Suu valley and the Chatkal around 1857. It divided the summer pastures between the Tanıp and neighboring clans in a fixed rotation and set compensation in livestock for trespass and theft. Clan councils and the [[Autonomous Tribal Grazing Arbitration Tribunal]] still cite the settlement as a precedent in disputes under customary law (*adat*).<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 5. David Sterling
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**David Sterling** (born 1946) is a retired [[wp:United States Army]] colonel who was the first US military liaison officer in [[Talas]] during [[Operation Starlight]] in 1992.<sup>[1]</sup>

A Russian-speaking foreign area officer, Sterling arrived with the first [[wp:United States Marine Corps|US Marines]] airlifted into the airfield west of Talas on June 6, 1992, during the final weeks of the siege of the city. He coordinated the defense of the perimeter between the Marines, Provisional Government units and the mounted clan irregulars of [[Kuban Mamytov]] and [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]] until the siege lines were broken by June 18. Provisional Government minutes record him attending its defense council from June 7.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> He then headed the US military liaison office in Talas until 1994 and worked on the first plans to expand [[Talas Air Base]] into a permanent installation. He retired from the Army in 1998.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 6. Kanybek Zhumaliev
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Kanybek Zhumaliev** (1947–2016) was an Iradi lawyer and politician who was Speaker of the Territorial House of Representatives from 1994 to 1998. He sponsored the Commercial Property and Tenure Acts of 1995.<sup>[1]</sup>

A collective-farm lawyer in the Talas valley in the Soviet period, Zhumaliev joined the reformist caucus of the Provisional Government in 1992 and was a founding member of the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] that October. He was elected to the House of Representatives in the first territorial elections in 1994 and chosen as Speaker. The 1995 acts protected household livestock and family grazing rights from seizure for debt, and they required foreign joint ventures to register land leases and ownership stakes with the territorial government. Herders' groups later argued that the acts protected household herds but left communal clan pastures without legal protection, a gap that became an issue in the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 7. Chingiz Asanov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Chingiz Asanov** (born 1963) is an Iradi mining engineer who has been Minister of Natural Resources of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] since 2016. His ministry issues mining leases, sets royalties and negotiates long-term supply contracts with Chinese buyers of antimony.<sup>[1]</sup>

Asanov trained as a mining engineer at the Frunze Polytechnic Institute and worked at the [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]] from 1985, becoming its chief engineer in 2001. He was appointed deputy minister in 2009. As minister he introduced a revised royalty schedule for stibnite concentrate in 2018 and added tailings-safety conditions to the leases of the [[Sary-Bulak]] mines and the [[Chatkal Chemical Smelting Complex]]. Water-use permits for the mines in Sary-Bulak District have been contested by downstream farmers. Licenses his ministry has issued in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] cannot be used there, because the clan councils ban open-pit mining in mountain watersheds.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 8. Altynai Bekzhanova
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Altynai Bekzhanova** (1903–1979) was an Iradi teacher who opened the first Iradi-language secondary schools in Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]) in the 1930s and wrote primers for the Latin-script Iradi alphabet introduced in 1928.<sup>[1]</sup>

Born in a village in the Talas valley, Bekzhanova trained as a teacher in [[wp:Tashkent]] in the 1920s. Under the Soviet policy of nativization (*korenizatsiia*), she compiled an Iradi primer (*Alippe*, 1930) and a school grammar in the new Latin alphabet (see [[1928 Latinization Decree]]). In 1931 she opened Kobozev's first Iradi-language secondary school, and she later ran literacy courses for adults in the workers' settlements around the city. Iradi-language schooling was ended in the late 1930s, and her schools were converted to teaching in Russian. She continued to teach in Kobozev until 1963.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 9. Samat Kasymov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Samat Kasymov** (born 1968) is an Iradi telecommunications engineer who has been Director-General of [[Attelkom]], the largest telecommunications company in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], since 2013.<sup>[1]</sup>

Kasymov studied radio engineering at the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]] and joined Attelkom in 1997 as an engineer on its first GSM network in [[Talas]]. He became its technical director in 2008. Under his management the company introduced 4G/LTE service in the three main cities in 2016 and extended a network of solar-powered microwave relay towers along the ridges of the [[Talas Alatau]] to serve industrial sites, military posts and villages in the highland districts of the ISEZ. Attelkom has almost no service in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].<sup>[1]</sup> In October 2023 the company cut mobile data in central Talas for three days during the student protests. Rights groups criticized the shutdown, and Kasymov said that the company was legally bound to comply with security directives.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 10. Biybolot Moldosh uulu
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Biybolot Moldosh uulu** (born 1944; recorded in Soviet documents as Biybolot Moldoshev) is an elder (*[[Aksakal (Social Term)|aksakal]]*) of the Karakoyun clan of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] and a mediator in disputes along the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]].<sup>[1]</sup>

He comes from the [[Kara-Koyun Valley]], the clan's ancestral homeland, and is known for his knowledge of the clans' oral genealogies (*[[Sanjyra (Genealogy)|sanjyra]]*). He was a member of the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] delegation at the [[2011 Bilateral Reunification Summit]], which walked out after the tribal regions were offered one-third of the seats in a unified legislature. Since 2008 he has attended the monthly meetings of the Joint Liaison Committee at Kara-Oi as an adviser to the tribal delegation. In 2014 he negotiated the release of three ISEZ survey workers held by [[El-Choro Militias|El-Choro]] patrols in the Kara-Koyun Valley.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 11. Mar Ephrem
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Mar Ephrem** (born 1955) is the Archbishop of [[Iyık Janyl]] and primate of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]. He was elected by the [[Syro-Iradi Episcopal Synod]] in 2012, confirmed by the [[wp:Holy See]] and enthroned at the [[Church of St. Janyl]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Born in the Talas valley, he was ordained in secret in 1981, when the church still had no legal existence in the Iradi SSR. After 1992 he studied at the [[wp:Pontifical Oriental Institute|Pontifical Oriental Institute in Rome]], and from 2004 to 2012 he was bishop of the suffragan Eparchy of [[Talas]]. As archbishop he presides over the synod and the church's four eparchies. He has kept the archbishopric neutral between the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] administration and the clan councils of the tribal regions, and Iyık Janyl has hosted inter-confessional meetings and talks between the two sides during his tenure. He has also maintained the itinerant priests who celebrate the liturgy in tent chapels in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 12. Dastan Isakov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Dastan Isakov** (born 1946) is an Iradi jurist who was Chief Justice of the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] from 1999 to 2007. He wrote the majority opinion in the [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)|Statutory Independence Ruling]] of December 2006, which led to the proclamation of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Career
Isakov graduated from the law faculty of Kazakh State University in [[wp:Almaty|Alma-Ata]] in 1969 and worked as a state arbitration lawyer in Kobozev. He was among the first justices appointed to the Supreme Court when it was established under the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]] in November 1992. In 1999 the Governor named him Chief Justice with the consent of the Territorial Senate.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Statutory Independence Ruling
On December 3, 2006, President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] by executive action. Within days, on an emergency petition brought by [[Bakyt Orozbekov]], the court held that territorial status had lapsed upon federal disengagement. Isakov's majority opinion reasoned that the treaty's statutory independence clause, carried into the Organic Act, had then taken effect and vested sovereignty in the people of Irad. The reasoning became known as the doctrine of automatic reversion. On the strength of the ruling, the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the Democratic Republic of Irad on December 9.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

On February 25, 2007, [[Erlan Toktarov]] proclaimed the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] and annulled the ruling by decree. The court never ruled on the annulment. After DPI forces took control of [[Talas]] in the summer of 2007, the emergency administration reconstituted the bench, and Isakov left the court that year. He has held no public office since and has given few interviews.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 13. Elena Petrova
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Elena Petrova** (born 1957) is a trade unionist and municipal politician in [[Nurzhan]] who represents the city's Russian- and Ukrainian-speaking industrial workforce. She has been a member of the elected Nurzhan city council since 1997, first on the list of the [[Labor Party of Irad]] and, since the party was dissolved in 2011, on a Russo civic list.<sup>[1]</sup>

Petrova worked as a crane operator at the Kobozev Tractor Combine from 1976 and became a shop-floor organizer in its trade union in the late 1980s. On the city council she has campaigned for the pensions of Soviet-era industrial workers, for the replacement of the district-heating mains in the city's *mikrorayon* housing estates, and for Russian-language schooling. Aligned with the Russophone *Russo* bloc, she has opposed the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] administration over language policy and over the city's share of oil revenue (see [[Russos of Nurzhan]]). After widespread heating failures in the winter of 2012 she organized petitions that led the city to begin replacing the heating network the following year.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 14. Mirlan Toktarov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Mirlan Toktarov** (born 1983) is an Iradi businessman and a nephew of Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]], the son of the Governor's younger brother. Since 2014 he has been managing director of the [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]], which handles pedigree certification and the air shipment of horses abroad.<sup>[1]</sup>

Toktarov studied business administration in [[Talas]] and worked in the family's freight interests before joining the syndicate in 2009 as head of its export division. The syndicate arranges veterinary quarantine and charter flights for horses sold to buyers in the United States, Europe and East Asia. Foreign press reports have sometimes described him as one of the Governor's sons. The Governor's children are his five sons, Ruslan, Alikhan, [[wp:Timur]], Adil and Bekzat, and his daughter Zara; Mirlan is their cousin.<sup>[1]</sup> Critics of the administration cite his appointment, together with the business roles of the Governor's sons, as an example of the family's influence over the ISEZ economy.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 15. Kuban Mamytov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Kuban Mamytov** (1949–2013), also known by the patronymic form Kuban Mamyt uulu, was a commander of mounted clan irregulars in the [[First Iradi Civil War]]. During the siege of [[Talas]] in 1992 his riders and those of [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]] raided supply convoys of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] and ambushed its armored columns in the mountain passes. Veterans called him "the Ghost of Chatkal".<sup>[1]</sup>

Mamytov was a herder from the Chatkal valley and a veteran of the [[wp:Soviet Army]]. During the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] he led riders from the Chatkal clans into Talas. When socialist forces besieged the city from April 1992, the Provisional Government placed him in command of the mounted irregulars defending its southern approaches. His men attacked depots along the railway and kept open the mountain tracks by which supplies reached the city from the southern valleys.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> Military historians regard the irregulars' contribution as secondary to the US intervention, [[Operation Starlight]], which broke the siege in June 1992.<sup>[2]</sup>

After US forces disarmed the militias in August 1992, Mamytov returned to herding in the Chatkal, and in 1994 he declined a commission in the newly organized Irad National Guard. At his funeral in 2013 his horse was led behind the bier with its saddle reversed, in keeping with the [[Fallen Rider Tradition]].<sup>[3]</sup>

#### 17. Nikolai Voronov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

{{Hatnote|Not to be confused with the Soviet artillery commander [[wp:Nikolay Voronov|Nikolai Nikolayevich Voronov]].}}

**Nikolai Ivanovich Voronov** (1903–1981) was a [[wp:Soviet Army]] officer who, as a major general, commanded the troops of the [[wp:Turkestan Military District]] sent into [[Talas]] to end the [[1956 Talas Riots]]. He served as military commandant of the city under the martial law and curfew declared on June 17, 1956.<sup>[1]</sup>

Voronov's units entered Talas on the morning of June 17, the fourth day of the riots, with internal troops flown in to the air-defense airfield outside the city. Armored vehicles were posted at the main intersections around Government Square, and the last crowds dispersed on June 18. Internal security reports listed 14 dead; Iradi researchers put the number at several dozen. More than 1,000 people were detained in the following weeks.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> He retired from the army in 1963. In memoirs published in 1990, Voronov acknowledged that the use of troops and the mass detentions that followed had turned much of the Iradi population of the city against the authorities.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 18. Azamat Temirov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Azamat Temirov** (born 1969) is an Iradi economist who has been chairman of the [[Bank of Irad]], the monetary authority of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], since 2010.<sup>[1]</sup>

Under the Banking and Commercial Currency Act of 1993, the US dollar is legal tender in Irad and the [[Iradi som]] circulates alongside it for retail payments, utility bills and public-sector wages. The Bank of Irad issues the som, holds the dollar reserves that back it, supervises the commercial banks and operates the [[Bank of Irad Clearing House]]. Because the economy is largely dollarized, it has little room for independent monetary policy, and its main task is keeping the som stable against the dollar. Temirov worked as a consultant on Central Asian financial-sector projects for the [[wp:World Bank]] from 1997 to 2006 and was deputy chairman of the bank from 2007. Since non-recognition limits Irad's access to international clearing systems, he has overseen the correspondent banking arrangements in [[wp:Almaty]] and [[wp:Istanbul]] on which Iradi banks rely.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 19. Kanatbek Niyaz uulu
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Kanatbek Niyaz uulu** (born 1950; recorded in Soviet documents as Kanatbek Niyazov) is a customary-law judge who presides over hearings under *[[Adat (Customary Law)|adat]]* in the high valleys of the Chatkal range, in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Clan councils in the Chatkal Highlands refer disputes over livestock, pasture boundaries and grazing rotations to him. He also sits as one of the elders of the [[Autonomous Tribal Grazing Arbitration Tribunal]]. His judgments are given orally and usually order restitution in livestock (*kun*) rather than punishment. In 2016 he settled a dispute between two clans over shared summer pastures with a rotation agreement that both clan councils accepted.<sup>[1]</sup> Human rights organizations have criticized the *adat* system in which he works, noting that women have limited standing before clan councils and that there is no appeal beyond the clan assembly.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 20. Fatima Yunusova
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Fatima Yunusova** (born 1960) is an Iradi agronomist who introduced drip irrigation to dryland farms in the lower [[Chu River Basin|Chu basin]]. She chairs a farmers' cooperative in Chu District.<sup>[1]</sup>

Yunusova trained at the Tashkent Institute of Irrigation and worked for the district water administration before setting up the cooperative in 2003. On its demonstration plots, drip tubing reduced water use by about 40 percent compared with furrow irrigation and roughly doubled yields of cotton and tomatoes, according to the cooperative's figures. Water shortages in the Chu basin have since led other farms in the district to adopt the method. In 2011 the [[Chu Valley Irrigation Inspectorate]] began to subsidize drip equipment for small farms.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 21. Oleg Kim
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Oleg Kim** (1929–2004) was a [[wp:Koryo-saram]] engineer who directed the Kobozev Tractor Combine in Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]) from 1969 to 1984.<sup>[1]</sup>

Kim was born in the [[wp:Russian Far East|Soviet Far East]]. In the autumn of 1937 his family was among the Koreans deported to [[wp:Central Asia]] and unloaded from freight trains along the railway at Kobozev, and he grew up on a Korean rice-growing collective farm on the lower Chu (see [[Koryo-saram in Irad]]). He graduated from the Kobozev Polytechnic Institute (now the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]]) in 1952 and spent his career at the tractor combine. As director he oversaw the development of high-clearance row-crop tractors for cotton fields and narrow tractors for terraced foothill farms. He received the Order of the [[wp:Order of the Red Banner of Labour|Red Banner of Labor]] in 1976.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 22. Zamirbek Bakiev
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Zamirbek Bakiev** (born 1970) is an Iradi politician and farmer. As a student he led the occupation of Central Square in [[Talas]] during the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]], and in 1994 he was elected to the first Territorial Senate.<sup>[1]</sup>

A history student at [[Talas State University]], Bakiev led the students who occupied Central Square in January 1992, during the strikes that followed the railway workers' walkout in Kobozev. He later sat on the youth committee of the Provisional Government. In the Territorial Senate, where he served from 1994 to 2000, he sponsored the 1996 act that gave public universities self-government over appointments and curricula. After leaving politics he organized farming cooperatives in [[Kara-Buura]] District in the lower Talas valley.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 23. Luka Ayyubov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Luka Ayyubov** (1939–2011) was a priest of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] and a linguist who translated the [[wp:New Testament]] into Latin-script Iradi. His translation was published in 1994.<sup>[1]</sup>

Ayyubov was ordained in secret in 1966 and worked as a schoolteacher in [[Iyık Janyl]] while serving the clandestine parishes of the Soviet period. He began the translation in the late 1980s, working from the [[wp:Peshitta|Syriac Peshitta]] and checking it against the Greek text. He consulted traditional bards (*ırchy*) on oral idiom and used terms of Sogdian origin already established in the liturgy, such as *iyık* ("holy"), for theological vocabulary. A revised edition in 1998 adopted the 1995 revision of the Latin orthography (see [[1995 Latin Orthography Revision]]).<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 24. Sergei Nechayev (Iradi Influence)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Sergei Nechayev** (Sergey Gennadiyevich Nechayev, 1847–1882) was a Russian revolutionary associated with the *Catechism of a Revolutionary* (1869), a manifesto of conspiratorial revolution whose authorship is sometimes shared with [[wp:Mikhail Bakunin]]. He died in prison in St. [[wp:Saint Petersburg|Petersburg]]. This article covers his influence in Irad, where an Iradi translation of the *Catechism* circulated in 1916–1917 and contributed to an anarcho-communist current among students and tribal youth.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## In Irad
The Iradi translation was an abridged version of the *Catechism*, probably made from a Russian text brought from [[wp:Tashkent]]. The translator is unknown. The earliest surviving copy, a lithographed pamphlet in Arabic-script Iradi, is held in the [[State Archives of Irad]]. It circulated among students in [[Talas]] and [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]] and among young men of the clans during and after the [[Semirechye Revolt (Irad)|revolt of 1916]]. Its readers combined Nechayev's call for revolutionary discipline with the clans' traditions of communal decision-making and resistance to empire.<sup>[2]</sup>

After the [[wp:October Revolution]] most Iradi clans sided with the [[wp:Bolsheviks]], and Iradi irregulars fought alongside the [[wp:Red Guards (Russia)|Red Guards]] who crushed the [[wp:Turkestan Autonomy|Kokand Autonomy]] in February 1918. Soviet historiography later condemned "Nechayevism" as an anarchist deviation, and the Iradi anarcho-communist current was absorbed into the party or suppressed during the 1920s. Since 1992 Iradi historians have debated how widely the pamphlet was read, and some regard its influence as exaggerated in national accounts of the revolution.<sup>[2]</sup>

</ol>

#### 25. Bermet Alimbekova
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Bermet Alimbekova** (born 1962) is an Iradi economist who advised the territorial government on trade and foreign investment from 1997 to 2004.<sup>[1]</sup>

Alimbekova studied economics in [[wp:Tashkent]] and, after 1992, took a doctorate at [[wp:Georgetown University]] on a territorial scholarship, completing it in 1997. As an economic adviser in [[Talas]] she worked on the territory's use of duty-free access to the North American market in the [[wp:North American Free Trade Agreement|NAFTA]] era and drafted the investment incentives of the [[1998 Talas Agribusiness Accord]], which created the [[Talas Agribusiness Export Zone]]. The zone attracted foreign beef processors, including the [[wp:Sukarne]] and [[wp:Conagra Brands|Conagra]] facilities near Talas. She also advised on the 1999 share auction that privatized [[Attelkom]]. Since 2008 she has taught economics at [[Talas State University]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 26. Talgat Saparov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Talgat Saparov** (born 1966) is an Iradi customs official who has been chief security commissioner of the [[Talas Free Economic Trade Zone]] since 2012. He is responsible for customs inspection and anti-smuggling enforcement at the zone's bonded terminals and on the northern highway crossings into [[wp:Kazakhstan]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Saparov served in the Soviet border troops and the territorial-era Irad National Guard before joining the customs service in 1998. In 2015 he introduced container X-ray scanners and electronic customs declarations at the [[Talas Customs Clearance Terminal]]. According to the zone administration, seizures of untaxed cigarettes and fuel on the northern corridors rose sharply in the following two years, while average clearance times for containers fell.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 27. Keneshbek Arstanov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Keneshbek Arstanov** (born 1951) is an Iradi horse breeder who bred *[[Zharkyn (Horse)|Zharkyn]]*, the golden-dun [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] stallion owned by Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Arstanov worked at the state stud near [[Talas]] from 1974 and set up his own stud farm in the Talas valley in 1993. He breeds Akyel horses with palomino and golden-dun coats with the metallic sheen (*doran*) valued in the breed, and his horses are registered in the National Akyel Studbook kept by the [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]]. Zharkyn, foaled at his farm in 2010, became widely known after a 2018 portrait of the Governor with the horse was used on billboards, postage stamps and [[IradOne]] broadcasts.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 28. Aizhan Kulova
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Aizhan Kulova** (born 1961) is an Iradi physician who has organized childhood immunization campaigns in remote mountain settlements of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR).<sup>[1]</sup>

Kulova graduated from the medical faculty of [[Talas State University]] in 1985 and worked as a pediatrician in [[Talas]]. In 2008 she became head of public health programs at the [[St. Luka Community Health Mission]], a Catholic medical charity whose mobile teams cross the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] along seasonal corridors negotiated through the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]]. Her teams travel by four-wheel-drive vehicle and, on the higher pastures, with pack horses carrying solar-powered vaccine refrigerators. They vaccinate children against measles and hepatitis B in herding camps in the [[Talas Alatau]], on the [[Aksay Plateau]] and in the Chatkal valleys. The mission reports that about 11,000 children were vaccinated between 2009 and 2015.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 29. Bolotbek Daniyarov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Bolotbek Daniyarov** (born 1964) is an Iradi journalist who has been managing editor of the *Irad Times*, an independent English-language weekly published in [[Talas]], since 2001.<sup>[1]</sup>

The *Irad Times* was founded in 1996, during the territorial period. In December 2006 and January 2007, under Daniyarov's editorship, it published internal documents on the planned sale of state oil, grain and mining assets by the government of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]]. The reporting preceded the protests over the [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]]. On February 26, 2007, the paper printed in a special edition the partition drafts that [[Erlan Toktarov]] had released the day before (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]).<sup>[1]</sup> Since 2007 the paper has operated under the media rules of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. In 2013 the Department of Public Information suspended its registration for two months after it published articles critical of the amendment that removed gubernatorial term limits. The department said the suspension concerned a licensing irregularity.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 30. Emil Bekzatov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Emil Bekzatov** (born 1975) is an Iradi oil executive, executive vice-president of [[NurGaz]] and the eldest son of its president, [[Kamran Bekzatov]]. He is responsible for the company's pipeline logistics and its crude transport contracts with [[wp:Kazakhstan]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Bekzatov studied petroleum economics at the [[wp:Gubkin Russian State University of Oil and Gas|Gubkin Institute in Moscow]], graduating in 1997, and joined NurGaz the following year. He has overseen the operation of the [[Northern Export Feeder]] since it opened in 2000. The 85-km pipeline carries crude from the NurGaz tank farm outside [[Nurzhan]] to the [[wp:KazTransOil]] network near [[wp:Shymkent]], where it is refined or shipped on to Russia. He negotiates the transit and refining arrangements on the Kazakh side. His younger siblings, [[Rustam Bekzatov]] and [[Asel Bekzatova]], also work in the family's business and philanthropy.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 31. Aigul Choro kyzy
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Aigul Choro kyzy** (1936–2012) was an Iradi ethnomusicologist who recorded horseback songs (*[[Irda (Vocal Tradition)|ırda]]*) and epic recitations across rural Irad.<sup>[1]</sup>

Born in the Talas valley, she studied at the conservatory in [[wp:Tashkent]] and joined the folklore department of the Iradi SSR Academy of Sciences in 1961. Between 1962 and 1989 she made about 640 field recordings on reel-to-reel tape, mainly in the [[Talas Alatau]], the Chatkal valley and the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] foothills. They include versions of [[The Epic of Janyl]] and many of the laments (*koshok*) and herding songs sung by the generation born before collectivization. Her transcriptions, published in 1978 and in an expanded edition in 1995, analyze the improvised structure of bardic performance and the tunings used for the [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]] in accompaniment. The recordings are held in the [[State Archives of Irad]]. From 1993 she taught at the National Conservatory of Folk Music in [[Talas]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 32. Ruslan Kadyrov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Ruslan Kadyrov** (born 1971) is a former Iradi gendarmerie officer who, as a major, went over to the [[Popular Front of Irad]] with his unit during the [[Storming of Talas (2007)|storming of Talas]] on June 4, 2007.<sup>[1]</sup>

When mounted Front columns entered [[Talas]], Kadyrov ordered the gendarmes under his command to stand down and opened the municipal armories to the Front's riders. Front histories credit his decision with hastening the collapse of the defense of the government district, where the cabinet of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] was dissolved the same day. ISEZ histories describe it as desertion.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> Kadyrov withdrew with the Front when DPI forces took control of the city that summer. The ISEZ authorities issued a warrant for his arrest, and he has lived in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] since 2007.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 33. Klara Zhumabek kyzy
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Klara Zhumabek kyzy** (born 1972) is a community organizer in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR) who founded and leads a network of women's felt-making cooperatives. She is the younger sister of the Tanıp chieftain [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]]; their father was Zhumabek Asanaly uulu (1934–2001).<sup>[1]</sup>

She grew up in the upper Kara-Suu valley. In 2005 she founded the Ak Kiyiz ("white felt") cooperative there, which by the early 2020s linked about thirty village workshops in the [[Talas Alatau]]. Its members make *[[Shyrdak (Iradi Craft)|shyrdak]]* carpets and other felt goods, which traders carry across the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] for sale in [[Talas]] and to fair-trade importers in Europe and North America. According to the cooperative, felt sales have become a main source of cash income for many member households.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> She has also spoken publicly in favor of women's participation in clan assemblies.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 34. Tolonbek Sydykov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Tolonbek Sydykov** (born 1949) is an Iradi saddler (*eerchi*) whose workshop in the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] makes decorated wooden saddles with chased-silver pommels and tooled leather fittings (see [[Eerchilik (Saddlery)]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

Sydykov learned the craft from his father and belongs to the citadel's hereditary leatherworkers' guild. Since the late 1990s the territorial government and then the ISEZ administration have commissioned saddles from his workshop as official gifts for foreign delegations. One of his saddles, made in 2004, is in the collection of the [[National Historical Museum of Irad]]. He has trained more than a dozen apprentices.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 35. Artem Kozlov
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Artem Kozlov** (born 1970) is an Iradi metallurgical engineer who has been chief engineer of the [[Chatkal Chemical Smelting Complex]] in Sary-Bulak District since 2014. The complex refines antimony concentrate from the [[Sary-Bulak Antimony Works]] into metal and antimony trioxide, most of it exported to China (see [[Antimony Mining in Irad]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

Kozlov graduated in metallurgy from the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]] in 1992 and joined the complex the following year. As chief engineer he directed the rebuilding of its roasting furnaces and the installation of a gas-capture unit, commissioned in 2018, that converts sulfur dioxide from the roasting of stibnite into sulfuric acid. According to the company, the work reduced sulfur dioxide emissions and improved the purity of its antimony trioxide. Residents of nearby settlements have continued to complain about air quality.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 36. Pyotr Kobozev
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Pyotr Alekseyevich Kobozev** (Russian: *Пётр Алексеевич Кобозев*; 1878–1941) was a Russian Bolshevik revolutionary and railway engineer. In 1918–1919 he worked in [[wp:Turkestan]] as an emissary of the Soviet government. From 1927 to 1992 the city of [[Nurzhan]], capital of the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]], was named Kobozev after him.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Life
Kobozev trained as a railway engineer and joined the [[wp:Russian Social Democratic Labour Party|Russian Social Democratic Labor Party]] around the turn of the century, later siding with its Bolshevik wing. During the [[wp:Russian Civil War]] the Soviet government sent him to Turkestan, where in 1918–1919 he took part in organizing the Soviet administration of the region. He died in 1941.<sup>[1]</sup>

## The city of Kobozev
The city grew from a railway settlement on the lower Chu, where the [[First Congress of Iradi Soviets]] met in 1925. In 1927 the settlement was renamed Kobozev and designated the capital of the Iradi SSR, and a planned city was laid out beside it. Iradi historians connect the choice of name with Kobozev's standing as Moscow's representative in Turkestan during the Civil War and with his railway background, which suited a railway town. Naming cities after living Soviet officials was common at the time.<sup>[2]</sup>

The name was carried by several institutions and events of the Soviet period, among them the Kobozev Polytechnic Institute (now the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]]), the Kobozev Heavy Machine Plant (now the [[Nurzhan Heavy Machine Plant]]), the [[1952 Kobozev Steel Strike]] and [[The Kobozev Bread Riots (1989)]]. In 1991–1992 the city was the seat of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]]. After its capture in August 1992, [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] renamed it Nurzhan ("light of life") in September 1992, and the Territorial Legislature confirmed the change in the Toponymic Heritage Statute of 1993 (see [[De-Russification Movement in Irad]]). Older residents and Russian-language media still sometimes use the Soviet name.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

#### 37. Baktygul Omurova
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Biographies*

**Baktygul Omurova** (born 1952) is an Iradi radio presenter who hosted the evening folklore program *Kechki Jomok* ("Evening Tales") on [[IradOne]] radio from 1994 to 2020.<sup>[1]</sup>

Omurova began her career in 1975 as an announcer for the republican radio service in Kobozev. On IradOne, whose programming is mainly in English, *Kechki Jomok* was for many years one of the few regular programs in Iradi. It broadcast recitations by traditional bards (*ırchy*), instrumental pieces (*küü*) for the [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]] and listeners' accounts of family genealogies, and its archive of studio recordings is held by the broadcaster. Its audience reached herding settlements on both sides of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

### 5.Category: Culture & Music (1 article)

#### 38. Iyık Janyl Seminary Choir
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

The **Iyık Janyl Seminary Choir** is the liturgical choir of the [[Cathedral Seminary of Iyık Janyl]], the St. Ephrem Major Seminary of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]. It was formed in 1994, when the seminary reopened, and sings the East Syriac chant of the [[wp:Holy Qurbana]] and Iradi hymns at the [[Church of St. Janyl]] in [[Iyık Janyl]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## History and repertoire
The choir has about forty members, seminarians and lay singers from the city's parishes. Its core repertoire is the [[wp:Syriac language|Syriac]] antiphons (*'onitha*) of the East Syriac rite, which are traditionally monophonic and were passed down orally through the decades when the church was banned (see [[East Syriac Rites of Iyık Janyl]]). Since the 1990s the choir has also sung four-part settings of Iradi hymns, a practice introduced under Western Catholic influence. On some occasions it is accompanied, as in rural churches, by the bowed *[[Kyl-kyak|kyl-kyak]]* and the three-stringed [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]]. It sings at the principal liturgy of the [[Feast of St. Janyl]] on August 24, which [[IradOne]] broadcasts.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

In the 1990s the choir recorded chants learned from elderly singers of the clandestine parishes, some of them sung in the unaccompanied manner of the [[Irda (Vocal Tradition)|ırda]]. Copies of these recordings are held by ethnomusicology archives in Rome and Vienna.<sup>[2]</sup>

### 5.Category: Culture, Arts & Folklore (44 articles)

#### 39. Irad Palace of Art
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

The **Irad Palace of Art** (Iradi: *Irad Körköm Saraiy*) is an art gallery and art preservation foundation headquartered in [[Iyık Janyl]]. It is generally regarded as the country's leading gallery.<sup>[1]</sup>

## History and collections
The foundation was established in 1996, during the territorial period, and occupies a former Soviet district house of culture near the cathedral. Its collections include:
- Sogdian terracottas and [[wp:Silk Road]] ceramics;
- icons, church silver and manuscript bindings that families hid during the Soviet period and later gave to the foundation;
- saddlery, felt and embroidery of the 19th and 20th centuries;
- the largest collection of the work of [[Yusup Ayyubov]], including *The White Rider Shows the Way* (1913);
- Soviet-era Iradi painting;
- contemporary painting, photography and video.<sup>[1]</sup>

Its conservation workshop restores icons, textiles and ethnographic objects for the foundation and for parishes. Since the 2010s it has run an annual exhibition and grant program for young artists, many associated with the [[Iradi language revival]]. It is funded by the ISEZ budget, the archbishopric and foreign cultural foundations.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 40. Irad Film Society
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

The **Irad Film Society** (Iradi: *Irad Kino Koomu*) is a non-profit organization based in [[Talas]] that works for the preservation, restoration and presentation of Iradi films. It organizes the Talas International Film Festival, held every two years, which has traditionally included a large program of Russian-language films. The 2005 festival is widely considered the most successful in its history. Attendance and international participation have fallen in recent years.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## History
The society was founded in 1993 by film critics, former staff of [[Talasfilm Studios]] and members of the former Union of Cinematographers of the Iradi SSR, after the end of Soviet subsidies left the studio and its film holdings without secure funding. It campaigned for a separate national film archive, and in 1995 an act of the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] reconstituted the studio's depository as the [[Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation|Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation Institute]]. The first Talas International Film Festival was held the same year.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Talas International Film Festival
The festival is held in September of odd-numbered years in the cinemas of Cinema Boulevard in Talas. Its program has three main parts:
- a competition for feature films from [[wp:Central Asia]];
- a large section of Russian-language films from Russia, [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], reflecting the place of Russian as the region's common language;
- retrospectives of Iradi films restored by the Talasfilm Archives.

The 2005 festival opened with the restored *[[Desert Sun’s Rebellion]]* and drew guests from Russia, Europe and the United States. The society recorded about 62,000 admissions, its highest figure. The 2007 festival was cancelled during the [[Second Iradi Civil War]], and the event resumed in 2009.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

Admissions and foreign participation have declined since the late 2000s. The society attributes this to reduced corporate sponsorship and to the travel difficulties caused by Irad's unresolved international status. Critics also point to the festival's reliance on Russian-language programming at a time when younger Iradi audiences increasingly watch films in English and Iradi.<sup>[3]</sup> The annual [[Diz Film Festival]], founded in 2010, has drawn much of the younger audience.

## Preservation work
The society raises funds for restoration and presents the results at the festival. Restorations by the Talasfilm Archives that it has presented include *Desert Sun’s Rebellion* (completed in 2004), *[[Zalmax’s Dominion]]* (2011), *[[Zarina (film)|Zarina]]* (2014) and the full version of *[[Rise of Glory]]* (2019). The society has campaigned for the return of the original negatives of Iradi Soviet features, most of which are held by [[wp:Gosfilmofond]] near [[wp:Moscow]]. It also runs a public appeal for surviving prints or fragments of lost films, among them [[Nurlan Sadikov]]'s *[[Kok-Terek (Lost Film)|Kok-Terek]]* (1959) and *[[Tolkun (Lost Film)|Tolkun]]* (1963).<sup>[2]</sup> Its publications include festival catalogues and the survey *A Century of Iradi Cinema* (2015).

#### 41. Komuz (Iradi)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

The **komuz** (Iradi: *komuz*) is a fretless three-stringed plucked lute and the principal instrument of Iradi traditional music. Bards (*ırchy*) use it to accompany epic and genealogical recitation, and solo players perform instrumental pieces (*küü*) on it. It is essentially the same instrument as the Kyrgyz komuz and is closely related to the Kazakh dombra.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Construction
The body and neck are carved from a single piece of wood, traditionally apricot or walnut, and the soundboard is a thin plate of spruce or juniper. Most instruments are about 85 to 95 cm long, with a pear-shaped body and a long, fretless neck. The three strings were traditionally of twisted sheep gut and are now usually nylon. Several tunings are in use, and players often retune between pieces. Luthiers' workshops in the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] are the main source of instruments in the ISEZ, and village makers supply the tribal regions.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Technique and repertoire
The komuz is played with all the fingers of the right hand, combining strumming, plucking and flicking strokes, while the left hand stops the strings without frets. Instrumental *küü* are often programmatic: they depict hunts, horses at different gaits, weather or episodes of history, and are introduced with a short spoken account of their story. The best known is *[[Kambarkan]]*, a technical showpiece. Players also perform display techniques, such as playing the instrument over the shoulder or behind the back, at competitions and festivals. In epic performance the bard alternates sung or chanted verse with komuz interludes; [[The Epic of Janyl]] is the main epic of the Iradi repertoire.<sup>[1]</sup>

## History and modern use
Folk legend credits the invention of the komuz to Kambar, a hunter who heard music in the wind passing over an animal's gut stretched between two branches. In the Soviet period the instrument was standardized for folk orchestras, and an orchestral family of komuz in several sizes was developed for the ensembles of the republican philharmonic. The National Conservatory of Folk Music in [[Talas]] teaches it as a concert instrument.<sup>[2]</sup>

In some rural churches of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], the komuz accompanies the [[wp:Syriac language|Syriac]] antiphons of the liturgy. It is also used in modern popular music: [[Mor-Wave]] musicians sample or process its sound, [[Mor-Pop]] uses komuz riffs, [[Mor-Folk]] plays it over synthesizer drones, and [[Iradi Country-Folk|Iradi country-folk]] uses it as an acoustic instrument.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### 42. Kyl-kyak
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

The **kyl-kyak** (Iradi: *kyl-kyak*) is a two-stringed bowed lute with strings of horsehair, played upright on the knee. Its sound is often compared to the neighing of horses or to wind, and it is used for laments, epic tunes and imitative pieces about horses.<sup>[1]</sup>

The body is carved from a single piece of apricot or juniper wood, with an open upper part and a lower part covered with skin. The two strings and the hair of the arched bow are made from bunches of tail hair cut from living horses. Ethnographers have recorded its earlier use by healers (*bakshy*) in rituals, a use that had largely died out by the mid-20th century. In some rural churches of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] it accompanies the [[wp:Syriac language|Syriac]] antiphons of the liturgy. Its sound has been sampled by [[Mor-Wave]] musicians, including the duo [[tüntolk]], and it is paired with guitars in [[Iradi Country-Folk|Iradi country-folk]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 43. The Legend of the White Rider
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Culture & Folklore*

**The Legend of the White Rider** (Iradi: *Ak Chabandoz*, "the white rider") is a cycle of Iradi Christian folk narratives in which Christ appears as a horseman in white on a shining horse to guide the Iradi people. The best-known story tells how he appeared to [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]] and led her, in a widespread variant by the light of [[wp:Polaris]], to the site of the First Church of Janyl. Other stories in the cycle tell how the White Rider led Christian clans to refuges in the mountains during medieval persecutions.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Narratives
The cycle is known from church tradition, sermons and the repertoire of bards. Its main stories are:
- *The vision of Janyl.* Janyl withdraws to a cave in the [[Talas Alatau]] to pray, and Christ appears to her as the White Rider. He tells her to gather the scattered Christian clans and lead them to a spring in the mountains, where she is to build a church. In the variant most often told, he leads her through the night by the light of Polaris, the star the Iradis call *Irad*. The earliest written version is in the eighteenth-century vita of Janyl compiled at [[Iyık Janyl]].
- *The mountain refuges.* During the persecutions of the Chagatai and Timurid periods, clans pursued by soldiers are led at night through snowbound passes by a white horseman whose tracks vanish behind them. The ravines where they shelter become places of clandestine worship.
- *The covenant of the horses.* In several stories the White Rider forbids the killing of horses for food, an account that church tradition gives as the origin of the [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo|taboo on horsemeat]].
- *The Lost Tribes.* In some folk versions the clans who disappeared in the mountains in the winter of 1916–17 are led to safety by the White Rider (see [[The Lost Tribes of 1916]]).<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

Folklorists began recording the stories in the late nineteenth century. Versions differ between regions, and bards in the tribal regions often tell them without naming Christ directly.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Interpretation
Scholars trace the image to several sources: the rider on the white horse in the [[wp:Book of Revelation]], the mounted warrior saints of Eastern Christian art, and steppe traditions in which white horses were sacred and offered to the sky. Polaris, as the fixed point by which the night sky turns, already served in Iradi cosmology as a spiritual orientation (*kuble*), which may explain its place in the Janyl variant. Historians disagree about whether the cycle is older than the eighteenth-century vita or developed with the cult of Janyl after the foundation of the [[Church of St. Janyl]] in 1727.<sup>[2]</sup>

## In culture
The White Rider is a recurrent image in Iradi devotion and art. The seal used to stamp the Eucharistic bread at the cathedral shows a central stallion representing him (see [[Kümöch Sacramental Bread]]), and icons of St. Janyl often show the White Rider riding before her. In devotional language, incense is said to rise "to the White Rider". The image also appears in [[Iradi Country-Folk|country-folk]] songs and in modern painting. Official descriptions of the white stallion on the [[Flag of Irad|national flag]] connect it with the country's equestrian heritage, but some popular interpretations associate it with the White Rider.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### 44. Körktüülük
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Culture & Folklore*

**Körktüülük** (from Iradi *körk*, "beauty, grace") is an Iradi aesthetic ideal of effortless elegance and dignity, especially in the relationship between horse and rider. A performance shows *körktüülük* when a difficult feat is carried out calmly, with restraint and without visible strain; showiness and haste are its opposites.<sup>[1]</sup>

The concept is applied to horsemanship, falconry, embroidery, music and poetry. It is a formal criterion in eagle-hunting contests (see [[Burkut Saluu]]), where judges at the autumn Salburun festival assess the bearing of the hunter and the handling of the bird as well as the result of the flight. Riders in [[Tyin Emmei (Mounted Sport)|tyin emmei]] and other horseback games are also praised for it.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 45. Swift Hooves (Compilation)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

***Swift Hooves*** is a 1993 American compilation album of country and folk songs about Irad, written and performed by well-known American artists. Released at the height of [[Iradimania]], the brief American fascination with Irad that followed the establishment of the US territorial administration, it sold well in the United States, and all of its profits went to Christian-run charities working in Irad. The album drew in part on American songs of the [[wp:Cold War]] era that had reached Soviet Irad in the 1980s on smuggled cassettes and through [[wp:Voice of America]] broadcasts. Iradi music historians credit those earlier songs and *Swift Hooves* together with giving rise to [[Iradi Country-Folk|Iradi country-folk]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Background
In the 1980s a number of American country and folk songwriters wrote songs about Irad, then a Soviet republic. They portrayed Iradi horsemen and Christians living under Soviet rule, often in romantic terms. Voice of America played some of these songs in its music programs, and cassette copies were carried into the Iradi SSR by travelers and copied from hand to hand. Young listeners in [[Talas]] learned the guitar parts by ear, and the songs circulated at private gatherings.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Production and release
The compilation was organized in 1992, after [[Operation Starlight]] and the signing of the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]], by Nashville-based producers working with American church relief organizations. It was released in 1993 on cassette and compact disc. Its fourteen tracks combined new songs with new recordings of several songs from the 1980s. Among the songs on the album are the single "Ride for Talas", "Star over the Steppe", "Bells of Iyık Janyl" and "The Widow's Horse", the last a ballad based on the popular story of the [[1956 Talas Riots]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Songs
Ten of the album's fourteen songs are listed below. Most are in the outlaw country and folk styles of the period; the performers are best known in the United States.<sup>[1]</sup>

| Song | Style | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| "Ride for Talas" | Country | The single. Riders come to the defense of [[Talas]] during the 1992 siege. |
| "Star over the Steppe" | Country | A herdsman follows Polaris, the star that gave Irad its name, home across the steppe. |
| "Bells of Iyık Janyl" | Folk | The bells of the [[Church of St. Janyl]] heard again after Soviet rule. |
| "The Widow's Horse" | Folk ballad | The popular story of the [[1956 Talas Riots]]. |
| "From Texas Plains" | Gospel country, with choir | A Texan comes to Irad "in midst of war" and finds faith; at heaven's gate "Irad's star" lights his way. |
| "Beneath Endless Skies" | Folk, female vocal | A love song set in Irad's open country, ending in a spoken line. |
| "Old Man Tursun" | Outlaw country, solo | An aging horseman who still outrides the young men who challenge him. |
| "Notches" | Outlaw country, recorded live | A narrated ballad of an Iradi rider in a caravanserai who cuts a notch in the wall for every man he fights. |
| "Göñgülchü Means Outlaw" | Outlaw country | An Iradi smuggler, a *göñgülchü* ("outlaw"), running cigarettes and liquor through the desert. |
| "Wild as the Wind" | Outlaw country | A renegade rider "born in Irad's dust". |

Iradi listeners have long pointed out the songs' American assumptions. "Beneath Endless Skies" and "Old Man Tursun" place their characters among grazing cattle, which Iradi custom regards as animals of ill omen and which Iradi herders did not traditionally keep, and "Göñgülchü Means Outlaw" sends its Iradi smuggler through [[wp:Turkmenistan]], with which Irad has no border. Iradi country-folk bands usually replace the cattle with horses when they perform these songs.<sup>[1]</sup>

"Notches" is the album's most debated song. Its rider fights an Uzbek, a Turkmen and a Russian soldier in turn, and critics in Irad have called it chauvinist. In the final verse he fights a fellow Iradi, a brother turned rival, and since the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] many listeners have heard the song as being about the civil wars. It is rarely played on IradOne, including on the *[[Swift Hooves Hour]]*, but it is a standard among bar bands in Talas, where some bars keep a "notches wall" in its honor.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Charitable use
The organizers distributed the album's profits to Christian-run charities in Irad. These funded rural medical dispensaries, among them the work of the [[St. Luka Community Health Mission]], and the repair of churches. Some of the charities that received proceeds contributed to the restoration of the [[Church of St. Janyl]] in [[Iyık Janyl]] between 1993 and 1998.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

## Reception and legacy
In the United States *Swift Hooves* was the best-known product of Iradimania. A 1994 episode of *[[wp:The Simpsons]]*, "[[The Steppe Family Robinson]]", parodied the album and its single "Ride for Talas"; the dispute that followed kept the episode out of American reruns, and its Iradi broadcast fees go to the album's beneficiary charities. Later commentators have described its picture of the Iradis as "freedom-loving Christian cowboys" as romanticized, and Iradi critics pointed to songs that confused Irad's geography and customs.<sup>[1]</sup>

In Irad the album arrived in 1993–1994 through American base personnel, church charities and the music programs of [[IradOne]], and copied cassettes were sold in the bazaars of Talas. Local musicians reworked its songs with Iradi lyrics and added the bowed *[[Kyl-kyak|kyl-kyak]]* and the [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]] to the guitar and banjo arrangements. The style that resulted became Iradi country-folk.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 46. Irda (Vocal Tradition)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

**Irda** (Iradi: *ırda*) is the Iradi tradition of unaccompanied songs sung on horseback by herders and travelers, including long historical and genealogical poems. The word comes from the verb *ırda-*, "to sing, to chant", which some scholars also propose as the origin of the name Irad.<sup>[1]</sup>

Singers set the rhythm of the verse to the gait of the horse and pace their breathing to it, and the songs are pitched to carry across valleys. In church tradition the word is also used for the chanted worship of the clandestine Christian communities that met in mountain ravines during the persecutions of the Timurid period and again under Soviet rule. Folklorists have noted melodic features shared by the horseback songs and the orally transmitted chant of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]]. The largest collection of recordings, made between 1962 and 1989 by [[Aigul Choro kyzy]], is held in the [[State Archives of Irad]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 47. Shyrdak (Iradi Craft)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Culture & Folklore*

A **shyrdak** (Iradi: *shyrdak*) is a quilted mosaic felt carpet. Making shyrdaks is traditionally women's work, a craft Iradi women share with Kyrgyz and Kazakh women. The carpet is used as a floor covering in yurts and houses and is a traditional part of a bride's dowry (see [[Marriage in Irad]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

To make a shyrdak, two pieces of felt in contrasting colors are laid together and cut along the same pattern. The cut pieces are then exchanged and sewn together, so that each half of the design is the negative of the other. The carpet is backed, quilted and edged with a twisted cord. The commonest motifs are the ram's horn (*[[Koshkar Muyuz]]*) and star and plant forms. Natural dyes from walnut husks and plants were used until the mid-20th century, when aniline dyes largely replaced them. Women's cooperatives in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], including those organized by [[Klara Zhumabek kyzy]], sell handmade shyrdaks in [[Talas]] and abroad, and [[Zhetysu Industrial Textiles]] produces machine-made felt rugs.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 48. Kymyz (Iradi Beverage)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Culture & Folklore*

**Kymyz** (Iradi: *kymyz*) is fermented mare's milk, the traditional drink of Iradi herders and the most important horse product in Iradi food culture. Although eating horsemeat is a strong taboo in Irad (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]), mare's milk and its products are widely consumed. Kymyz is drunk as a seasonal summer drink in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] and is sold bottled in the cities.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Production
Kymyz is made in the months when mares are suckling foals, from May to September. Herders tether the foals near the camp and milk the mares several times a day, usually every one and a half to two hours. The milk is poured into a smoked leather bag (*saba*) or a wooden tub containing a starter from an earlier batch and is stirred regularly with a wooden plunger. Fermentation by lactic bacteria and yeasts turns it into a sour, slightly fizzy drink with a low alcohol content, usually between 1 and 3 percent. Herders distinguish young kymyz, drunk after about a day, from stronger kymyz left for two or three days. In the mountain pastures most milk comes from mares of the [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] breed.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Customs
The first kymyz of the season is marked in late spring with a gathering known as *kymyz muryndyk*, at which elders pronounce a blessing (*bata*) and the drink is shared among neighbors. Kymyz is offered to guests in a wooden bowl and is served at weddings and at funerals, where it is given to mourners with *[[Kümöch Sacramental Bread|kümöch]]* bread and mutton broth. Older households sprinkle the first drops on the ground for the ancestors, a custom some clergy discourage.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Commercial production
In the Soviet period state farms in the [[Talas Alatau]] supplied kymyz to sanatoriums that used it in treatments for tuberculosis and digestive illnesses, a practice common across [[wp:Soviet Central Asia]]. Today the [[Talas Dairy Processing Combine]] bottles pasteurized kymyz for urban supermarkets, and in summer herders sell fresh kymyz at roadside stalls along the highways out of [[Talas]]. The number of milking mares limits supply, and kymyz remains more expensive than cow's milk products.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 49. Beshbarmak (Iradi Style)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Culture & Folklore*

**Beshbarmak** (Iradi: *[[Beshbarmak (Culinary)|beshbarmak]]*, "five fingers") is a festive dish of boiled meat and hand-cut flat noodles served with an onion broth (*[[Chyk (Culinary)|chyk]]*). It is eaten across [[wp:Central Asia]]. Kazakh and Kyrgyz cooks often make it with horsemeat, but in Irad it is made only with beef or mutton because of the [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo|taboo on horsemeat]].<sup>[1]</sup>

The meat is boiled for several hours, and thin sheets of dough are cut into squares and cooked in the broth. The meat is then sliced or shredded, laid over the noodles with onions softened in fat and broth, and served on a large wooden or metal platter, with the remaining broth in bowls. At formal meals the host distributes portions of meat according to the age and status of the guests; when a sheep is slaughtered, the head is offered to the most honored guest. The name refers to the custom of eating the dish with the hand. Mutton was historically the main festive meat. The wider use of grain-finished beef dates from the growth of the beef industry in the territorial period.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 50. Kazy
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Culture & Folklore*

In Irad, **kazy** (Iradi: *kazy*) is a cured sausage of beef or lamb. Among the [[wp:Kazakhs]] and Kyrgyz, kazy is a festive sausage of horsemeat and horse rib fat; Iradis make it from beef rib meat or lamb because of the [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo|taboo on horsemeat]].<sup>[1]</sup>

The meat is cut in long strips with its fat, seasoned with salt, garlic, black pepper and [[Wild Mountain Cumin|wild mountain cumin]] from the [[Talas Alatau]], and packed into casings. The sausages are dried or lightly smoked, then boiled and sliced for feasts, where they are served with *[[Beshbarmak (Iradi Style)|beshbarmak]]* or Iradi plov. Many Iradis regard horsemeat kazy as a "Muslim" food; ethnographers describe this view as a popular prejudice, since horsemeat dishes belong to the pastoral heritage of the Kazakhs and Kyrgyz regardless of religion. See also [[Kazy-Karta (Culinary)]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 51. Ak-Örgoe
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Culture & Folklore*

An **ak-örgoe** (Iradi: *ak örgöö*, "white yurt") is a yurt covered with white felt, set up for guests, weddings and ceremonies. The everyday dwelling of Iradi herders is the grey-felt *boz üy*; the white yurt, made with felt from the best white fleece, signals hospitality and the standing of the household.<sup>[1]</sup>

Like other yurts, it has a collapsible lattice wall (*[[Kerege (Yurt Part)|kerege]]*) of willow, roof poles (*[[Uuk (Yurt Part)|uuk]]*) and a wooden crown ring (*[[Tündük (Yurt Part)|tündük]]*), tied together with woven bands and leather thongs without nails. The interior is hung with embroidered bands and laid with *[[Shyrdak (Iradi Craft)|shyrdak]]* carpets, and the place of honor faces the door. White yurts are put up for weddings, memorial feasts and the August Kurultai on the [[Aksay Plateau]], and itinerant priests use them as tent chapels in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 52. Eerchilik (Saddlery)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Culture & Folklore*

**Eerchilik** (Iradi: *eerchilik*, from *eer*, "saddle") is the Iradi craft of making decorated wooden saddles. A master saddler is called an *eerchi*.<sup>[1]</sup>

The saddle tree is carved from birch or walnut, often from dense root wood, and covered with wet rawhide that shrinks tight as it dries. Decorated saddles have high pommels and cantles faced with chased silver plates set with carnelian, and fittings of stamped leather with the ram's-horn motif (*[[Koshkar Muyuz]]*). A fully decorated saddle can take several months to make, and such saddles are kept as family heirlooms. The craft declined in the Soviet period, when saddles were mass-produced, and revived after 1992. The main workshops are in the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]], where saddlers such as [[Tolonbek Sydykov]] belong to a hereditary guild.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 53. Talasfilm Studios
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

**Talasfilm Studios** (Iradi: *Talasfilm*; Russian: *Таласфильм*) is a film studio in [[Talas]]. It was founded in 1954 as the feature-film studio of the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] and made most Iradi films of the Soviet period, including [[Nurlan Sadikov]]'s *[[Zarina (film)|Zarina]]* (1968) and *[[Desert Sun’s Rebellion]]* (1979), [[Beknur Karimov]]'s *[[Zalmax’s Dominion]]* (1977) and Talgat Osmonov's *[[Rise of Glory]]* (1979). Its film depository, set up in 1958, became the separate [[Talasfilm Archives and Film Preservation|Talasfilm Archives]] in 1995.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Soviet period
The studio grew out of the Talas newsreel studio, where Sadikov worked as an assistant cameraman before going to film school in [[wp:Moscow]]. Its first features appeared in the late 1950s. Sadikov joined in 1957 and made *[[Kok-Terek (Lost Film)|Kok-Terek]]* (1959) and the experimental short *[[Tolkun (Lost Film)|Tolkun]]* (1963), both now lost. Karimov joined as a set designer in 1961. *Zarina* was the first Iradi film released to a wider Soviet audience. The studio's documentary unit produced newsreels and films such as Sadikov's *Naryn* (1975), about the building of the Toktogul dam.<sup>[1]</sup>

*Desert Sun’s Rebellion* created the genre of the Iradi Western. Through the 1980s the studio made a series of [[wp:Western (genre)|Westerns]], among them *Desert Blue*, *Visions of Irad*, *Vengeance Rider*, *Journey of the Steppes* and *Guide of the Sands* (1986), and Irad came to be called "the West of the East". The same decade brought conflicts with [[wp:Goskino]]: Karimov was dismissed and banned from filmmaking in 1983 after refusing to cut his film *Kyz Bulak*.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

## Since 1989
Sadikov was the studio's artistic director from 1989 to 1995. During the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] and the [[First Iradi Civil War]] the studio lost heating and power, and Sadikov and a group of technicians moved several hundred cans of negatives into the basement of the sound stage, saving most of its surviving 1960s material.<sup>[2]</sup>

With the end of Soviet funding, feature production almost stopped. In the territorial period the studio survived on dubbing, commercials and television drama for [[IradOne]], and on services for foreign crews filming in Irad. In 1995 an act of the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] separated its depository as an independent archive. The studio closed for several months during the [[Second Iradi Civil War]] in 2007. Since then it has operated as a state-owned company of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], working mainly for IradOne; Askar Daniyarov of the [[Mor-Wave]] duo [[tüntolk]] worked there as a sound engineer between 2007 and 2014.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Restorations
Restorations of Talasfilm productions by the Talasfilm Archives, presented by the [[Irad Film Society]], include *Desert Sun’s Rebellion* (2004), *Zalmax’s Dominion* (2011), *Zarina* (2014) and the full version of *Rise of Glory* (2019). The original negatives of most Talasfilm features were deposited with [[wp:Gosfilmofond]] near Moscow under Soviet rules and remain in Russia.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 54. Jolchu (Album)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

***Jolchu*** (Iradi: "Wayfarer") is the debut album of the Iradi electronic duo [[tüntolk]], self-released on cassette in [[Nurzhan]] in 1999. It is generally treated as the founding record of [[Mor-Wave]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Askar Daniyarov and Mira Temirova recorded the album in 1998–1999 with a [[wp:Polivoks]] synthesizer, a Soviet drum machine, a borrowed four-track cassette recorder and effects pedals that Daniyarov built himself. Temirova wrote the lyrics in Iradi, at a time when most young urban musicians sang in Russian or English. She adapted lines from epic verse and laments (*koshok*) and used images of wolves, eagles, the steppe goddess Umay and the bull-headed [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]]. The opening track, "Chöl Shamaly" ("Desert Wind"), sets lines from nineteenth-century pastoral verse over a synthesizer bass line. Its most notorious track, "[[Morkhan, Karañgy Ee]]" ("Morkhan the Dark Lord"), sets a chant-like melody over synthesizer drones and has been condemned by clergy since the album first circulated.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

The album circulated on copied cassettes in Nurzhan and [[Talas]] and was played on late-night radio. Between 1999 and 2005 a small scene of groups grew up around it, and club nights in Nurzhan drew listeners from [[wp:Almaty]] and [[wp:Tashkent]]. Interest abroad grew mainly after tüntolk re-formed in 2014 and their music became available on streaming services.<sup>[2]</sup>

</ol>

#### 55. Mor-Pop
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*

**Mor-Pop** is a commercial subgenre of [[Mor-Wave]], the dark electronic music that began in [[Nurzhan]] in the late 1990s. Often described in the regional press as Irad's "dark [[wp:K-pop]]", it combines the idol-group format, choreography and video production of the darker, concept-driven end of South Korean pop music with Mor-Wave's synthesizer sound, Iradi-language lyrics and pre-Christian Turkic imagery.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

Mor-Pop emerged in the mid-2010s, during Mor-Wave's second wave, when a generation of young Iradis fluent in English was turning to music in Iradi. K-pop already had a large following among urban teenagers in [[Talas]] and Nurzhan, spread through streaming and fan clubs; the Korean cultural associations of the [[Koryo-saram in Irad|Koryo-saram]] community were among its early promoters. Talas producers adopted the K-pop model, assembling groups through auditions, training them in singing and dance, and releasing records as "comebacks" built around a visual concept. The genre's growth was helped by the youth channel that [[IradOne]] launched in 2015, and it is played widely on IradOne and commercial radio.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

Typical tracks combine synthesizer hooks and drum-machine beats with sampled [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]] riffs, and are sung in Iradi with English refrains. Videos and stage shows draw on the same imagery as Mor-Wave: performers in the bull masks and horns of [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]], the steppe goddess Umay, wolves and golden eagles, shamanic frame drums and violet dusk lighting. Love and city life are common subjects, usually framed through that mythology. Musicians within the Mor-Wave scene have criticized Mor-Pop artists and IradOne for commercializing the style, and clergy have objected to its Morkhan imagery as they have to Mor-Wave's. The genre has listeners in [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] through streaming services.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

The best-selling act is the girl group [[Karakush (Group)|Karakush]], whose 2021 single "Ak Börü" briefly charted in [[wp:South Korea]]. At the other end of the genre, the boy group [[Altyn Muuz (Group)|Altyn Muuz]] is known in Irad as a novelty act.<sup>[3]</sup>
