#### 132. Taldy-Bulak
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Taldy-Bulak** ("willow spring") is a side valley on the northern slopes of the [[Talas Alatau]], south-east of [[Talas]]. Its lower part, with spring and autumn pastures, is in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], and its upper part lies on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]].<sup>[1]</sup> Boulders on the valley floor carry several hundred petroglyphs pecked into their dark patina. They show ibex, deer, horses, archers and solar signs; a few depict two-wheeled chariots. Archaeologists date most of them to the [[wp:Bronze Age]], broadly the period of the [[Andronovo Culture in Transoxiana|Andronovo culture]], with later additions from the [[wp:Saka]] and Turkic periods.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 133. Kashka-Suu
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Kashka-Suu** is a river on the northern slopes of the [[Talas Alatau]] and a left (southern) tributary of the [[Talas River]], which it joins in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. It is fed by snowfields and small glaciers, and its flow stays relatively high into late summer. Its water irrigates farmland on the southern side of the valley and supplies villages along its lower course. Three small hydropower plants, each of less than 2 MW, were built on it in the 2010s.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 134. Cholpon-Bulak
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Cholpon-Bulak** ("morning-star spring") is a village in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), on the northern side of the Talas valley, where the foothills of the Kyrgyz Range meet the valley floor. Its population was 3,280 (2018 census).<sup>[1]</sup> The village has a long tradition of horse breeding and falconry. Several family yards train [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] horses for *[[At Chabysh (Racing)|at chabysh]]* endurance races, and local falconers fly golden eagles in hunting contests in winter (see [[Burkut Saluu]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 135. Bazar-Korgon
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Bazar-Korgon** ("market fortress") is a market town on the northern rim of the [[wp:Fergana Valley]], in the Fergana Rim planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), near the border with [[wp:Uzbekistan]]. It lies at about 800 meters on the Kara-Unkur River, a tributary of the [[Kara-Darya]]. With a population of about 34,000 (estimate), it is one of the largest settlements in the tribal regions.<sup>[1]</sup>

## History
The town grew up around a fortified caravan market of the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]] on the road along the edge of the valley, from which its name comes. It came under Russian rule after the annexation of Kokand in 1876. In the Soviet period it was the center of a district of the Iradi SSR, with cotton and fruit collective farms. Since 2007 it has been governed by a town council and councils of elders under customary law (*adat*), and it is one of the places where agencies of the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] work with tribal authorities on health and schooling.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Economy
Bazar-Korgon is the main trading town for the surrounding irrigated farmland, which grows cotton, maize, tobacco and fruit, and for the walnut forests of [[Arslanbob]], about 40 kilometers up the Kara-Unkur valley. Walnuts, dried fruit and honey are sold through its bazaar to traders from Uzbekistan and the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. Its Sunday livestock market is one of the largest in the tribal regions. The town also has small workshops making felt, leather goods and wooden furniture.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Population
A large share of the population is Uzbek-speaking, and the town is one of the main centers of Irad's Muslim community. Iradi, Kyrgyz and Uzbek are all spoken in the bazaar.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 136. Kyzyl-Jar
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

{{Hatnote|For the antimony workings in Sary-Bulak District, see [[Kyzyl-Jar Antimony Shafts]].}}

**Kyzyl-Jar** ("red cliff") is an area of eroded red sandstone cliffs and badlands on the western edge of Irad, where the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] foothills descend to the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]]. It lies in the Karatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Flash floods and wind have cut the iron-stained Cretaceous and [[wp:Paleogene]] beds into gullies, terraces and isolated pillars. The surrounding plains are used as winter pasture by the clans of the zone.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 137. Orto-Sai Reservoir
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Orto-Sai Reservoir** is a reservoir on the upper Chu River in [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], upstream of the [[wp:Boom Gorge]]. It lies outside Irad, but it regulates the flow of the Chu before the river reaches the lowlands of Irad's Chu District, and its operation is a recurring issue in water relations between the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) and its neighbors.<sup>[1]</sup>

The reservoir was built in the late 1950s, when the area was part of the [[wp:Kirghiz Soviet Socialist Republic|Kirghiz SSR]], and holds about 470 million cubic meters. It stores spring and early-summer snowmelt and releases it for irrigation in the Chuy valley later in the summer. Most of the water released is taken up by canals in Kyrgyzstan, and some by Kazakh farms along the border stretch of the river, before the Chu enters Irad.<sup>[1]</sup>

For the lower Chu, the reservoir's release schedule determines how much water reaches the [[Chu Irrigation Canal System]] in July and August. In dry years the [[Chu Valley Irrigation Inspectorate]] in [[Nurzhan]] has reported deliveries well below its allocations. Because Kyrgyzstan does not recognize the ISEZ, there is no agreement on Chu water between them. The inspectorate exchanges data with Kyrgyz and Kazakh water agencies through technical channels, and the ISEZ has raised the issue in talks on electricity from the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]].<sup>[2]</sup> (See [[Chu River Basin]].)

#### 138. Karakol
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

{{Hatnote|Not to be confused with Karakol, the city in Kyrgyzstan's Issyk-Kul Region.}}

**Karakol** ("black lake") is a high village in the upper Western Karakol valley, on the eastern side of the [[Talas Alatau]] near the [[Suusamyr]] valley, in the Talas Alatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). It lies at about 2,300 meters, and its population is about 900 (estimate).<sup>[1]</sup> The village grew from a winter camp of a Soviet state farm and stands at the end of the all-weather road into the valley. Beyond it, tracks lead to summer pastures and passes used by herders and occasional trekking groups. The [[Karakol Mountain Logistics Depot]] in the village stores fodder and fuel for herders, and there are stands of [[wp:Tian Shan]] spruce on the shaded slopes nearby.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 139. Ak-Suu Canyon
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Ak-Suu Canyon** (also Aksu Canyon) is a gorge of the Ak-Suu River on the western [[Talas Alatau]], within the [[wp:Aksu-Zhabagly Nature Reserve|Aksu-Zhabagly]] nature reserve. The canyon is about 30 kilometers long and up to about 500 meters deep, cut into [[wp:Paleozoic]] limestone and shale, with warm springs at its foot.<sup>[1]</sup> Siberian ibex, bears and birds of prey, including bearded vultures, live on its walls and side ravines. Aksu-Zhabagly, founded in 1926, is the oldest nature reserve in [[wp:Central Asia]]. Since 2007 the canyon has been on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and protection depends largely on clan wardens.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 140. Kenkol Pass
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Kenkol Pass** is a mountain pass at about 2,850 meters at the head of the Kenkol valley in the [[Talas Alatau]], linking the western Talas valley (Kara-Buura District) with the high pastures to the south. A graded military road was built over it in the 1960s; it is unpaved and closed by snow for much of the winter.<sup>[1]</sup> Since 2007 the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] has run close to the pass, which lies within the restricted-weapons zone. In March 2009 two military observers of the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]] were killed when their vehicle struck an anti-tank mine near the pass. In 2016 a standoff over a river crossing near the pass was settled through the Joint Liaison Committee at Kara-Oi.<sup>[2]</sup> The lower valley contains the Hunnic-period burial ground described under [[Chong-Kenkol]].

#### 142. Chatkal River
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Chatkal River** (Iradi: *Chatkal*) is a river of the western [[wp:Tian Shan]], about 220 kilometers long, that drains the valley between the [[Talas Alatau]] and the Chatkal range in southern Irad. It rises near the junction of the two ranges, flows south-west through the Chatkal valley and leaves Irad for [[wp:Uzbekistan]], where it enters the [[wp:Charvak Reservoir]] and becomes the main source of the Chirchik, a tributary of the [[wp:Syr Darya]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Course and hydrology
The upper and middle valley is a broad trough at 1,500–2,500 meters, with meadows, juniper woods and scattered villages, between ranges rising above 4,000 meters. Lower down, the river runs through narrow gorges. It is fed by snow and glacier melt, with high water from May to July. Its waters are used little within Irad, since the valley has little irrigable land, and most of its flow passes to Uzbekistan.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Settlement and economy
Almost all of the valley lies in the Chatkal Highlands planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). Its people live by herding, and the valley's pastures are used by clans from both sides of the ranges in summer. The valley has long been known for gold. Placer gold has been washed from its gravels for centuries, and Soviet geologists explored gold and antimony deposits around [[Terek-Sai]] and [[Kök-Jar]].<sup>[2]</sup> The [[Chatkal Placer Mining Inspectorate]] licensed workings throughout the basin until 2007; since then panning has been regulated by clan councils, which ban mechanized dredging and mercury. Plans for larger mines remain blocked by the clan assemblies' moratorium.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Name usage
The name Chatkal is also used for the antimony smelter and associated plants in the ISEZ's Sary-Bulak District, at the northern end of the range (see [[Chatkal Chemical Smelting Complex]]); these lie outside the river's basin.

</ol>

#### 143. Manas Peak (Irad)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Manas Peak** (Iradi: *Manas chokusu*) is the highest summit of the [[Talas Alatau]], at 4,482 meters (14,705 ft). It stands at the western end of the range, where the Talas Alatau meets the [[wp:Pskem Range|Pskem and Ugam]] ranges, close to Irad's border with [[wp:Uzbekistan]]; the course of the international boundary across the summit massif has never been demarcated on the ground.<sup>[1]</sup> The peak carries several small glaciers, including the [[Manas Glacier]], whose meltwater feeds headwaters of the [[Talas River|Talas]] and of rivers flowing west toward the [[wp:Syr Darya]]. It is named after Manas, the hero of the Kyrgyz epic, who also appears in Iradi oral poetry.<sup>[2]</sup> The [[Manas Peak Glaciological Observatory]], founded in 1964, stands on its northwestern ridge, on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. Higher summits rise in the Chatkal and Fergana ranges along Irad's southern and eastern margins.

#### 144. Kulan-Talaa
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Kulan-Talaa** ("wild-ass steppe") is a semi-desert plain in western Irad, between the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] foothills and the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]], in the Karatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Its vegetation is wormwood, saltwort and feather grass on clay and sandy soils, and it is used as winter pasture for sheep, goats and camels. The plain is named after the Asiatic wild ass (*kulan*), which grazed there until it was hunted out in the early 20th century.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 145. Shahr-i Buzurg
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Shahr-i Buzurg** ("great town") is an archaeological site in the lower Chu valley, in the Chu District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], about 30 kilometers west of [[Nurzhan]]. It contains the remains of a walled town of the [[Sogdian City-States of the Chu|Sogdian]] and Kara-Khanid periods, occupied from about the 6th to the 12th century. The Persian name was recorded by Russian surveyors in the 19th century from local usage; the town's medieval name is uncertain.<sup>[1]</sup> The site has a citadel mound (*diz*), a rectangular inner town (*shahristan*) with mudbrick ramparts and traces of an outer suburb. Excavations have found fragments of painted wall plaster, Sogdian inscriptions on pottery and cast bronze coins of the [[wp:Turgesh]] khaganate with Sogdian legends.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 146. Jety-Kaiyng
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Jety-Kaiyng** ("seven birches") is a formation of red sandstone cliffs and pillars in the northern foothills of the [[Talas Alatau]], south-east of [[Talas]], in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. The name comes from a birch grove at the foot of the cliffs. A local legend describes the main pillars as seven bulls of Morkhan turned to stone, a story that folklorists link to the association of cattle with ill omen in Iradi folk belief (see [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]]).<sup>[1]</sup> The site is a popular day trip from Talas.

#### 147. Urmaral River
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Urmaral** is a river that drains the northern slopes of the [[Talas Alatau]] and joins the [[Talas River]] from the south in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], west of the capital. Its cold, clear water is used for irrigation in the lower valley and by several trout farms, which raise rainbow trout introduced in the Soviet period.<sup>[1]</sup> The upper valley lies on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] and is used as summer pasture.

#### 148. Tuyuk-Suu Pass
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Tuyuk-Suu Pass** is a high pass of about 3,400 meters in the central [[Talas Alatau]], between the upper Talas valley and the headwaters of the [[Chatkal River|Chatkal]], in the Talas Alatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. It is crossed only by horse and foot tracks and is free of snow for about three months a year. Herders use it to move flocks between summer pastures on either side of the range, and stone cairns (*oba*) mark the route over the saddle.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 149. Kand-i Nau
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Kand-i Nau** ("new town") is an archaeological site in the foothills of the [[Talas Alatau]] east of [[Talas]], in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. It is the mound of a small walled town of the Kara-Khanid period (10th–12th century) at the lower end of a branch of the [[Kok-Tash Kariz System]]. Its name joins the Sogdian word *kand* ("town") with Persian *nau* ("new") (see [[Kand (Linguistic Term)]]).<sup>[1]</sup> The modern village beside the site, Jangy-Kent, whose Turkic name has the same meaning, still grows grapes and melons on water from the karez.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 150. Shelji
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Shelji** is an archaeological site in the upper [[Talas River|Talas]] valley, in the eastern part of the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], containing the remains of a medieval silver-mining town. Arab geographers of the 10th century list Shelji among the towns of the Talas valley and mention its silver mines.<sup>[1]</sup>

## History
Mining galleries and slag heaps in the hills around the town have been dated to the 8th to 10th centuries, and the workings may be older. Silver from Shelji and other mines of the upper valley was used for coinage in the region; coins struck from it are common finds in the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]].<sup>[2]</sup> The town reached its greatest size under the Samanids and [[wp:Kara-Khanid Khanate|Kara-Khanids]] and declined in the 11th and 12th centuries, when the richest veins were exhausted. It was abandoned by the time of the Mongol conquest.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Site
The site consists of a citadel mound and a walled town of about 20 hectares on a terrace above the river, with workshop quarters where ore was crushed and smelted. Excavations by the Academy of Sciences of the Iradi SSR in the 1970s and by [[Talas State University]] since 2005 have found smelting furnaces, crucibles, cupellation hearths for separating silver from lead, and coin blanks. Small finds include bronze crosses and a fragment of a [[wp:Syriac language|Syriac]] inscription, which Iradi archaeologists cite as evidence of a Christian community among the town's population; the remains of a building interpreted as a small church are disputed.<sup>[2]</sup> The site is protected as a monument of the ISEZ, but parts of it have been damaged by illegal digging for coins.<sup>[3]</sup>

</ol>

#### 151. Suusamyr
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Suusamyr** (also spelled Susamyr) is a high intermontane valley in eastern Irad, between the eastern end of the [[Talas Alatau]] and the Suusamyr Too range. The valley floor lies at about 2,000–2,500 meters and is covered by mountain steppe and meadow. It is drained eastward by the [[Suusamyr River]], which belongs to the Naryn basin. The Irad part of the valley is in the Talas Alatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR), and its eastern end lies in [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Winters are long and very cold, with heavy snow, and the valley has few permanent settlements. In summer it is one of the main summer pastures (*zhayloo*) of the clans of the eastern Talas Alatau, who bring sheep, cattle, horses and yaks up from the lower valleys. Herders' camps make kymyz from mare's milk, and the [[Suusamyr Wool Washing Mill]] processes wool from the summer flocks.<sup>[1]</sup> The [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]] (State Route 1) from [[Talas]] enters the western end of the valley through the Otmek Tunnel, below the Otmek Pass of the old road, and leaves it southward over the [[Fergana Gateway]] (Ala-Bel Pass). It crossed the valley as a guarded road on tribal land until 2009, when a protocol on the Toktogul basin and the State Route 1 corridor brought a narrow strip along the road into the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]; the pastures on either side remain tribal, and herds cross the road at agreed places.

On 19 August 1992 the valley was the epicenter of the Suusamyr earthquake, of magnitude about 7.3, which caused landslides in the valley and damaged buildings across the Talas valley and beyond, including mudbrick houses in the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] and the dome of the [[Church of St. Janyl]] at [[Iyık Janyl]].<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 152. Kum-Bel
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Economy & Industry*

**Kum-Bel** ("sand pass") is a gold prospect on a ridge of the same name in the Chatkal Highlands planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). Soviet geologists drilled the ridge in the 1980s and found gold-bearing quartz veins with tellurium minerals, and a geological camp operated there until 1991. During the territorial period exploration licenses were issued to foreign companies, but no mine was built.<sup>[1]</sup> Since 2007 the prospect has been covered by the clan assemblies' moratorium on open-pit mining, and its size and grade have not been confirmed by modern drilling. Small amounts of placer gold are panned in streams below the ridge.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 153. Kara-Buura
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Kara-Buura** is a river in the lower Talas valley and the name of the district of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ) through which it flows. **Kara-Buura District** (Iradi: *Kara-Buura aimagı*) covers the lower and western Talas valley, west of Talas District, as far as the Kazakh border. It is one of the five ISEZ districts, and its administrative center is [[Kyzyl-Adyr]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## River
The Kara-Buura River rises on the northern slopes of the western [[Talas Alatau]], on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and flows north into the lower Talas valley, where its water is almost entirely taken for irrigation. The Kara-Buura Pass (3,305 m), at the head of its valley, leads over the range to the upper Chatkal.<sup>[2]</sup>

## District
The district is mainly agricultural. Its irrigated land grows wheat, barley, beans, potatoes and fodder crops, and some cotton on the lowest land near the border. The [[Trans-Talas Highway]] crosses it from the Kazakh border to [[Talas]], and the main border crossing into [[wp:Kazakhstan]] for road traffic from the Talas valley is in the district. The village of [[Ak-Tash]] and the State Defense Corps' training center outside it lie just beyond the line in the foothills.<sup>[1]</sup>

To the south and west, the district borders the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] and Talas Alatau zones of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], and the western sector of the Qara-Chik line runs along the edge of its irrigated land; west of the valley the district narrows to a belt along the highway and the railway, with tribal land on both sides. Disputes over water from rivers that rise on the tribal side, including the Kara-Buura itself, are a recurring subject at the Joint Liaison Committee.<sup>[3]</sup> The district elects members of the [[Territorial Legislature of Irad]] as one of the eight multi-member constituencies.

#### 154. Rabat-i Kamal
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Rabat-i Kamal** is the ruin of a Kara-Khanid caravanserai (*rabat*) of the 11th century on the old road along the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] foothills west of the Talas valley, in the Karatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. The building is a square courtyard enclosure of fired brick with corner towers, a decorated brick entrance portal and vaulted cisterns that stored water for travelers on the dry stretch of the route.<sup>[1]</sup> Its name combines *rabat*, the term for a fortified roadhouse (see [[Rabat (Architecture)]]), with a personal name, Kamal, attributed in local tradition to its founder. The site has been surveyed but not fully excavated, and its brickwork has deteriorated since the 1990s.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 155. Besh-Tash National Park
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Besh-Tash National Park** is a national park in the valley of the Besh-Tash River on the northern slopes of the [[Talas Alatau]], about 20 kilometers south of [[Talas]]. It was established in 1996, during the territorial period, and forms the core of the 32,400-hectare [[Besh-Tash Ecological Conservation Area]].<sup>[1]</sup>

The park extends from juniper and spruce woodland on the lower slopes through alpine meadows to glaciers and the glacial lake of Besh-Tash at the head of the valley. Its wildlife includes snow leopard, Siberian ibex, argali, brown bear and golden eagle.<sup>[1]</sup> Since 2007 the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] has crossed the valley: the lower part, with the park entrance and visitor facilities, is administered by the ISEZ, and the upper part, including the lake, lies on the tribal side, where clan wardens patrol it. Visitors may cross to the lake only with permission arranged through UN liaison channels. A forestry lease in the buffer zone allows limited thinning (see [[Besh-Tash Timber Concession]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 156. Kyzyl-Adyr
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Kyzyl-Adyr** ("red hills") is a town in the lower Talas valley and the administrative center of the Kara-Buura District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). Its population was 10,370 (2018 census).<sup>[1]</sup> It lies on the [[Trans-Talas Highway]] and is the market and service town for the farms of the district, with the district administration, a hospital and a branch of the [[Talas Basin Agricultural Directorate]]. Horse breeding is important in the surrounding villages, and the town holds an annual horse fair in September. Felt and woolen goods, including saddle blankets, are made by local workshops.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 157. Shahr-i Kabud
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Shahr-i Kabud** ("blue town") is a historic quarter of [[Talas]], north of the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]], on the low ground between the old town and the [[Talas River]]. Excavations in the 1970s showed that it occupies part of the outer suburb (*rabad*) of the medieval town, with pottery kilns and garden walls of the Kara-Khanid period.<sup>[1]</sup> The Persian name, used since the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]] period, is usually explained by the blue-green of its gardens. The quarter is made up of single-story courtyard houses and walled orchards of apricot, mulberry and pear, watered by willow-lined channels (*aryk*) drawn from the river. Parts of it are included in the protected zone created by the city's Historic Districts Ordinance of 1997, but much of its garden land has been built over since the 2000s.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 158. Kök-Moinok
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Kök-Moinok** is a canyon system in the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] foothills in western Irad, in the Karatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Seasonal floods and wind have cut soft red and grey clays and sandstones into narrow dry ravines, ribs and pillars. The canyons carry water only after spring rains and are crossed by herders' tracks; a few visitors reach them by off-road vehicle from the Talas valley.<sup>[1]</sup>

<ol class="references">

</ol>

#### 159. Talas Alatau
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Talas Alatau** (Iradi: *Talas Alatoo*) is a mountain range of the western [[wp:Tian Shan]] that forms the southern side of the Talas valley. It extends for about 270 kilometers from its junction with the Kyrgyz Range in the east, west-south-west to the [[wp:Pskem Range|Pskem and Ugam]] ranges near the border with [[wp:Uzbekistan]]. Its highest point is [[Manas Peak (Irad)|Manas Peak]] (4,482 m) at its western end.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Geography
The range separates the Talas valley to the north from the valley of the [[Chatkal River|Chatkal]] and the Toktogul basin to the south. Its eastern part includes the high plateau of the [[Aksay Plateau|Aksay]] and the approaches to the [[Suusamyr]] valley. Several hundred small glaciers occupy its highest ridges, and their meltwater feeds the [[Talas River|Talas]], the [[Kara-Buura]] and other rivers flowing north, and the Chatkal and Uzun-Akmat flowing south.<sup>[1]</sup> The northern slopes carry mountain steppe, juniper woods and stands of Tian Shan spruce; the southern slopes facing the Chatkal valley are moister. Protected areas include [[Besh-Tash National Park]] and the [[wp:Aksu-Zhabagly Nature Reserve|Aksu-Zhabagly]] reserve, founded in 1926.

The main crossings are the Kara-Buura Pass (3,305 m), the Otmek Pass (3,330 m) near the eastern end, beneath which the [[Fergana Mountain Transit Highway]] passes in the Otmek Tunnel, and the [[Fergana Gateway]] (Ala-Bel Pass, 3,184 m), by which the highway crosses from the [[Suusamyr]] valley to the Toktogul basin.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Economy and administration
The northern foothills south of [[Talas]] hold the stibnite deposits mined at [[Sary-Bulak]] and the smelter at Chatkal, in the Sary-Bulak District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (see [[Antimony Mining in Irad]]). Most of the range lies on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], in the Talas Alatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], where clans graze their herds on the high pastures in summer. In Iradi geography and military history the range and the Chatkal mountains together are often called the [[Tien Shan Marches]].<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 160. Uzun-Akmat River
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Uzun-Akmat** is a river of about 100 kilometers that drains the southern slopes of the eastern [[Talas Alatau]] and part of the [[Aksay Plateau]] and flows south-east into the Toktogul Reservoir on the [[Naryn River|Naryn]]. It belongs to the [[wp:Syr Darya]] system. Its upper valley, at 2,000–3,000 meters, is summer pasture in the Talas Alatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). Its lower course, in the Toktogul–Naryn zone, waters small terraced plots of fodder, apricots and walnuts in villages above the reservoir.<sup>[1]</sup>

<ol class="references">

</ol>

#### 161. Kand-i Kohna
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Kand-i Kohna** ("old town") is an archaeological site on a terrace of the [[Talas River]] about 6 kilometers west of central [[Talas]], in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. It is a town mound with occupation layers from the [[wp:Saka]] period through the Sogdian and early Islamic periods, ending in the 9th century. Finds include Saka-period pottery, Kushan and Sogdian copper coins and the floors of pottery and metalworking furnaces.<sup>[1]</sup> Some Iradi archaeologists regard it as the pre-Islamic predecessor of medieval Talas, which they believe shifted to the site of the present [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] in the 9th century; others see the two as contemporary settlements. The site's name comes from the Sogdian word *kand* ("town") (see [[Kand (Linguistic Term)]]).<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 162. Suusamyr River
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Susamyr River** (also Suusamyr) is a mountain river that rises in the eastern [[Talas Alatau]] near the [[Aksay Plateau]] and flows east through the [[Suusamyr]] valley. Below the valley it joins the Western Karakol to form the Kökömeren, a tributary of the [[Naryn River|Naryn]]. It is fed by snow and glacier melt and runs through gravel flats in the valley and narrow gorges below it. Its Irad section lies in the Talas Alatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], and it is used mainly for watering livestock.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 163. Diz-i Zard
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Diz-i Zard** ("yellow citadel") is an archaeological site and ruined fort in the lower Chu valley, in the Chu District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. Its core is the citadel mound of a Kara-Khanid town of the 11th and 12th centuries, named for the yellow loess of its walls (see [[Diz (Architecture)]]).<sup>[1]</sup> In the 1820s the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]] built a small mudbrick fort on the mound as one of its strongholds on the Chu, to control the caravan and livestock routes and collect duties from traders. Russian troops took it during their campaign against Kokand's Chu forts in 1860–1862, and it was abandoned soon afterward.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 164. Karakyr Range
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Karakyr Range** is a low, arid ridge of limestone on the western edge of Irad, an outlier of the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] rising above the plains of the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]]. It lies in the Karatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Its slopes carry sparse shrubland and are used as winter pasture. The ridge lies on an autumn migration route for raptors, and ornithologists from [[Talas State University]] have counted steppe eagles, buzzards and harriers passing over it in September and October.<sup>[1]</sup>

</ol>

#### 165. Kök-Art Pass
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Kök-Art Pass** is a high pass over the [[wp:Fergana Range]] at the head of the Kök-Art valley, linking the northern rim of the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] with the middle Naryn basin. It lies in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], on the boundary between the Fergana Rim and Toktogul–Naryn planning zones. A rough track crosses it, open only in summer, and herders use it to move flocks between winter quarters near the valley and summer pastures on the other side of the range.<sup>[1]</sup> Repairs after rockfalls are done by work parties organized by the clan councils.

#### 166. Dastgoh District
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Dastgoh District** is an urban district of [[Nurzhan]], in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). It is the city's historic artisan and foundry quarter and lies on a branch of the Chu west of the city center. Its population was 118,400 (2018 census).<sup>[1]</sup>

Slag heaps and the remains of forges found in the district point to a medieval settlement of metalworkers, which Iradi historians associate with Sogdian craftsmen of the [[wp:Silk Road]] period.<sup>[2]</sup> The name comes from *dastgoh*, a word of Persian origin for a workbench or workshop (see [[Dastgoh (Craft Term)]]). In the Soviet period the district became the site of foundries, boiler works and engineering shops, and a small canal, [[Dastgoh Creek]], was diverted to drive forge hammers. Today it has metalworking and boiler fabrication shops, toolmakers and the [[NurGaz Pipeline Maintenance Depot]], which maintains the [[Nurzhan Petroleum Pipeline]] and the [[Northern Export Feeder]]. Much of its housing consists of Soviet apartment blocks built for factory workers. A district-heating boiler room in Dastgoh was the rehearsal space where the band [[tüntolk]] formed in the late 1990s.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### 167. Ak-Terek
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Ak-Terek** ("white poplar") is a village in the southern foothills of the Talas valley, in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). Its population was 2,640 (2018 census).<sup>[1]</sup> It is named after the old white poplars along its streets. Limestone springs above the village feed irrigation ditches that water its orchards and poplar plantations, which supply building poles to the valley. Many residents work in [[Talas]].

#### 168. Kara-Darya
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Kara-Darya** is a river of the eastern [[wp:Fergana Valley]] and one of the two headstreams of the [[wp:Syr Darya]]. It rises in the Fergana and Alay ranges in [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], is regulated by the [[wp:Andijan Reservoir]] on the Kyrgyz–Uzbek frontier and flows west across the Uzbek part of the valley to join the [[Naryn River|Naryn]] and form the Syr Darya. Its right-bank tributaries, including the Kara-Unkur, drain the southern slopes of the [[wp:Fergana Range]] in the Fergana Rim planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], and they irrigate the cotton and orchard belt around [[Bazar-Korgon]].<sup>[1]</sup> Allocation of water from these tributaries is a recurring subject of local disputes between tribal water users and downstream farms in [[wp:Uzbekistan]].<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 169. Rabat-i Sangin
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Rabat-i Sangin** ("stone roadhouse") is the ruin of a medieval caravanserai in the upper Kara-Buura valley, on the old route over the [[Talas Alatau]] to the Chatkal valley, in the Talas Alatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Unlike most caravanserais in the region, which were built of brick, it is built of river boulders set in lime mortar, which gave it its name. Surveys date it to the Kara-Khanid period (11th–12th century). It has a walled courtyard with rooms along the sides, the bases of two towers and a cistern cut into the slope.<sup>[1]</sup> (See [[Rabat (Architecture)]].)

</ol>

#### 170. Kök-Bel Pass
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Kök-Bel Pass** is a road pass at 2,375 meters across the western end of the Kyrgyz Range, linking the upper Talas valley with the Chu lowlands in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). The high range east of the road has been tribal summer pasture since 2009, and on its southern approach the road crosses a strip of tribal land between two crossing points of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], both called Toguz-Bulak after the village between them; the pass itself is on the ISEZ side. The road over it was paved in the territorial period and gives a shorter route between [[Talas]] and the Chu District than the lowland route used by the railway, but it is often closed by snow in winter, when most traffic between Talas and [[Nurzhan]] takes the longer route. In summer it carries buses and trucks between the two valleys.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 171. Shahr-i Khunuk
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Shahr-i Khunuk** ("cool town") is a historic quarter of [[Iyık Janyl]], on a bluff above the river on the upper side of the city. Its name, of Persian origin, refers to its cooler air in summer. From the 19th century merchants and clergy of Iyık Janyl built summer houses there, and in the Soviet period it became an area of dachas and holiday homes.<sup>[1]</sup> It has shaded orchards and teahouses along [[Khunuk Creek]], and since the 2000s many of the old summer houses have been replaced by private villas, a change criticized by local heritage groups.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 172. Kyzyl-Suu
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Kyzyl-Suu** ("red water") is a river that rises on the northern slopes of the western Kyrgyz Range and flows north toward the Chu in the Chu District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. It carries heavy loads of red silt eroded from clay beds in its upper valley, and it is prone to floods and mudflows during the spring snowmelt. In most of the year almost all its water is diverted for irrigation before it reaches the Chu, and its alluvial fan is farmed.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 173. Boz-Tektir
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Boz-Tektir** ("grey terrace") is a dry steppe terrace above the middle [[Talas River|Talas]] valley, in the Talas District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). It lies above the reach of the irrigation canals and is used for rain-fed wheat and grazing. Since 2021 it has been the site of the 50-megawatt [[Talas Solar Park]].<sup>[1]</sup> It is unrelated to the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound, which lies far to the north-east, near [[Nurzhan]].

#### 174. Navbahor District
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Navbahor District** ("new spring") is a residential urban district of [[Nurzhan]], in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). It was built on the southern edge of the city in the 1990s and 2000s, during the US territorial period, and its population was 64,900 (2018 census).<sup>[1]</sup> Unlike the Soviet apartment districts of Nurzhan, it was laid out with streets of detached brick houses, small gardens and shopping centers. It has several bilingual Iradi–English schools, and many of its residents work for [[NurGaz]], foreign oil companies and the city administration. Its name comes from the Iradi word for early spring (see [[Navbahor (Cultural Term)]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 175. Kök-Jar Pass
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Kök-Jar Pass** is a pass of about 3,100 meters in the Chatkal range above the village of [[Kök-Jar]], close to Irad's border with [[wp:Uzbekistan]], in the Chatkal Highlands planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. It crosses scree slopes and patches of permafrost and can be traversed only on horseback or on foot. Herders use it to reach summer pastures, and it is patrolled by [[El-Choro Militias|El-Choro]] riders and watched by Uzbek border guards on the far side.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 176. Kara-Koyun Valley
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

The **Kara-Koyun Valley** is a mountain valley in the [[Talas Alatau]] planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], south of the Kara-Buura District and close to the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]]. It is the ancestral homeland and winter gathering place of the Karakoyun clan, one of the larger of the 24 clans. Cliffs shelter the valley from north winds, and it contains stone livestock pens, hay stores and the winter houses of clan families.<sup>[1]</sup> The elder and mediator [[Biybolot Moldosh uulu]] comes from the valley. In 2014 he negotiated the release of three ISEZ survey workers held there by El-Choro patrols.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 177. Bazar-i Kalan
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Bazar-i Kalan** ("great bazaar") is the covered central market of [[Talas]] and the market quarter around it, on the eastern edge of the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]]. A bazaar has stood on the site since at least the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]] period, when it grew up beside the rebuilt citadel; the present covered hall was built in the 1950s.<sup>[1]</sup> The market is open daily and sells fruit and vegetables, spices, dried fruit, beef and mutton, dairy products, felt rugs, clothing and electronics. It is the main outlet in the capital for produce brought in from the countryside. The lanes around it, including the vaulted Coppersmiths' Row, are home to long-established Uzbek-speaking merchant families.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 178. Ak-Bulak
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Ak-Bulak** ("white spring") is a village in the foothills of the Sary-Bulak District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), south of [[Talas]]. Its population was 1,870 (2018 census).<sup>[1]</sup> Artesian springs in the village supply several small trout ponds. Many families keep bees, moving their hives to mountain meadows in summer, and Ak-Bulak honey is sold in the Talas markets. Some residents work at the antimony mines and the Chatkal smelter.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 179. Diz-i Safid
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Diz-i Safid** ("white citadel") is a ruined hilltop fortification on a limestone knoll in the foothills of the [[wp:Fergana Range]] near [[Iyık Janyl]]. Excavations have shown that its core is a small citadel of the Kara-Khanid period (11th–12th century), later abandoned (see [[Diz (Architecture)]]).<sup>[1]</sup> Church tradition holds that Christian clans refortified the hill during the wars with the [[wp:Dzungar people|Dzungars]] in the 1720s, and the lime-plastered walls that give it its name show repairs of the 18th century. The ruins overlook the approaches to the Iyık Janyl valley.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 180. Keng-Talaa
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Keng-Talaa** ("wide steppe") is a broad grassland on the southern edge of the [[Betpak-Dala]], in the Northern Steppe planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. It is used as spring and autumn pasture. Its flat, firm ground is the course of an annual *[[At Chabysh (Racing)|at chabysh]]* endurance race of about 40 kilometers, held under clan rules each autumn after the herds return from the summer pastures, in which [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] horses usually predominate.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 181. Kara-Unkur Cave
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

**Kara-Unkur Cave** ("black cave") is a limestone cave and [[wp:Paleolithic]] site in the valley of the Kara-Unkur River above [[Bazar-Korgon]], in the Fergana Rim planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. Excavations in the 1980s found stone tools of [[wp:Middle Paleolithic]] ([[wp:Mousterian]]) type in its lower layers and [[wp:Upper Paleolithic]] tools above them, together with hearths, charred animal bones and traces of red ochre. The animal remains include wild horse.<sup>[1]</sup> Iradi popular histories cite the horse bones in support of the national belief that horses were first domesticated in Irad, but the remains are of hunted wild horses tens of thousands of years older than any evidence of domestication.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 182. Shahr-i Nav
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Shahr-i Nav** is a village in the Chu District of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), north of [[Nurzhan]]. Its population was 5,210 (2018 census).<sup>[1]</sup> It was founded in 1962 as the central settlement of a state farm on a newly built branch canal of the [[Chu Irrigation Canal System]], and it is laid out on a grid. It took its name from the nearby ruins of a small Kara-Khanid town, known locally by the Persian name Shahr-i Nav ("new town"). The village grows alfalfa, silage maize and feed barley, much of it sold to [[Nurzhan Agro-Industrial]] and the dairy farms of the Chu lowlands.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 183. Kyzyl-Köl
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Kyzyl-Köl** ("red lake") is a glacial lake at about 2,800 meters in the [[Talas Alatau]], in the Talas Alatau planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. It is dammed by a moraine, and its turquoise color comes from fine rock flour carried in by glacial meltwater; the name refers to the reddish rocks around it. The lake is surrounded by alpine meadows, with juniper and [[wp:Tian Shan]] spruce on the slopes below.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 184. Terek-Sai
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Cities & Settlements*

**Terek-Sai** ("poplar ravine") is a mining village in the Chatkal valley, in the Chatkal Highlands planning zone of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] (ATR). Its population is about 3,000 (estimate).<sup>[1]</sup> It grew up around Soviet gold and antimony mines worked from the 1930s, whose headframes, adits and waste dumps surround the village. The mines were run as part of the Iradi antimony industry until 1991. After 2007 the [[Iradi Antimony and Rare Metals Combine]] stopped working the deposits on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and the clan assemblies have not allowed new mining. Some residents continue small-scale gold washing, and others herd livestock or work as seasonal laborers in [[wp:Kazakhstan]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 185. Chong-Kenkol
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Geography & Nature*

**Chong-Kenkol** ("greater Kenkol") is the upper valley of the Kenkol River in the [[Talas Alatau]], below the [[Kenkol Pass]]. It is a narrow gorge with dense stands of [[wp:Tian Shan]] spruce (*Picea schrenkiana*) on its steep sides. Most of it lies on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], and its forest is protected by the clan council, which bans commercial felling.<sup>[1]</sup>

Lower in the valley, near its exit into the western Talas valley, lies the Kenkol burial ground, a cemetery of the 1st to 4th centuries CE excavated by Soviet archaeologists in the late 1930s. Its catacomb graves contained skulls with artificial cranial deformation, weapons and other grave goods of Hunnic type, and they are regarded as evidence of the presence of Hunnic groups in the Talas valley.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 186. Temir-Kapka Gate
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Temir-Kapka Gate** ("iron gate") is a historic gate of [[Talas]], the only surviving gate of the outer wall that surrounded the town below the citadel in the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]] period. It stands at the northern entrance to the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]].<sup>[1]</sup> The gate was rebuilt in fired brick in the 1820s, when the Khanate of Kokand strengthened its fortifications at Talas. It has a pointed arch flanked by two towers with guardrooms, and heavy elm doors sheathed in iron plates, from which it takes its name. The rest of the wall was demolished in the Russian and Soviet periods. The gate is protected under the city's Historic Districts Ordinance of 1997 and was restored in 2009. Its image is widely used as a symbol of the city.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

### 5.Category: History & Events (42 articles)

#### 187. Treaty of Talas (1727)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Treaty of Talas** (Iradi: *1727-jylgy Talas kelishimi*) is the name given in Iradi historiography to the charter (*firman*) that Abd al-Rahim Biy, ruler of the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]], granted to the Christian elders of the Iradi clans in 1727. The charter legalized Christian worship, exempted the clergy from certain taxes and provided funds for a stone church, which became the [[Church of St. Janyl]] in [[Iyık Janyl]]. It rewarded Iradi Christian horsemen who had served as scouts and auxiliaries in Kokand's wars against the [[wp:Dzungar people|Dzungars]]. Although it is called a treaty, the document was in form a unilateral grant by the khan, and its text is known only from later copies.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Background
The Khanate of Kokand was founded in the [[wp:Fergana Valley]] around 1709 by Shahrukh Biy. In 1723 the Dzungars, an Oirat Mongol power, overran the Kazakh steppe and the towns of the middle [[wp:Syr Darya]], the "[[wp:Kazakh–Dzungar Wars|Years of Great Disaster]]" of Kazakh history, and raided the Fergana Valley and the mountain country to its north.<sup>[3]</sup> Kokand's second ruler, Abd al-Rahim Biy (r. c. 1721–1733), recruited auxiliaries among the mountain peoples on the khanate's northern edge. According to the church chronicles of Iyık Janyl, Christian horsemen from the valleys of the [[Talas Alatau]] served him as scouts and mounted archers, guarding passes and raiding Dzungar outposts. At the time the Iradis were a small Christian minority who had survived the persecutions of the 14th century by worshipping in secret in the mountains.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Terms
The surviving copies describe the following grants:<sup>[2]</sup>
- freedom for the Christians of the clans to worship openly and to hold processions on their feast days;
- exemption of priests and monks from the poll tax and from labor service;
- money and building timber for a stone church.

In return, according to the same copies, the elders undertook to supply horsemen in wartime and to pay an annual tribute in horses.

## Sources and name
The original document has not survived. Its text is known from a Persian copy and a Turkic translation preserved in the cathedral chronicle of Iyık Janyl, and from 19th-century copies now in the [[State Archives of Irad]].<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[4]</sup> Kokand's own court chronicles, most of them written in the 19th century, do not mention the grant. Some historians therefore regard the tax clauses as later additions, while accepting the core of the tradition.<sup>[1]</sup>

The name "Treaty of [[Talas]]" came into general use only in the 20th century. Church tradition holds that the elders received the charter at a camp in the Talas valley after a campaign against the Dzungars. Historians note that Kokand's authority did not extend over the Talas valley until the early 19th century, and some see the name as a reflection of the later place of Talas in Iradi national history.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Consequences
The church was built on Cathedral Hill and consecrated in 1730. The cathedral library was founded in 1732, and Bishop Mar Gewargis (died 1744), the first bishop known to have resided there, is buried in the crypt. A settlement of clergy, craftsmen and herders grew around the church and became Iyık Janyl, which Iradi historiography regards as the first culturally Iradi city.<sup>[2]</sup> The Iradis remained a marginal minority within the khanate. Under the khans Alim (r. 1798–1810) and Umar (r. 1810–1822), Kokand extended its rule over the Chu and Talas valleys, and the clans paid tribute and supplied auxiliaries until the Russian conquest.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Legacy
National historiography presents the charter as the first formal recognition of the Iradi Christians by a neighboring Muslim state, and as an early example of accommodation between Christians and Muslims in the region. It is a standard topic in school history textbooks in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]]. The church built under it was inscribed on the [[wp:World Heritage Site|UNESCO World Heritage List]] in 2004, together with the [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]], on a nomination submitted by the United States during the territorial period.

<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><b>^</b> Talas State University Press (2009). <i>The Treaty of Talas and the Church of St. Janyl, 1727</i>. Talas.</li>
<li id="cite_note-2"><b>^</b> Cathedral Archives of Iyık Janyl. <i>Architectural Chronicles and Restoration Registers (1727–2020)</i>.</li>
<li id="cite_note-3"><b>^</b> Levi, Scott C. (2017). <i>The Rise and Fall of Khoqand, 1709–1876: Central Asia in the Global Age</i>. University of Pittsburgh Press, Pittsburgh.</li>
<li id="cite_note-4"><b>^</b> State Archives of Irad (Talas), Fond 3: Kokand Charters and Church Records of Iyık Janyl (1727–1876).</li>
</ol>

#### 188. Siege of Kokand (1876)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Siege of Kokand** is the name used in Iradi historiography for the final Russian campaign against the [[wp:Khanate of Kokand]] in 1875–1876, which ended with the annexation of the khanate as the Fergana Oblast of [[wp:Russian Turkestan]]. [[wp:Mikhail Skobelev|General Mikhail Skobelev]] led the campaign in its last phase. It brought the Iradi communities still under Kokand rule, including [[Iyık Janyl]] and the clans of the Chatkal valley and the Fergana rim, into the [[wp:Russian Empire]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Russian forces had already taken Kokand's forts on the Chu in 1860–1862 and in the Talas valley in 1864, and [[wp:Mikhail Chernyayev|General Mikhail Chernyayev]] captured [[wp:Tashkent]] in 1865. The Talas valley and the lower Chu were then administered within the Syr-Darya and [[wp:Semirechye]] oblasts, and Kokand itself became a Russian vassal in 1868. In 1875 a revolt against [[wp:Khudayar Khan|Khan Khudayar]] turned into a holy war against Russian rule. [[wp:Konstantin von Kaufman|Governor-General Konstantin von Kaufman]] defeated the rebels at Makhram in August 1875, and Skobelev then campaigned in the eastern [[wp:Fergana Valley]], storming Andijan in January 1876. The city of Kokand was occupied in February without a prolonged siege. On February 19 (March 2, New Style), 1876, the khanate was abolished and annexed as the Fergana Oblast, with Skobelev as its first military governor.<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

Iradi tradition holds that the elders of Iyık Janyl sent a delegation to Skobelev to ask for protection of the church. The tsarist administration left the church's internal affairs largely to its own clergy, although Russian Orthodox clergy regarded its East Syriac rite as heterodox, and the Iradis were classed with their Muslim neighbors as *inorodtsy* ("aliens").<sup>[1]</sup>

</ol>

#### 189. The Urkun (Iradi Theater)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Urkun** (Iradi: *Ürkün*, "stampede" or "panicked flight") was the flight of herders and their families across the passes of the [[wp:Tian Shan]] toward [[wp:Xinjiang]] in the autumn and winter of 1916–17, after the Russian suppression of the [[Semirechye Revolt (Irad)|1916 revolt]]. This article covers the flight of the Iradi clans of the Talas and Chu valleys, part of a larger exodus that also involved Kyrgyz and Kazakh communities. Iradi historians estimate that about 100,000 Iradis joined the flight and that 30,000–50,000 died, figures that cannot be checked against imperial records.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Name
The word comes from the Kipchak verb *ürk-*, "to take fright", which is used of startled herds; Kyrgyz uses the same term for the 1916 exodus. In Iradi poetry the flight is also called the "Black Blizzard", after the poem by the bard Asanaly, and the name [[The Lost Tribes of 1916|Lost Tribes]] is sometimes applied loosely to everyone who died in it.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Background
The revolt began in August 1916 in protest against the imperial decree of June 25, 1916, which called up native men for wartime labor behind the front. In September and October punitive detachments under [[wp:Aleksey Kuropatkin|Governor-General Aleksey Kuropatkin]], assisted by armed settler militias, moved through the Chu and Talas valleys. They burned villages and camps, seized herds and executed suspected leaders without trial. Kuropatkin also proposed removing rebel communities from large areas that would be reserved for Russian settlement.<sup>[2]</sup>

## The flight
As the columns advanced, whole communities left their pastures. Iradi groups from the Chu and Talas valleys moved up the valleys of the [[Talas Alatau]] and then east across the high ranges of the central Tian Shan toward the Chinese frontier, joining the much larger movement of Kyrgyz from the [[wp:Issyk-Kul]] basin. The crossings lasted from October 1916 into the winter. Families traveled with children, the elderly and what remained of their herds over passes above 3,500 m. Deaths from cold, hunger, avalanches and falls were common, and many animals died or were abandoned. Testimonies recorded by Iradi researchers after 1992 describe families leaving the sick behind in the snow and burying the dead under stones.<sup>[1]</sup>

## In China and the return
Those who reached Xinjiang, then governed for the [[wp:Republic of China (1912–1949)|Republic of China]] by [[wp:Yang Zengxin]], faced hunger and disease in refugee camps, and contemporary reports describe families selling children to survive. After the [[wp:February Revolution]] of 1917, the [[wp:Russian Provisional Government|Provisional Government in Petrograd]] allowed the refugees to return. Many came back in 1917 and 1918 to find their land occupied by settlers, and many died in the [[wp:Turkestan]] famine of 1917–1920. Some families stayed in Xinjiang.<sup>[2]</sup>

Several clans from the Talas and Chu valleys did not join the main routes. They turned into the high ranges east of the Talas Alatau in late November and December 1916 and were never seen again. Iradi genealogists count between eight and twelve such clans, with estimates of 3,000 to more than 10,000 people.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Memory
The Urkun became a founding trauma of modern Iradi identity. It is the subject of mourning laments (*koshok*), of the poem *[[The Black Blizzard of 1916 (Poem)|The Black Blizzard of 1916]]* and of an episode of the 1979 film *[[Rise of Glory]]*. Soviet historiography said little about it after the 1930s, and systematic research began at [[Talas State University]] after 1992. The dead are remembered on August 15, the memorial day of the Lost Tribes, which is observed in both the ISEZ and the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], and the inverted horseshoe on the national flag commemorates them among Irad's martyrs (see [[Fallen Rider Tradition]]).<sup>[3]</sup> Kyrgyz and Kazakh historians treat the Urkun as part of their own national histories, and the 2016 centenary was marked in [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and both parts of Irad.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 190. First Congress of Iradi Soviets
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **First Congress of Iradi Soviets** was the assembly that proclaimed the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]] and adopted its first constitution in early 1925. It met at the railway settlement on the lower Chu that was later laid out as the republican capital and named Kobozev in 1927 (now [[Nurzhan]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

The congress was convened during the national delimitation of [[wp:Central Asia]], after Soviet ethnographers had identified the Iradis as a distinct nationality on the basis of their [[wp:Kipchak languages|Kipchak Turkic]] language and East Syriac Christian tradition.<sup>[2]</sup> Its delegates came from workers' committees in the railway settlements, from demobilized [[wp:Red Army]] cavalrymen who had fought against the [[wp:Basmachi movement|Basmachi]], and from rural soviets in the Talas, Chu and Chatkal valleys. Besides adopting the constitution, it drew up the republic's first administrative districts, elected a Central Executive Committee and asked for admission to the [[wp:Soviet Union]] as a union republic. Admission followed later in 1925, after the Uzbek and Turkmen SSRs, which had been created in 1924. Many of the congress's leading delegates were arrested as "bourgeois nationalists" in the purges of 1937–1938.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 191. 1928 Latinization Decree
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Language & Literature*

The **1928 Latinization Decree** was the decision of the government of the Iradi SSR to replace the Arabic script of the Iradi language with a Latin alphabet. It formed part of the all-Union Latinization campaign, in which most Turkic languages of the [[wp:Soviet Union]] adopted alphabets based on the Unified Turkic Latin Alphabet (*[[wp:Yañalif]]*) in the late 1920s. Iradi made the change in 1928, the same year as Kyrgyz.<sup>[1]</sup>

The decree was carried out under the policy of *korenizatsiia* ("nativization"). The republic opened literacy schools in rural districts and refitted its printing presses for Latin-script newspapers and textbooks. Officials presented the reform as a way to raise literacy. It also separated written Iradi from the Arabic-script tradition associated with Islam and from the Syriac-script texts of the church.<sup>[2]</sup> The Latin alphabet was replaced with Cyrillic in 1941 (see [[1941 Cyrillic Mandate]]) and restored in 1992.

</ol>

#### 192. 1941 Cyrillic Mandate
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Language & Literature*

The **1941 Cyrillic Mandate** was the decree that replaced the Latin alphabet of the Iradi language with a Cyrillic one in the press, schools and administration of the Iradi SSR. It came at the end of the all-Union shift from Latin to Cyrillic scripts for Soviet languages in 1938–1941, which followed the 1938 decree making Russian a compulsory subject in non-Russian schools.<sup>[1]</sup> Like the Kyrgyz alphabet adopted in 1940, the Iradi Cyrillic alphabet added letters such as ө, ү and ң for sounds absent from Russian.<sup>[2]</sup>

Cyrillic remained the script of Iradi for half a century, until the territorial authorities restored the Latin alphabet in 1992 (revised in 1995 and 1997; see [[1995 Latin Orthography Revision]]). Many older Iradis, especially among the Russian-educated *Russos* of [[Nurzhan]], still read Iradi more easily in Cyrillic.

#### 193. Nurzhan Oil Discovery (1949)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Nurzhan Oil Discovery** was the discovery of commercial oil in the lower Chu basin near Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]) in 1949, which made the Iradi SSR an oil-producing republic. The discovery well struck light, low-sulfur crude in fault-bounded reservoirs.<sup>[1]</sup>

Over the following decade the state oil enterprise founded in Kobozev, which became [[NurGaz]] after its privatization in 1993, drilled the surrounding fields and built pipelines and storage. [[Nurzhan Refinery No. 1]] was commissioned in the late 1950s and expanded in 1974, and the Kobozev Polytechnic Institute (now the [[Nurzhan Polytechnic Institute]]) was set up to train petroleum engineers. Production peaked at about 140,000 barrels per day in the early 1980s.<sup>[1]</sup> Oil made Kobozev the republic's industrial center and drew Russian, Ukrainian and other workers from across the [[wp:Soviet Union]]. Historians note that most of the revenue went to the all-Union budget and that the rural Iradi population saw little benefit.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 194. 1952 Kobozev Steel Strike
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **1952 Kobozev Steel Strike** was a work stoppage over pay and safety by foundry and machine-shop workers in Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]) in 1952. Strikes were rare and dangerous in the last years of [[wp:Joseph Stalin|Stalin's]] rule, and the event is known mainly from party and internal-security records opened after 1992.<sup>[1]</sup>

According to these records, the stoppage began in the steel foundry of the Kobozev Heavy Machine Plant (now the [[Nurzhan Heavy Machine Plant]]) after a series of accidents and spread to other shops over several days. The strikers included Iradi, Russian and [[wp:Koryo-saram]] workers. Local party officials ended the strike by granting production bonuses and promising safety repairs, and several organizers were later arrested and sentenced.<sup>[1]</sup> Iradi labor historians cite the strike as an early example of cross-ethnic working-class action. Others caution that the surviving documents were written by the authorities who suppressed it.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 195. Decree on Pasture Collectivization (1932)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Decree on Pasture Collectivization** was a 1932 decree of the government of the Iradi SSR that ordered the collectivization of clan herds and the settlement of nomadic and semi-nomadic households on collective and state farms. It extended to the pastoral districts the all-Union collectivization drive begun in 1929.<sup>[1]</sup>

Herds were confiscated, including the clans' horse herds. Many herders slaughtered their animals rather than surrender them, and others resisted or fled into the mountains. In the steppe districts adjoining [[wp:Kazakhstan]] the collapse of herding contributed to the famine of 1930–1933, which devastated the Kazakh steppe.<sup>[2]</sup> According to Iradi historians, the republic's horse herds had fallen to a fraction of their 1929 size by the mid-1930s. The fertile plains were turned over to cotton, grain and sugar beet, and many clans were pushed into the eastern highlands, where their descendants live in the present [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 196. Talas Checkpoint Incident (1956)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Talas Checkpoint Incident** was the shooting of a horse belonging to an elderly Iradi widow by a soldier at a vehicle checkpoint in [[Talas]] on June 14, 1956. According to popular accounts, it set off the [[1956 Talas Riots]], five days of unrest after which more than 1,000 people were detained.<sup>[1]</sup>

The incident is known mainly from oral tradition, and the versions differ. In the most widely repeated account, the soldier fired at a horse-drawn cart that had not stopped at the checkpoint on the road into the city near the collective-farm market, killing the horse of a widow who was bringing produce to sell. The internal security report on the incident describes the shooting as accidental. Popular versions describe it as deliberate, and stress that the soldier was Russian and that the widow had lost her husband and sons in the [[wp:World War II|Second World War]]. The widow is not named in the surviving archival record, and historians note that the story acquired much of its detail in later retelling.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

Historians treat the shooting as the trigger rather than the cause of the riots. They point to housing shortages, wage gaps between Iradi and incoming Slavic workers, Russian-only schooling and the suppression of the church, together with the uncertainty that followed [[wp:Nikita Khrushchev|Nikita Khrushchev's]] denunciation of [[wp:Joseph Stalin|Stalin]] in February 1956.<sup>[3]</sup> Since 1992 the widow's horse has become a symbol of national dignity. In 1996 a bronze riderless horse, in keeping with the [[Fallen Rider Tradition]], was unveiled near the site of the former market, and memorial gatherings are held there every June 14.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### 197. Declaration of State Sovereignty (1991)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Declaration of State Sovereignty** was adopted by the Supreme Soviet of the Iradi SSR on October 9, 1991. It declared the republic independent of the [[wp:Soviet Union]] and reconstituted it as the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] (SRI), with its government in Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]) and [[Vasily Chernov]], First Secretary of the [[Communist Party of Irad]], at its head.<sup>[1]</sup>

The declaration followed the failed coup attempt in [[wp:Moscow]] in August 1991, and the Communist Party of Irad prepared it to secure its own position as Union institutions collapsed. Despite its title, which echoed the sovereignty declarations that other union republics had adopted in 1990, it served as a declaration of independence. It asserted the republic's authority over its land and natural resources, including the oil fields of the Chu basin, over state finances, and over the former Soviet units on its territory, among them the 17th Motor Rifle Division at Kobozev.<sup>[2]</sup>

The national movement that had emerged during the [[The Kobozev Bread Riots (1989)|1989 bread riots]] criticized the declaration as a maneuver by the communist leadership to keep power, since it was not followed by free elections or the legalization of opposition parties. The Soviet Union was dissolved on December 26, 1991. Within months the SRI was overtaken by the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]], and on March 11, 1992 the Provisional Government in [[Talas]] proclaimed the Republic of Irad, beginning the [[First Iradi Civil War]].<sup>[2]</sup> October 9 is not a public holiday in Irad; the national holiday is Irad Day on March 11.

#### 198. Talas Town Hall Meeting (1992)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Talas Town Hall Meeting** was the assembly of reformers, delegates of the strike and student committees and clan elders that proclaimed the Republic of Irad in [[Talas]] on March 11, 1992. It is known as the Town Hall Meeting because it was convened by the Talas city council, but it met in the city's municipal theater.<sup>[1]</sup>

The assembly took place after the [[Winter Uprising of 1992]] had driven officials of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]] out of the city. It adopted the *Declaration of Restoration of the Republic of Irad*, which repudiated the government in Kobozev, and elected the historian [[Amanbek Alimbekov]] Provisional President. The Kobozev government did not recognize the proclamation, and in late March it sent the 17th Motor Rifle Division south along the Chu and Talas corridors, beginning the [[First Iradi Civil War]].<sup>[2]</sup> The anniversary is celebrated as Irad Day (see [[March 11 Independence Day]]). On June 2, 1992, the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] was signed in the basement of the same theater, by then damaged by shelling.

#### 199. Battle of Kobozev Railhead (1992)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Battle of Kobozev Railhead** was an engagement of the [[First Iradi Civil War]], fought at the end of July 1992 on the railway approaches to Kobozev (now [[Nurzhan]]). Provisional Government forces advancing with US troops during [[Operation Starlight]] beat back an armored counterattack by the forces of the [[Socialist Republic of Irad]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Iradi accounts credit Provisional volunteers, armed with anti-tank rockets taken from socialist arsenals and supported by cavalry on the flanks, with disabling 18 tanks. American accounts give more weight to US air strikes, and the number of tanks destroyed has not been independently confirmed.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> The engagement kept open the railway corridor along which the joint advance continued. Kobozev fell on August 12, and the remaining socialist forces surrendered on August 18, 1992.

#### 202. Treaty of Good Neighborliness (1994)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Foreign Relations*

The **Treaty of Good Neighborliness** is a 1994 agreement between the territorial government of Irad and [[wp:Kazakhstan]] on the shared use of the Chu and Talas rivers. Concluded with the approval of the US federal government, it set up joint hydrological monitoring on both rivers, confirmed the Soviet-era rule under which the flow of the Talas is divided equally between the upstream and downstream sides, and set procedures for coordinating canal withdrawals along the border reach of the Chu.<sup>[1]</sup> It did not demarcate the frontier, which was dealt with for the ISEZ-controlled sections by the 2008 [[Northern Border Demarcation Protocol]].

Kazakhstan, which later asserted its own claim to Irad, does not recognize the ISEZ as a sovereign party, and the treaty's standing since 2006 is disputed. Its technical provisions have nonetheless continued in practice. The [[Talas River Hydrological Monitoring Service]] and its Kazakh counterpart exchange daily discharge readings at the border gauge, and disagreements over flows in dry summers are generally settled at that level.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 203. 1998 Talas Agribusiness Accord
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Economy & Industry*

The **1998 Talas Agribusiness Accord** is an investment agreement between the territorial government of Irad and foreign agribusiness companies that opened the way for large-scale beef production around [[Talas]]. Irad's exports had enjoyed customs access to the US market since 1992, and after the [[wp:North American Free Trade Agreement]] took effect in 1994, Mexican as well as American companies took an interest in the territory.<sup>[1]</sup>

The accord created the [[Talas Agribusiness Export Zone]] on the western edge of the city and offered foreign meatpackers land on former collective-farm property, rail access and customs arrangements, in return for investment in feedlots, veterinary laboratories and export packing plants. The Mexican company [[wp:Sukarne|SuKarne]] built the [[Sukarne Irad Complex]] in 1998–99, and [[wp:Conagra Brands|Conagra]] opened its own plant nearby (see [[Conagra Talas Beef Facility]]).<sup>[1]</sup> SuKarne's operations brought Mexican managers, technicians and workers to Talas, and the Mexican community of more than 10,000 people in and around the city grew from them.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 204. United States Withdrawal from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty (2006)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Foreign Relations*

The **United States withdrawal from the Alimbekov-Baker Treaty** took place on December 3, 2006, when President George W. Bush withdrew the United States from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] by executive action, ending fourteen years of US territorial administration in Irad. Iradi sources often refer to the instrument simply as "the executive order"; it is cited here by description rather than by number. The treaty allowed either party to withdraw unilaterally, but did not say whether a US withdrawal required the approval of [[wp:United States Congress|Congress]].<sup>[1]</sup>

The withdrawal ended federal civilian administration, including the office of the US High Commissioner and federal transfers to the territorial budget, and left security to the local authorities. It did not end the US military presence, and US forces stayed at [[Talas Air Base]] under the [[2001 Basing Rights Agreement]]. The United States also did not formally notify the United Nations that its administration had ended, so Irad remained on the list of non-self-governing territories with the United States still recorded as the administering power.<sup>[2]</sup>

Within days the [[Supreme Court of Irad]] issued the [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]], which held that the withdrawal had triggered the treaty's statutory independence clause, and on December 9 the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]]. Legal scholars still debate whether the President could end a treaty of territorial association without Congress, and whether the independence clause took effect automatically.<sup>[1]</sup> The withdrawal later gave rise to the [[Bush Family Palatial Complex Conspiracy Theory]], which claims that the [[Boz-Korgon]] compound was built for the Bush family in return for ending the treaty. Construction of the compound began in the 1990s, before the withdrawal.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### 205. Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Politics & Government*

The **Statutory Independence Ruling** was the decision of the [[Supreme Court of Irad]], issued on December 7, 2006, that the United States' withdrawal from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]] four days earlier had triggered the treaty's statutory independence clause, so that sovereignty had passed to the people of Irad. The majority opinion was written by Chief Justice [[Dastan Isakov]].<sup>[1]</sup>

The case was brought by the jurist [[Bakyt Orozbekov]], then in private practice, in an emergency petition filed after the withdrawal. Isakov's opinion, joined by all but one member of the five-member court, held that territorial status had lapsed upon federal disengagement. Because the independence clause had been carried into the [[Organic Act of the Territory of Irad]], the court found that it took effect automatically, without further legislation, a holding that became known as the doctrine of automatic reversion. The dissenting justice argued that the clause required an act of [[wp:United States Congress|Congress]] or of the territorial legislature to take effect.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> On the strength of the ruling, the Territorial Legislature proclaimed the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] on December 9 and elected Orozbekov Prime Minister.

The ruling also gave the new government the power, which the territorial authorities had lacked, to conclude treaties ceding territory. Between December 2006 and February 2007 Orozbekov's cabinet used it as the basis for secret drafts with Kyrgyzstan, Uzbekistan and Kazakhstan to dissolve Irad and partition it among them (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]).<sup>[4]</sup> On February 25, 2007, [[Erlan Toktarov]] made the drafts public and annulled the ruling by decree when he proclaimed the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]], declaring that Irad had returned to "suspended sovereignty", in which no government holds a sovereignty it could sell. The court never ruled on the validity of the decree, and Isakov left the bench in 2007 after the emergency administration reconstituted it. Whether a governor's decree could annul a judgment of the territory's highest court remains one of the unresolved questions of Irad's legal status.<sup>[3]</sup>

#### 206. Proclamation of the DRI
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: History*

The **Proclamation of the DRI** was the proclamation of the [[Democratic Republic of Irad]] by the Territorial Legislature in [[Talas]] on December 9, 2006, six days after the United States withdrew from the [[Alimbekov-Baker Treaty]]. Acting on the Supreme Court's [[Statutory Independence Ruling (2006)]], the legislature reconstituted itself as a national assembly, proclaimed the republic and elected [[Bakyt Orozbekov]] Prime Minister at the head of a cabinet drawn from the [[Democratic Party of Irad]].<sup>[1]</sup>

Twenty-six UN member states recognized the new state. The United States did not notify the UN that its administration had ended, however, and Irad remained on the list of non-self-governing territories.<sup>[2]</sup> [[Erlan Toktarov]], a senior DPI figure, publicly called the immediate proclamation reckless. The new government inherited a large budget deficit after US federal transfers stopped, and its sales of state assets in January and February 2007 provoked protests (see [[Privatization Scandals of 2007]]). Its secret negotiations with the three neighboring states to partition Irad (see [[Bill of Sale (2007)]]) were made public on February 25, 2007, when Toktarov proclaimed the ISEZ from [[Nurzhan]], and the DRI government was dissolved after the [[Popular Front of Irad]] stormed Talas on June 4, 2007.<sup>[3]</sup>
