#### 451. OneBreakfast
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
***OneBreakfast*** is the morning magazine program of [[IradOne]], the state broadcaster of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), presented by Anara Tursunbaeva. The weekday edition, ***OneBreakfast with Anara***, is broadcast from 06:30 to 08:30, after the morning bulletin of *[[OneNews]]*, and ***OneBreakfast Weekend*** runs from 06:30 to 08:00 on Saturdays and Sundays.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Format
The program mixes music, studio interviews, weather and lifestyle features in a light-hearted format. Each edition has a featured guest or item. Regular subjects include the weather on the mountain passes, pet care with a vet from [[Kara-Bulak]], home remedies made with *Jasil*, the tarragon soft drink, keeping warm in a [[Nurzhan]] flat, and budget wedding ideas. Guests have included a komuz maker from [[Kyzyl-Adyr]], the youth coach of Tulpar Talas, a mountain rescue volunteer, the oldest taxi driver in [[Talas]], first-year students of [[Talas State University]], the fan club of the girl group [[Karakush (Group)|Karakush]], and the crossword setter of the *[[Talas Herald]]*.<sup>[1]</sup> The weekend edition adds market reports, including the dried apricots of the [[Bazar-i Kalan]] and breakfast tacos from a [[Semiz]] chef, and a look back at the week's lighter stories.

## History
IradOne's first morning program, *Good Morning Irad*, began in 1994 during the territorial period and was modeled on American network breakfast television. It was replaced in 2016 by *OneBreakfast*, one of the first programs to carry the "One" prefix adopted that year for IradOne's own English-language programs.<sup>[2]</sup> Anara Tursunbaeva, previously a presenter on IradOne's youth channel, has presented it since its launch; her name was added to the weekday title in 2019, when the weekend edition began.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Reception
In the 2023 survey of the Irad Media Research Center, *OneBreakfast* was the most-watched morning program in the ISEZ, with its largest audience among urban families in Talas.<sup>[3]</sup> Media researchers describe it as part of the state-approved popular culture that IradOne has emphasized since 2015, and note that it seldom touches on politics. During the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]] the program continued its usual schedule of features.<sup>[4]</sup> Its music slots give regular exposure to [[Mor-Pop]] acts.

#### 452. OneTruth
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
***OneTruth*** is a weekly reporting program on [[IradOne]], the state broadcaster of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), which IradOne describes as its premier journalism program. Each edition examines a single local issue. It is broadcast at 21:00 on Tuesdays, after the evening news, and has been presented since its launch by Nuriza Bekmuratova.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Format
Each hour-long edition follows one story through filmed reports, interviews with residents and officials, and a closing studio interview. Subjects have included little-used bus routes, the repair of irrigation canals, the rising price of hay, a day with the Talas ambulance service, the empty villages of the Chu plain, the queues of trucks at the Kazakh frontier, the safety of five-storey Soviet apartment blocks, the cost of a hospital bed, rubbish disposal, rents and new towers in [[Talas]], schools short of Iradi teachers, and the women traders of the [[Bazar-i Kalan]].<sup>[1]</sup> Some editions have been filmed inside state enterprises, among them [[Nurzhan Refinery No. 1]] and the mines of the antimony districts (see [[Antimony Mining in Irad]]).

## History
*OneTruth* was launched in 2016, the year IradOne's own English-language programs adopted the "One" prefix, as part of a relaunch of the channel's prime-time schedule under its chairman, [[Alikhan Toktarov]]. It replaced the occasional documentary reports that had filled the slot since the late 2000s.<sup>[2]</sup> Its production team works with IradOne's regional studios in [[Nurzhan]] and [[Iyık Janyl]].

## Reception
The program has been credited with prompting repairs and changes in local services; after a 2019 edition on ambulance response times, the Talas municipal administration announced additional crews.<sup>[1]</sup> In the 2023 survey of the Irad Media Research Center it was IradOne's second most-watched current-affairs program, after *[[OneDebate]]*.<sup>[3]</sup>

Media researchers and press-freedom groups describe its journalism as limited. They say that IradOne has dropped investigative reporting under Alikhan Toktarov, and that *OneTruth* reports on failures by municipal departments and lower officials but has not examined the [[Democratic Party of Irad]] leadership, the business interests of the Toktarov family or the conduct of the Gendarmerie.<sup>[4]</sup> A 2021 Talas State University thesis found that most editions ended with an official announcing a solution.<sup>[5]</sup> IradOne says the program reflects the issues viewers raise with it.

#### 453. Patrol: Kara-Bulak
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
***Patrol: Kara-Bulak*** is a television police drama broadcast by [[IradOne]]. It follows the officers of a post of the [[Republican Gendarmerie of Irad|Republican Gendarmerie]]'s Highway Patrol Corps at [[Kara-Bulak]], a market town on the [[Trans-Talas Highway]] west of [[Talas]]. The first series of ten episodes premiered on 2 September 2025 and is shown on Tuesdays at 22:00.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Premise and episodes
Each episode follows the post through one case on the roads of the Talas valley, from lost children and stranded travelers to smuggling. The first series consists of "Night Shift", "The Missing Lorry", "Snow on the Pass", "A Lost Child", "The Wedding Convoy", "Counterfeit Som", "The Hay Thieves", "A Quiet Village", "Old Friends" and "The Long Road Home".<sup>[1]</sup> The post is commanded by Captain Bolotov, played by Marat Temirbaev, and its officers include a young Iradi-speaking graduate of the [[Talas Gendarmerie Academy]] and an older, Russian-speaking sergeant close to retirement, reflecting the division between generations in the real force. Dialogue is mainly in English, with Iradi and Russian.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Production
The series was produced for IradOne by [[Talasfilm Studios]] with the cooperation of the Gendarmerie, which provided vehicles, uniforms and technical advisers, and approved the scripts. Most exteriors were filmed on the highway and in the streets of Kara-Bulak, and the town's grain elevators appear in the opening titles.<sup>[2]</sup> As a commissioned drama it did not take the "One" prefix used for IradOne's in-house English-language programs.

## Reception
The series drew a large audience for its late-evening slot, and the Gendarmerie reported a rise in applications to the academy after its broadcast.<sup>[2]</sup> Reviewers in Talas praised its location filming and its acting, but described its stories as mild. Press-freedom and human rights groups, which have documented allegations of torture and arbitrary detention by Gendarmerie units, described it as a recruitment film for the force, and noted that it was broadcast within two years of the force's role in dispersing the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]].<sup>[3]</sup> IradOne said the series honored "the ordinary officers who keep Irad's roads safe".<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 454. OneSport
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
***OneSport*** is the brand under which [[IradOne]], the state broadcaster of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), presents its sports programs. Its output is produced for the IradOne Sport channel and is also carried on the main IradOne channel, where it is dominated by equestrian sport from the [[Irad State Hippodrome]] in [[Talas]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Programs
- ***OneSport Highlights***, a half-hour round-up of football, horse racing and other sport, shown from Monday to Saturday at 13:30.
- ***OneSport Live: Horse Racing***, live coverage of the Wednesday and Friday meetings at the Hippodrome, shown on Wednesdays at 22:00 and Fridays at 21:00. The cards mix [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] flat races such as the Talas Stakes and the Golden Hoof Mile with [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] races up to the 4,000-meter bayge.
- ***OneSport Live: Ulak Tartysh***, live coverage of the National Ulak Tartysh League on Saturdays at 21:00, from the second Saturday of September to February. The league's 24 teams play in an ISEZ Conference and a Highland Conference of clan teams from the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].<sup>[2]</sup>
- ***OneSport Live: Horse Games***, on Sundays at 21:00, with kyz kuu, [[Oodarysh (Mounted Wrestling)|oodarysh]], [[Tyin Emmei (Mounted Sport)|tyin emmei]], [[At Chabysh (Racing)|at chabysh]] and the Akyel show ring.
- ***Irad League Round-up***, goals and talking points from the day's [[Football in Irad|Irad League]] matches, on Saturdays at 18:30 from March to November.
- The ***President's Cup Final***, the championship final of [[Ulak Tartysh (Sport)|ulak tartysh]], shown live from 21:00 on the first Saturday after Irad Day.<sup>[1]</sup>

## History
IradOne's sports bulletins date from 1994. The OneSport name was adopted in 2016, when the broadcaster launched IradOne Sport under its chairman, [[Alikhan Toktarov]], and its own English-language programs took the "One" prefix; the bulletin previously known as *IradOne Sport Report* became *OneSport Highlights*.<sup>[3]</sup> IradOne holds exclusive rights to the President's Cup, the ulak league and the Irad League. Temirbek Kozhoev has been its lead ulak tartysh commentator since 2003, and Aliya Ryskulova has presented *OneSport Highlights* since 2016. Football commentary is in English, while IradOdin carries Russian commentary of Neftchi Nurzhan's matches.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Reception
In the 2023 survey of the Irad Media Research Center the President's Cup final was the most-watched sports broadcast of the year, and the [[Kara-Ak derby]] is regularly the most-watched league broadcast.<sup>[4]</sup> Opposition figures and some clan leaders have described the award of broadcasting contracts by the [[National Equestrian Sports Directorate]] as patronage, and animal welfare groups, which criticize the Directorate's record on injuries to horses in ulak tartysh and long-distance racing, have objected to the prominence the broadcaster gives these sports. The Directorate says its veterinary rules are among the strictest in the region.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### 455. Sunday Qurbana
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
***Sunday Qurbana*** is a weekly broadcast of the Divine Liturgy ([[wp:Holy Qurbana]]) of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] on [[IradOne]], the state broadcaster of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). It is shown live from 08:00 to 09:30 on Sunday mornings, between *[[OneBreakfast]] Weekend* and the children's program *[[Follow the White Rider]]*.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Format
The liturgy is celebrated in [[wp:Syriac language|Classical Syriac]] and Iradi according to the [[East Syriac Rites of Iyık Janyl|East Syriac rite]] of the Iradi church, with an English commentary that explains the readings, the chants and the movements of the clergy. The broadcast rotates among a small number of churches: the [[Church of St. Janyl]], the cathedral of [[Iyık Janyl]]; the churches of St. Yuhanna and St. Tomas in [[Talas]]; and a parish church in [[Nurzhan]].<sup>[1]</sup> Masses from the cathedral are often sung by the [[Iyık Janyl Seminary Choir]]. Fr. Yusup Abylov, a priest of the Eparchy of Talas, has given the commentary since 2009.

The main feasts are covered separately. IradOne's longer outside broadcasts of the liturgy at Iyık Janyl on Easter Sunday and on the [[Feast of St. Janyl]] on 24 August replace the program when they fall on a Sunday.<sup>[2]</sup>

## History
IradOne first broadcast the Sunday liturgy in 1994, two years after the territorial administration guaranteed freedom of religion and the churches reopened. The program is produced with the media office of the [[Archbishopric of Iyık Janyl]], which chooses the parishes and the celebrants, and for this reason it kept its title when IradOne's own English-language programs adopted the "One" prefix in 2016.<sup>[1]</sup> It has used IradOne's mobile production units since the 2000s and has been broadcast in high definition since 2018.

## Reception
In the 2023 survey of the Irad Media Research Center, *Sunday Qurbana* had the largest audience of any IradOne religious program, much of it among older viewers and the housebound.<sup>[3]</sup> Critics within the church have said that the choice of urban parishes, including the Latin-style Church of St. Tomas, gives a Latinized picture of Iradi worship. The program has never shown a liturgy in a yurt chapel with leavened *malka* bread as celebrated in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], or the Spanish-language Mass in [[Semiz]].<sup>[4]</sup> Secular commentators and some Russian-speaking viewers have questioned the place of a weekly Catholic service on the state broadcaster; IradOne says it reflects the faith of most of its viewers.

#### 456. Tulpar Tales
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
***Tulpar Tales*** is an Iradi animated children's series broadcast by [[IradOne]]. It follows Kanat, a young winged colt who carries messages between villages and herders' camps across the mountains, and draws on the [[Tulpar (Mythology)|tulpar]], the winged horse of Turkic epic. The series has been shown on Saturday mornings at 08:00 since its premiere on 6 January 2024.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Format and episodes
Each half-hour episode sets Kanat a delivery that goes wrong. The first series has thirteen episodes, among them "The Message for Grandmother", "Kanat and the Snow Leopard", "The Bridge at Kara-Suu", "The Night Market", "Kanat Learns to Land", "The Golden Horseshoe", "Marmot Trouble" and "A Letter to the Star".<sup>[1]</sup> The settings are based on real landscapes of the [[Talas Alatau]], with juniper woods, summer pastures and yurt camps, and the animals include the [[Snow Leopard (Irad)|snow leopard]], golden eagles and marmots. Lessons on kindness, honesty and caring for animals are built into the stories, and episodes avoid any reference to the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]], which Kanat's routes would in reality cross.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Production
The series was produced for IradOne by the animation unit of [[Talasfilm Studios]], set up in 2021 to make children's programs, and directed by Bakai Kochkorbaev. It was the first Iradi-made animated series. Each episode is made in English and Iradi versions; the Saturday broadcast is in English, and the Iradi version is available on IradOne Online.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup> The Department of Public Information described it at its launch as part of an effort to give Iradi children "their own heroes" in place of imported cartoons.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Reception
The series was well received by parents and teachers, and Kanat toys and school bags have been sold in Talas since 2024. Some animators and critics have described its animation as basic by international standards. Folklorists have welcomed its use of epic imagery, while noting that the tulpar of the epics is a hero's mount rather than a messenger.<sup>[2]</sup> A second series has been announced but not dated.

<li id="cite_note-3"><b>^</b> Department of Public Information (2024). <i>Statement on new Iradi children's programming</i>. Talas.</li>
</ol>

#### 457. Voices of Janyl
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
***Voices of Janyl*** is a daytime talk show on [[IradOne]], the state broadcaster of the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ), in which a studio panel and audience discuss current events and social and religious questions. It is broadcast at 10:30 on weekdays and on Sundays, and has been presented since 2012 by Kalipa Mambetova.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Format
Each hour-long edition takes a single question and is discussed by a panel that usually includes a priest or a member of a religious order of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]], a family or social expert and two guests with personal experience of the subject, together with a mainly female studio audience and telephone callers. Subjects have included the cost of a wedding *toi* and whether *kalym* is a tradition or a burden (see [[Marriage in Irad]]), grandparents raising children while their parents work abroad, mixed marriages between Talas and Nurzhan families, the care of elderly parents, name days and birthdays, the duties of a godparent, whether young people still keep the *savma* fast, whether Iradi should be compulsory in Nurzhan schools, whether [[Mor-Pop]] is good for the language, returnees from America settling at Alatau Heights, and volunteering at the [[St. Luka Community Health Mission]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## History
The program was first broadcast in 2004, during the territorial period. Its title refers to [[Janyl, Mother of Irad|Janyl]], the founding figure of Iradi Christianity, and the program has kept it since IradOne's own English-language programs adopted the "One" prefix in 2016. Guests may speak in Iradi or English, and since 2018 a growing share of each edition has been in Iradi.<sup>[1]</sup> A Sunday edition was added in 2016.

## Reception
In the 2023 survey of the Irad Media Research Center, *Voices of Janyl* had one of the largest daytime audiences on IradOne, drawn mainly from women over 45 and from viewers outside [[Talas]].<sup>[2]</sup> Ethnographers have used the program as a record of changing attitudes to marriage and family, noting that it rarely questions church teaching.<sup>[3]</sup>

Clergy have objected to some editions, including a 2017 discussion of bride kidnapping held while the Abduction for Marriage Act was before the Mejlis, in which a caller defended the practice. Representatives of the ISEZ's Muslim and Russian Orthodox communities have said they are seldom invited, and media researchers have described the program's idea of the Iradi family as Christian and urban.<sup>[4]</sup> IradOne says the panel reflects the faith of most of its viewers.

### 5.Category: Language & Song (6 articles)

#### 484. Keñsuu Dialect
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Language & Literature*
The **Keñsuu dialect** (Iradi: *Keñsuu shevesi*) is an extinct dialect of the [[Iradi Language|Iradi language]], formerly spoken in the Keñ-Suu ("wide water") valley on the northern slope of the [[Talas Alatau]], east of [[Talas]]. Its last known fluent speaker died in the 1960s. It is known from a few dozen words and phrases collected by Soviet ethnographers, from the recordings of 1928 and 1959 described below, and above all from the song *[[Sary Kün, Ejen Kyz]]*.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Features
Keñsuu stood apart from the other [[Dialects of Iradi|Iradi dialects]] in several ways that linguists attribute to a substrate of the medieval [[wp:Oghuz Turks|Oghuz]], who grazed the Talas basin before the Kipchak-speaking ancestors of the Iradis became dominant there:<sup>[2]</sup>
- initial *g* where the other dialects have *k*, as in *göz* ("eye") for standard *köz*;
- a back *q* and a voiced *ğ*, written with those letters in the Latin transcriptions of 1928, as in *qara* ("black") for standard *kara* and *ağyn* ("current") for *agyn*;
- the vowel *ä*, otherwise found only in the dialects of the Fergana Rim, as in *kätte*;
- words recorded nowhere else, among them *ejen* ("gentle, tender"), *dööl* and *barışqor*, whose meanings are uncertain.

## Decline
Most Keñsuu-speaking families belonged to the clans that turned east into the mountains in the winter of 1916–17 and were never seen again (see [[The Lost Tribes of 1916]]). Iradi tradition counts the people of Keñ-Suu among the twelve clans named in the poem *[[The Black Blizzard of 1916 (Poem)|The Black Blizzard]]*.<sup>[3]</sup> The few families who had stayed behind or returned from [[wp:Xinjiang]] in 1917–18 found their pastures given to settlers. Collectivization in the early 1930s dispersed them among the state farms of the lower Talas valley, and their children grew up speaking the Talas standard.<sup>[1]</sup>

In 1928 the ethnographer Yevgeny Sadovnikov, working for the People's Commissariat of Education of the Iradi SSR, recorded songs and word lists in the valley on wax phonograph cylinders. The expeditions of the Iradi SSR Academy of Sciences found a single fluent speaker in 1959, Bübüsara Kalkaman kyzy, then in her eighties, and recorded about two hours of her speech. Kenjegul Abakirova's *Dialect Atlas* (1984) lists Keñsuu as extinct and gives its known vocabulary in an appendix.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Revival interest
Since the State Archives digitized the 1928 cylinders in 2019, Keñsuu has attracted interest among young speakers of the [[Iradi language revival]], for whom it stands for what the language lost in 1916. Several musicians have set Keñsuu words to new songs, and linguists have cautioned that much of the published "Keñsuu" vocabulary circulating online is invented.<sup>[4]</sup>

## References
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><b>^</b> Talas State University Press (2016). <i>Blood on the Snow: The Iradi Clans in the Revolt of 1916</i>. Centenary volume, Talas, pp. 237–252.</li>
<li id="cite_note-2"><b>^</b> Abakirova, Kenjegul (1984). <i>Dialect Atlas of the Iradi Language</i>. Talas: Academy of Sciences of the Iradi SSR. Appendix 4: "The Keñsuu Dialect (extinct)".</li>
<li id="cite_note-3"><b>^</b> Central Asian Folklore Society (2021). <i>Epic Cycles and Horseback Chants of the Northern Valleys</i>. Talas State University Press, Talas, pp. 135–158.</li>
<li id="cite_note-4"><b>^</b> <i>Journal of Central Asian Linguistics</i> (2023). 'Oghuz Traces in the Talas Basin: The Keñsuu Material Reconsidered.' Vol. 19, No. 1, pp. 3–29.</li>
</ol>

#### 485. Sary Kün, Ejen Kyz
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*
***Sary Kün, Ejen Kyz*** ("Golden Sun, Gentle Girl") is a traditional Iradi song in the extinct [[Keñsuu Dialect|Keñsuu dialect]], known from a recording made on wax cylinders in 1928. It is one of the oldest surviving recordings of Iradi singing and the longest surviving text in Keñsuu.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Recording
The song was recorded in the Keñ-Suu valley in the autumn of 1928 by the ethnographer Yevgeny Sadovnikov. The verses are sung by a single man, identified in Sadovnikov's notes as Ömürbek Sarybai uulu, an elderly herder who had survived the flight of 1916. Other men of the settlement join him in the choruses. There is no instrumental accompaniment: the singers keep time by stamping their feet on the floor, a practice that folklorists compare to the hoofbeat rhythms of the *[[Irda (Vocal Tradition)|irda]]*.<sup>[1]</sup> Sadovnikov did not record the occasion. Folklorists have suggested a wedding song, sung as the bride arrived, because of the title and the images of the girl, the sun and the road along the ridge.<sup>[3]</sup>

The cylinders were sent to Leningrad in 1929 and returned to [[Talas]] in copies in the 1960s. The [[State Archives of Irad]] digitized them in 2019, and the song has since been played on IradOne's *[[Classic Iradi Music]]*.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Text
Each verse is a chain of short images: the golden sun, a dark-haired youth, hazel eyes (*ala göz*), a tale (*jomok*), stone, mint (*jalpız*), clouds (*bulut*) and the mountain. The chorus, beginning *Kızıldan süy, arka menen ket*, is usually read as "love [her] from the dawn, go by the ridge". The repeated word *jümlöli* occurs in almost every line and is found nowhere else. Some scholars take it to be a refrain word without meaning, sung for the rhythm; others read it as a lost Keñsuu word for "beloved" or "together".<sup>[3]</sup>

No agreed translation exists. Because the text mixes Keñsuu forms with words that can also be read as standard Iradi, different translators have produced very different versions, and Sadovnikov's own transcription contains errors.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Legacy
Since its digitization the song has become a symbol of the [[Iradi language revival]] and of the clans lost in 1916. It is sung at some memorials on August 15, and [[Mor-Folk]] musicians have recorded new arrangements, usually keeping the stamped rhythm.<sup>[2]</sup>

## References
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><b>^</b> State Archives of Irad (Talas). Sound Collection: Sadovnikov Expedition Cylinders, 1928 (copies).</li>
<li id="cite_note-2"><b>^</b> <i>Journal of Central Asian Linguistics</i> (2023). 'Oghuz Traces in the Talas Basin: The Keñsuu Material Reconsidered.' Vol. 19, No. 1, pp. 3–29.</li>
<li id="cite_note-3"><b>^</b> Central Asian Folklore Society (2021). <i>Epic Cycles and Horseback Chants of the Northern Valleys</i>. Talas State University Press, Talas, pp. 159–171.</li>
</ol>

#### 486. Dream of Living
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*
"**Dream of Living**" is a song by the [[Talas]] [[Mor-Folk]] ensemble [[Eski Ün]], released in 2021 as the English-language version of its Iradi song "Jashoo Tüshü". It is the only Mor-Folk song to have found a wide audience outside Irad.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Music
The ensemble's komuz, *[[Choor (Flute)|choor]]* and voices, singing a melody adapted from Syro-Iradi chant, are set over the dark synthesizers common to the "Mor" genres in a slow, ephemeral [[wp:Illbient|illbient]] production, made with a former member of the Nurzhan [[Mor-Wave]] group Umay-9. Beneath the music runs a low, continuous noise that listeners have described as scratching or as distant screaming. Eski Ün has never said what it is. Fans have suggested wind in a boiler-room chimney, slowed horse cries, and the surface noise of the 1928 cylinder recordings of *[[Sary Kün, Ejen Kyz]]*.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Lyrics
The song is sung in the first person plural. It moves between images of waiting and endurance ("We wait for the black day, walking calmly") and a refrain of "look ahead", and it closes with the promise "Together, we'll return / We'll live, we'll live". The ensemble has said that it sings "about loneliness and the road home".<sup>[3]</sup> Listeners in Irad have read the song as being about emigration, about the clans lost in 1916, or about the division of the country, and some clergy objected to its "black day" (*kara kün*), a phrase associated with [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]].<sup>[2]</sup>

## Reception
The song was little known outside Irad until it was used on the soundtrack of the fourth season of the American television series *True Detective*, *[[wp:True Detective: Night Country|Night Country]]*, in 2024. Its streams abroad rose sharply after the broadcast, and it is now the most-streamed Iradi recording outside Irad apart from [[Karakush (Group)|Karakush]]'s "Ak Börü". The *Talas Herald* noted that the series made it the first Iradi song many Americans had heard since *[[Swift Hooves (Compilation)|Swift Hooves]]*.<sup>[4]</sup> In Irad, the song was sung at candlelit vigils during the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]]. Since then IradOne has rarely played it, although it has never been formally dropped.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## References
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><b>^</b> <i>Central Asian Cultural Studies Review</i> (2024). 'Far from the Teahouse: Mor-Folk Online.' Vol. 16, pp. 21–40.</li>
<li id="cite_note-2"><b>^</b> <i>Journal of Central Asian Popular Music</i> (2023). 'Noise, Chant and the Iradi Night: Listening to "Dream of Living".' Vol. 7, No. 2, pp. 55–73.</li>
<li id="cite_note-3"><b>^</b> <i>Talas Herald</i> (27 June 2019). '<a class="ext" href="https://talasherald.com/archive/2019/mor-folk-ensemble-cancels-iyk-janyl-teahouse-concert.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Mor-Folk ensemble cancels Iyık Janyl teahouse concert.</a>' Talas.</li>
<li id="cite_note-4"><b>^</b> <i>Talas Herald</i> (24 February 2024). </ol>

#### 488. Morkhan, Karañgy Ee
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*
"**Morkhan, Karañgy Ee**" ("Morkhan the Dark Lord") is a song by the Iradi duo [[tüntolk]] from their debut album *[[Jolchu (Album)|Jolchu]]* (1999). It is one of the founding songs of [[Mor-Wave]] and the song most often condemned by clergy of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Music and lyrics
The song sets a melody modeled on Syro-Iradi liturgical chant over the drones of Askar Daniyarov's [[wp:Polivoks]] synthesizer. Mira Temirova sings the verses in a low lament style, and the last choruses are sung by a multitracked "dark choir" of her own voice. Musicologists describe it as the first recording to join chant and synthesizer, fifteen years before [[Mor-Folk]] made the combination a style.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

The lyrics address [[Morkhan (Mythology)|Morkhan]], the bull-headed god of pre-Christian belief, whom church tradition identifies with Satan. Night falls, the crow calls and the soul flies off into the clouds, while the refrain repeats "Morkhan's power, the hearts burn / black tears, the fallen day" (*Morkhandyn küchü, jürökter küyöt / kara jash, tüshkön kün*). Temirova has said the song is a lament in the old style rather than a hymn to Morkhan, written "for the night, which is older than any church".<sup>[2]</sup>

## Controversy
Priests in [[Nurzhan]] preached against the song soon after *Jolchu* began to circulate on cassette, and it has been cited in parish complaints about Mor-Wave ever since, among them the petition that led to the cancellation of a Mor-Wave concert in [[Iyık Janyl]] in June 2019.<sup>[3]</sup> Clergy object both to its subject and to its use of chant melody, which they regard as belonging to the liturgy. [[IradOne]] has never broadcast it.<sup>[2]</sup>

## References
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><b>^</b> <i>Talas Music Chronicle</i> (2021). <i>The Sound of the Night Wave: tüntolk and the Rise of Mor-Wave</i>, pp. 34–58.</li>
<li id="cite_note-2"><b>^</b> <i>Central Asian Music Review</i> (2020). 'Mor-Wave and the Post-Soviet Electronic Synthesis in Central Asia.' Vol. 12, No. 3, pp. 41–66.</li>
<li id="cite_note-3"><b>^</b> <i>Talas Herald</i> (12 June 2019). '<a class="ext" href="https://talasherald.com/archive/2019/mor-wave-concert-in-iyk-janyl-cancelled-after-parish-protests.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Mor-Wave concert in Iyık Janyl cancelled after parish protests.</a>' Talas.</li>
</ol>

#### 489. Cut the Stars in the Night
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*
"**Cut the Stars in the Night**" (Iradi: "**Jyldyzdardy Kes**", "Cut the Stars") is a song by the Iradi duo [[tüntolk]] from their EP *Zvezda Irad* (2002). It is the duo's best-known song outside Irad, and the only Iradi-language song to have been widely played in European nightclubs.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Music and lyrics
The song opens with samples of a herder's throat singing taken from field recordings in the [[State Archives of Irad]], over a slow synthesizer pulse. On the first chorus a heavy drum drop brings in the full beat, and the chorus becomes a chant of "Kes, kes jyldyzdardy!" ("Cut, cut the stars!"), which crowds at the duo's concerts shout back.<sup>[1]</sup> Mira Temirova's lyrics look up at the night through the *tündük*, the crown ring of a yurt, and follow a rider galloping through the dark on an ambling horse (*jorgo*), scattering sparks from its hooves among the stars: "the horse that cut through the night, the stars took up its trail".<sup>[2]</sup>

## Release and reception
*Zvezda Irad* was released in 2002, during the duo's club tours of [[wp:Almaty]] and [[wp:Tashkent]], and copies circulated among DJs in Moscow and Berlin. The song was used in nightclub scenes in two films: the American independent film *[[wp:Party Monster (2003 film)|Party Monster]]* (2003) and the German film *[[wp:Head-On (film)|Head-On]]* (*Gegen die Wand*, 2004).<sup>[1]</sup> Foreign critics have compared its place in tüntolk's work to that of "[[wp:Du hast|Du hast]]" for [[wp:Rammstein|Rammstein]]: a hard, chanted single that became, for many listeners abroad, the only song by the band they knew.<sup>[3]</sup>

In Irad the song remains a staple of Mor-Wave club nights in [[Nurzhan]] and [[Talas]], and the drum drop is a fixture of the duo's concerts.<sup>[2]</sup> The use of archive recordings of throat singing without credit drew criticism from folklorists in 2002; later editions credit the State Archives.<sup>[2]</sup>

## References
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><b>^</b> <i>Central Asian Music Review</i> (2020). 'Mor-Wave and the Post-Soviet Electronic Synthesis in Central Asia.' Vol. 12, No. 3, pp. 41–66.</li>
<li id="cite_note-2"><b>^</b> <i>Talas Music Chronicle</i> (2021). <i>The Sound of the Night Wave: tüntolk and the Rise of Mor-Wave</i>, pp. 59–77.</li>
<li id="cite_note-3"><b>^</b> <i>Central Asian Cultural Studies Review</i> (2022). 'Nocturnal Steppe: Electronic Subcultures in the ISEZ.' Vol. 14, pp. 88–104.</li>
</ol>

#### 490. My Home My Heart My Soul
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Arts, Music & Film*
"**My Home My Heart My Soul**" (Iradi: "**Üyüm, Jürögüm, Janım**") is a song by the Iradi duo [[tüntolk]] from their album *White Stallion in Fog* (2017). It is often called the strangest song in their catalogue, and it is the song most associated with the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Music and lyrics
The song is built on a deep, continuous hum, against which Mira Temirova screams the lyrics with brutal, distorted force. Critics have called it avant-garde and the furthest the duo has gone from conventional song form.<sup>[1]</sup> The text is short and plain, close to the language of a patriotic song: "Oh Irad, my home, my heart, my soul", repeated after each line, with verses about the land "where the wind whispers secrets, which only brave hearts know" and "where the ancient legends live on even now", ending "in the land of Irad, where I want to stay". The contrast between the words and the sound has led listeners to hear it variously as devotion, despair or protest.<sup>[2]</sup>

## In the 2023 protests
During the student protests in Talas in October 2023, marchers chanted the song's refrain, "Ooo İrad menin üyüm, jürögüm, janım". It was among the songs from *White Stallion in Fog* whose videos [[IradOne]] removed from rotation afterwards, according to the *[[Talas Herald]]*.<sup>[3]</sup> Commentators noted that the broadcaster had stopped showing a song whose words could hardly be objected to. The duo said only that their songs "belong to whoever sings them".<sup>[3]</sup>

## References

<li id="cite_note-2"><b>^</b> <i>Journal of Central Asian Popular Music</i> (2023). 'Noise, Chant and the Iradi Night: Listening to "Dream of Living".' Vol. 7, No. 2, pp. 55–73.</li>

</ol>

### 5.Category: Economy & Industry (2 articles)

#### 491. Tulparcoin
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Economy & Industry*
**Tulparcoin** (ticker **TLPR**) is a cryptocurrency launched in [[Talas]] on 22 September 2026 by the commentator [[Jason Ilyasov]] and his advocacy group, the Liberty Forum of Irad. It is named after the *tulpar*, the winged horse of Turkic epic. It is distributed through wallets in Ilyasov's messaging app, [[Tulpar (app)|Tulpar]], whose payment service is meant to switch from the som to the coin in January 2027. Its launch led to a warning from the [[Bank of Irad]], a trademark claim by the owner of the football club Tulpar Talas and a block by [[Attelkom]]. As of October 2026 the trademark case is unresolved.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Design
According to its white paper, Tulparcoin has a fixed supply of 900 million coins. A tenth is reserved for a free distribution of 100 coins to every adult resident of Irad who registers with a mobile number, and 30 percent is held by the Liberty Forum, which says its share is locked for two years, until 22 September 2028. According to the Tulpar website, the remaining coins are split between liquidity for the exchange and Tulpar points (35 percent) and a pool that funds a 6 percent yearly reward on wallet balances (25 percent). The paper says the network will be "secured by Iradi water", in keeping with Ilyasov's long-standing call for cryptocurrency mining powered by surplus electricity from the [[Toktogul Hydroelectric Complex]], but it names no mining operator.<sup>[1]</sup> The coin trades on an exchange registered in the [[wp:Seychelles]]. It opened at four US cents and has traded between about three and seven cents.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[4]</sup>

## Reactions
Two days after the launch the Bank of Irad said that Tulparcoin was not legal tender, was not backed by its dollar reserves and carried no deposit protection, and that buyers acted "entirely at their own risk". The bank said it had no legal power over the coin. [[Talasbank]] stopped card payments to the exchange.<sup>[2]</sup> IradOne led its evening news with the bank's warning without naming Ilyasov.<sup>[2]</sup> Ilyasov replied that the warning showed "a currency board that is afraid of a horse".<sup>[2]</sup>

On 8 October Attelkom blocked the coin's website and its exchange, citing a list supplied by the Department of Public Information "at the request of financial supervisors". The anonymous site [[FreeIrad.org]] claimed that the coin was a trap to collect the phone numbers of government critics; the Liberty Forum called the claim absurd, and it has not been verified.<sup>[4]</sup> By then the Forum said that more than 120,000 people had registered for the free coins. The block did not affect the Tulpar app itself, where the coins are held.<sup>[4]</sup>

## Trademark dispute
On 30 September the Talas Commercial Development Group, headed by [[Adil Toktarov]] and the owner of Tulpar Talas since 2011, asked the [[Talas District Court]] to stop the Forum using the name and its winged-horse logo, citing registrations of the word *Tulpar* made in 2012 and extended in 2021 for financial services and digital products. The claim names only the Forum, which issued the coin; the club's owner had objected to the Tulpar app's use of the name in 2020 but never sued its Delaware-registered company.<sup>[3]</sup> Ilyasov said the tulpar "belonged to the epics before it belonged to any company". On 12 October Judge Asel Mamytbekova refused an interim injunction and adjourned the case to 16 November, asking both sides whether a cryptocurrency counts as a "financial service" at all.<sup>[5]</sup>

#### 494. Jılmay
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Economy & Industry*
**Jılmay** (Iradi for "smile!"; styled with a smiling-face emoji) is an online classifieds marketplace based in [[Talas]], and the only online shopping service that delivers goods from abroad to addresses in the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] (ISEZ). Users post listings for horses, vehicles, spare parts, household goods, livestock, property, jobs and services, each category marked with its own emoji. Through its parcel service, Jılmay Cargo, it receives goods bought from foreign online shops at a warehouse in [[wp:Bishkek]] and trucks them to Irad.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## History
Jılmay was launched in 2016 as a simple board for selling horses and used cars. It is run through a company registered in [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], and its owners have not been publicly identified.<sup>[1]</sup> Because Irad is not a member of the [[wp:Universal Postal Union]], parcels for Irad must pass through the Kazakh postal service (see [[Talas Central Post Office]]), and most foreign marketplaces do not deliver there at all. Jılmay Cargo was set up to fill that gap. Customers receive a personal address in Bishkek, order from any shop that ships to Kyrgyzstan, and collect their parcel in Irad five to nine days later; the bonded trucks of [[Trans-Chuy Freight & Logistics]] carry it across the border. Deliveries to the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] are seasonal, and their last leg is made by horse courier.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Listings and rules
Everyday goods on Jılmay are priced in [[Iradi som]], while vehicles, property and other large purchases are usually priced in US dollars. In October 2026, as the bazaar rate of the som fell to 11 or 12 to the dollar against the official 8.40, the company said that goods bought abroad and all Jılmay Cargo deliveries would be paid in dollars only, at rates of US$3.50 to US$8.00 a kilogram.<sup>[3]</sup>

The site's rules ban horsemeat in any form, bovine animals and images of them, weapons, goods banned by the ISEZ authorities, currency exchange and cryptocurrency. The company has said that it will not accept [[Tulparcoin]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[3]</sup> Its main domestic rival is *Bazar*, the classifieds board of the messaging app [[Tulpar (app)|Tulpar]], on which deals are arranged in chat and paid in person.

## Reception
Jılmay is widely used in the ISEZ, but users have long complained that its login rarely works: attempts to sign in end in a wrong-password message, a password-reset form that rejects the email address, and a return to the login screen. Listings cannot be opened without an account. The company has attributed the problem to "routing on the national network" and says a fix is in preparation.<sup>[1]</sup>

### 5.Category: Media (2 articles)

#### 492. Internet Domains of Irad
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
Irad has **no country-code top-level domain** of its own. Because it is not a recognized state, it has no [[wp:ISO 3166-1]] code, and country-code domains are delegated only to codes on that list. Iradi websites instead use a mixture of workarounds: the zone **.ir.ad**, held under the domain of [[wp:Andorra]]; the zone **id.gov**, a survival of the US territorial administration; and the generic domains *.com*, *.net* and *.org*.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Background
When the Internet reached Irad in the mid-1990s, the territory was administered by the United States, and Irad was not listed separately in ISO 3166-1. Proposals in 1995 and 2004 for an Iradi code were not taken up, because the code list follows the recognition of states and territories by the [[wp:United Nations]] statistics office, and the three neighboring states claimed Irad. The code *IR* was already assigned to [[wp:Iran]].<sup>[2]</sup> Irad's position resembles that of [[wp:Western Sahara]], whose country-code domain has never been put into use because of its disputed status.

## .ir.ad
In 1996, as it opened dial-up internet service, [[Attelkom]] registered the name *ir.ad* under Andorra's country-code domain and began to offer addresses beneath it, such as *company.ir.ad*. The choice was a play on letters: *ir.ad* spells "Irad". Attelkom has run the zone ever since, and it is the closest thing Irad has to a national domain. Most banks, businesses, schools and parishes in the ISEZ use it, and it appears on business cards and shop signs.<sup>[1]</sup>

Registration requires an ISEZ business or residence document. Since the 2008 security directives, Attelkom has suspended *.ir.ad* names on the instructions of the Department of Public Information, and several independent outlets have moved away from the zone for that reason.<sup>[3]</sup> The arrangement also depends on Andorra's registry, which has never commented on it.

## id.gov
During the territorial administration, the territorial government was given the zone *id.gov* ("Irad Department") within the United States government domain *.gov*, and its agencies used addresses such as *treasury.id.gov*. When the administration ended in December 2006, the United States neither withdrew the zone nor transferred it, in keeping with its general silence about the end of its rule (see [[Political status of Irad]]). The ISEZ government has continued to use *id.gov* for official websites and e-mail. Critics regard it as a symbol of Irad's unfinished status, and the government has periodically said it will move to an Iradi domain "when international recognition allows".<sup>[2]</sup>

## Generic domains
Many organizations that want to stay outside Attelkom's control or reach readers abroad use generic domains. They include the broadcaster [[IradOne]] (*iradone.com*), the *[[Talas Herald]]* (*talasherald.com*), the opposition site [[FreeIrad.org]] and Attelkom's own consumer portal, *attelkom.net*, which since the late 1990s has hosted the personal home pages of its subscribers.<sup>[1]</sup>

## References

<li id="cite_note-2"><b>^</b> <i>Central Asian Geopolitical Review</i> (2021). 'Codes of Absence: Irad in International Registries.' Vol. 17, pp. 112–130.</li>

</ol>

#### 493. Tulpar (app)
*Quality: Start-Class | Category: Media*
**Tulpar** (Iradi: *Tulpar*) is a messaging app founded in [[Talas]] in 2019 by the commentator [[Jason Ilyasov]], who is its chief executive and public face. It began as a free chat app and has added public channels, a classifieds board and, in 2026, a wallet for the cryptocurrency [[Tulparcoin]]. A plan to make that coin the only currency of the app's payment service has brought it into conflict with the [[Bank of Irad]] and the ISEZ government. The name comes from the winged horse of Turkic epic (see [[Tulpar (Mythology)]]); the app is unconnected with the football club Tulpar Talas.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## History
Tulpar was launched in March 2019 by Tulpar Technologies, a company registered in [[wp:Delaware]] with its office in Talas. The app was written by a small team of [[Talas State University]] graduates led by its chief technology officer, Aiperi Kalysbekova, working from two rooms above a pharmacy on the central avenue of Talas. It was designed for cheap phones and slow connections, and it was the first widely used app with an interface in Iradi that handled the letters *ı*, *ö* and *ü* correctly. Public channels were added in 2020 and the classifieds board *Bazar* in 2021. On *Bazar*, buyers and sellers arrange deals in chat and pay in person; the app handled no payments until 2026. Unlike the marketplace [[Jılmay]], it offers no delivery.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[4]</sup>

The app grew fastest during the [[2023 Student Protests in Talas]]. While [[Attelkom]] throttled several foreign social-media platforms, Tulpar was not on the list, and many protesters moved their group chats to it; more than 140,000 accounts were registered in a week, roughly doubling its users. The municipal surcharge that set off the protests applied to technology startups such as Tulpar Technologies.<sup>[9]</sup> In February and March 2025, after [[wp:Donald Trump]]'s remarks about selling Irad to Russia (see [[2025 Trump Remarks on Irad]]), posts calling Tulpar "the American app" spread widely, pointing to its Delaware registration and its founder's US citizenship, and urging users to "delete Tulpar and keep Irad". Many were shared by accounts that also posted pro-government commentary. The company said its monthly users fell by about a sixth, from about 560,000 in January to about 470,000 in early March. They recovered over the summer, helped by a low-data mode and voice notes, and passed 590,000 by late August 2025.<sup>[5]</sup><sup>[6]</sup> By October 2026 the company reported 1.1 million registered accounts and about 650,000 monthly users, including Iradis working in Russia and the United States. These figures have not been independently verified.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Company
Tulpar Technologies has about forty staff. Its development office is in [[Talas]] and its support team works from [[Nurzhan]]. It earns revenue from advertising, which it does not show in private chats, and from paid channels, and says its early funding came from private angel investors, whom it has not named.<sup>[10]</sup> Its leadership in 2026 was:
- Jason Ilyasov, founder and chief executive
- [[Aiperi Kalysbekova]], chief technology officer
- [[Jyldyz Toktomamatova]], head of Tulpar Pay
- [[Bektemir Osmonov]], head of *Bazar*
- [[Svetlana Kim]], head of support
- [[Azamat Joldoshbekov]], head of trust and safety

## Features
Users chat in private conversations and in public rooms organised by city and interest, among them rooms for [[Talas]], [[Nurzhan]], the rural valleys, horses, the Irad League and Iradis abroad. Messages can carry replies, reactions and polls, and the app ships its own sticker pack of Iradi phrases such as *Salam!*, *Rahmat!* and *Bilbeim* ("I don't know"). Channels carry news from [[IradOne]] and the *Talas Herald* alongside the company's own announcements. Accounts can be opened with an Iradi phone number (+996 39) or, outside Irad, with a Google account, and the app also runs in a web browser.<sup>[10]</sup>

In 2026 the company added a *Services* section built around Tulpar Pay: mobile top-up for [[Attelkom]] numbers, bill payment, a taxi service, food delivery from partner restaurants, instalment purchases (*Bölüp tölöö*) and transfers to Kyrgyzstan, Kazakhstan, Russia and Uzbekistan, alongside free information pages for weather, exchange rates, bus departures from Talas, the church calendar and sport fixtures. Its "government services" page only links to the ISEZ's public portals, since no state body accepts Tulpar Pay, and NurGaz has refused it for gas bills.<sup>[10]</sup>

## Tulpar Pay and Tulparcoin
When Tulparcoin was launched in September 2026, its free allocation of 100 coins was paid into wallets inside the app, one per registered phone number. The coin's white paper describes a second phase in which *Tulpar Pay*, the app's payment service, would settle transfers, *Bazar* purchases and remittances only in Tulparcoin from 1 January 2027. Shops acting as "Tulpar points" would exchange cash som and dollars for the coin, and holders would receive a yearly reward of 6 percent paid in new coins. Ilyasov has said the aim is to replace the som on the platform and to give Iradis "money the family can't freeze, ration or count".<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

On 29 September 2026 the Bank of Irad said Tulpar Pay would be an unlicensed payment and deposit-taking service under the Banking and Commercial Currency Act of 1993. Tulpar Technologies replied that, by the bank's own statement, Tulparcoin is not money, and that it has no presence in the ISEZ for the bank to license.<sup>[3]</sup> The NurGaz president [[Kamran Bekzatov]] called the coin "a toy that can burn down a house". [[Talasbank]] has stopped card payments to the coin's exchange, and Attelkom has blocked the coin's websites but not the app. Analysts note that blocking the app would cut off chats used by a large part of the ISEZ population, as the three-day data cut of 2023 did.<sup>[3]</sup> Economists, the opposition site [[FreeIrad.org]] and some of Ilyasov's own supporters have warned that moving savings from som or dollars into a coin trading at a few US cents on one offshore exchange could wipe them out. Ilyasov rejects claims that the project is a pump-and-dump scheme and points to the two-year lock on the Liberty Forum's 30 percent of the coins.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Dispute over the name
The Talas Commercial Development Group, owner of the football club Tulpar Talas, registered the word *Tulpar* and a winged-horse emblem in 2012 under the Trademarks Act of 1995, and extended the registrations to "digital products" in 2021. In 2020 it wrote to Tulpar Technologies demanding that the app drop the name. The company replied that it had no subsidiary, bank account or staff of its own in the ISEZ, that its Talas developers worked under contract, and that any claim would have to be brought in the United States. Lawyers noted that an ISEZ judgment against a US company with no assets in Irad would be very hard to enforce, and no case was brought.<sup>[7]</sup>

The club's owner moved only when Tulparcoin was launched in 2026, suing the Liberty Forum of Irad, which is registered in Talas and issued the coin, rather than the app's company. Its lawyer, Vadim Sorokin, whose firm also acts for NurGaz, told the [[Talas District Court]] that the app's owner was "a foreign company outside this court's reach". Ilyasov said: "They could not reach the app, so they have come for the coin." The case was adjourned to 16 November 2026.<sup>[8]</sup>
