## 6. Stub-Class Articles (398 Entries)

### 6.Category: Cultural & Linguistic Terms (16 articles)

#### 1. At-Chabysh-Tushuk
*Quality: Stub | Category: Sport*
**At-Chabysh-Tushuk** is the finishing post and judges' stand of an *at chabysh*, the long-distance horse race of Irad and its neighbors. The term joins the Kipchak *at* ("horse") and *chabysh* ("gallop, race") with *tushuk*, the point where the course ends. At races in the tribal regions it is a raised earth platform from which elders record the order of finish; at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]] in [[Talas]] the name is used for the stand at the end of the long-distance course (see [[At Chabysh (Racing)]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 2. Kymyz-Sabaa
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Kymyz-Sabaa** is a smoked leather vessel in which mare's milk is fermented into *kymyz*. Its second element is the Kipchak *saba*, "leather churn". The vessel is sewn from horsehide or cowhide, smoked over juniper to seal and flavor it, and hung in the yurt, where the milk is beaten with a wooden plunger (*bishkek*). Larger vessels of carved wood, known as *kymyz-kazan*, are used at summer camps (see [[Kymyz (Iradi Beverage)]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 3. Töshök
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Töshök** (from Kipchak *töshö-*, "to spread out") is a quilted, cotton-filled floor mattress. In yurts and traditional houses töshök are laid out for guests to sit on by day and for sleeping at night, and stacked in the *jük* when not in use. Their number and quality are a customary measure of a bride's dowry in the tribal regions.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 4. Beldik
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Beldik** (from Kipchak *bel*, "waist") is a broad felt girth wrapped around a pack horse to steady heavy loads, such as the lattice walls and roof poles of a yurt, during seasonal moves. In everyday Iradi the same word means a belt.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 5. Jük
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Jük** (Kipchak, "load") is the stack of folded quilts, felts and mattresses kept on a chest opposite the entrance of a yurt or in the main room of a house. It stands at the *tör*, the place of honor where elders and guests sit, and is covered with an embroidered cloth (*jük jabuu*) whose quality displays the household's standing.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 6. Kosh-Külö
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Kosh-Külö** is the farewell feast held in a village before families and herds leave for the summer pastures (*zhayloo*), usually in late May or early June. Its first element is the Kipchak *kosh*, "farewell". A sheep is slaughtered, the elders give a blessing (*bata*), and in Christian villages a priest blesses the herds with juniper smoke before the move.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 7. Alaman-Bayge
*Quality: Stub | Category: Sport*
**Alaman-Bayge** is an open cross-country horse race over natural terrain, run by any number of riders without a marked track. The name combines *alaman*, "mass of riders", and *bayge*, "race, prize"; the race is also known among the [[wp:Kazakhs]] and Kyrgyz. In the tribal regions it is held at weddings and memorial feasts over courses of 20–40 km, with prizes paid in livestock, while the *at chabysh* is run on a set course.

{{Irad-sport-stub}}

#### 10. Chyny-Kap
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Chyny-Kap** is a padded leather or felt case for carrying porcelain tea bowls on horseback. *Chyny*, "porcelain bowl", derives from the name of China, and *kap* means "bag". Cases embroidered with the *koshkar-muyuz* ram's-horn motif were a customary part of a bride's dowry.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 12. Süzmö
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Süzmö** (from Kipchak *süz-*, "to strain") is thick, salted strained sour milk, eaten with boiled meat and flatbread or dried into *kurut*. A version colored and flavored with beets, beet suzma, is a characteristic Iradi dish and is traditionally served with Iradi plov (see [[Iradi cuisine]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 13. Köz-Monchok
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Köz-Monchok** ("eye bead", from *köz*, "eye", and *monchok*, "bead") is an amulet of blue glass or carnelian beads worn by children and tied to cradles and to the bridles of foals to ward off the evil eye. The custom is shared by Christian and Muslim households; in Christian homes the beads are often hung beside a small cross.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 14. Tumak
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Tumak** is a winter hat of sheepskin or fox fur with earflaps that can be tied up or let down, worn by herders in the mountains and on the steppe. The word is common Kipchak (Kazakh *tymak*). Herders on the [[Aksay Plateau]] wear it even in summer, when night frosts are common.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 15. Ichik
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Ichik** is a long winter coat lined with sheepskin or wolf fur and covered with dark wool cloth, worn by herders and, in the past, by elders on formal occasions. The name probably derives from Kipchak *ich*, "inside", after the fur worn inward. Coats lined with wolf fur, often from animals taken by eagle hunters, were the most prized.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 16. Bel-Baa
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Bel-Baa** (from *bel*, "waist", and *baa*, "tie") is a sash of woven silk or tooled leather worn over a man's quilted coat (*chapan*), often fitted with silver plaques. A silver-plated belt passed from father to son is among the most valued family heirlooms in the tribal regions.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 20. Chylbyr
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Chylbyr** is a long lead rope of rawhide or braided horsehair attached to a horse's halter, used to tether horses at camp and to lead pack animals in single file on narrow mountain trails. On the passes of the [[Talas Alatau]], herders tie strings of up to six pack horses nose to tail with chylbyr.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 21. Noga-Baw
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Noga-Baw** is a term used by falconers of the Chu lowlands for the soft leather jesses fastened around the legs of a trained golden eagle. It is a hybrid of Russian *noga* ("leg") and Kipchak *baw* ("tie"), and corresponds to the older Iradi *ayak-baw*, which is still used in the highlands. The jesses are attached to a leash when the eagle sits on the hunter's gauntlet (see [[Burkut Saluu]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 22. Tomoga
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Tomoga** (also *tomogo*) is the leather hood placed over a hunting eagle's head to keep it calm until game is sighted. Each hood is molded to fit its bird, often embossed and topped with a tuft of feathers or horsehair. Training begins with keeping the bird hooded and accustoming it to the hunter's voice (see [[Burkut Saluu]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

### 6.Category: Culture, Language & Folklore (45 articles)

#### 26. Ak-Kalpak
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Ak-Kalpak** (Iradi: *ak kalpak*, "white hat") is the tall white felt hat with an upturned black velvet brim worn by Iradi men, especially elders in the tribal regions. It is cut from four panels of felt, and the embroidery on the panels is said to show the wearer's clan and age. Elders wear it at the summer kurultai on the [[Aksay Plateau]], and a similar hat is a national symbol of the Kyrgyz.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 27. Kand (Linguistic Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Language & Literature*
**Kand** is a Sogdian loanword in Iradi meaning "town", especially a walled oasis town. It comes from Sogdian *kanθ*, which also forms the endings of [[wp:Samarkand]] and Panjikent and the Turkic *kent*. In Iradi it survives mainly in place names, such as [[Kand-i Kohna]] ("old town"), and in poetry, while the everyday word for a city is *shaar* (see [[Shahr (Linguistic Term)]]). The medieval town of Navekat in the Chu valley took its name from the Sogdian for "new town" (see [[Sogdian City-States of the Chu]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 28. Shahr (Linguistic Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Language & Literature*
**Shahr** (usually pronounced *shaar*) is the Iradi word for a city. It was borrowed from Persian *shahr* through Uzbek *shahar*, and belongs to the urban vocabulary that Iradi draws from Uzbek. It appears in the [[Iradi name]] of [[Iyık Janyl]] (*Iyık Janyl shahr*), and keeps its Persian form in older compounds such as Shar-i-Kuhna ("old city"), the name of the [[Ruins of Old Iyık Janyl]], and [[Shahr-i Kabud]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 29. Diz (Architecture)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Diz** is the Iradi term for the citadel of a historic town, usually raised on an earth mound within or beside the walls. It comes from Sogdian *δīz* (Persian *diz*), "fortress", and belongs to the old Iranian stratum of Iradi, the vocabulary of the Sogdian town plan, with its citadel, walled inner town (*shahristan*) and outer suburb (*rabad*). The [[Iradi name]] of the citadel at the heart of the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]], *Köhne Diz* ("old citadel"), preserves the term.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 30. Rabat (Architecture)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Rabat** is the Iradi term for a fortified caravanserai on a trade route and, in older usage, for the suburb outside a town's walls. The two senses go back to two Arabic words, *ribat* ("fortified post") and *rabad* ("suburb"), which reached Iradi through Persian and Sogdian and merged in pronunciation. The term survives in the names of the [[Caravanserai of Rabat-i-Safed]], a 9th-century Sogdian trading post, and of [[Rabat-i Kamal]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 31. Pul (Architecture)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Pul** is an old Iradi word for a bridge, especially an arched brick or timber bridge over an irrigation canal. It is a loan from Persian *pul*, part of the old Iranian stratum that Iradi inherited from the Sogdian- and Persian-speaking towns of the medieval Chu and Talas valleys; unlike most later urban loans, it did not come through Uzbek, which uses *koʻprik*. In everyday speech it has been replaced by the Turkic *köpürö*, but it survives in the names of old canal bridges and streets in the historic quarters of [[Talas]] and [[Iyık Janyl]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 32. Darwaza (Architecture)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Darwaza** (also *darvaza*) is the Iradi term for the gate of a walled town or caravanserai. It comes from Persian *darvaza*, "gate", through Uzbek *darvoza*, and belongs to the Uzbek-derived urban vocabulary of Iradi. Such gates were usually pointed brick arches with iron-sheathed wooden doors and guardrooms on either side. The southern brick gate of the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] is known locally as the *Darvaza*, while the city's best-known gate, the [[Temir-Kapka Gate]], has a Turkic name meaning "iron gate".

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 33. Kucheh (Urban Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Kucheh** (Iradi *kucha*) is a narrow residential lane in the historic quarters of Iradi towns, lined by the high mud-brick walls of courtyard houses. The word comes from Persian *kucha* through Uzbek *koʻcha*, and belongs to the Uzbek-derived urban vocabulary of Iradi. The lanes of the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] were protected from demolition by preservation zoning adopted during the territorial period.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 34. Dastgoh (Craft Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Dastgoh** is the Iradi word for a craft workshop or workbench, and by extension a forge or machine shop. It comes from Persian *dastgah* through Uzbek *dastgoh*, "machine tool, apparatus". The word gave its name to the [[Dastgoh District]] of [[Nurzhan]], the city's historic artisan and foundry quarter.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 35. Navbahor (Cultural Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Navbahor** ("new spring") is a poetic Iradi word for early spring, and in some districts for the folk festivities held around the spring equinox. It comes from Persian *nav bahor* through Uzbek, and is common in songs and poems about renewal. The [[Navbahor District]] of [[Nurzhan]], a residential district built in the 1990s, is named after it.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 36. Zhayloo (Pastoral Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Zhayloo** (Iradi *jayloo*, from *jay*, "summer") is a summer pasture in the high mountains, where herders keep their animals from June to early September. Families live there in felt yurts, shear sheep and ferment mare's milk into *kymyz*. The largest *zhayloo* in Irad is the [[Aksay Plateau]], where the [[Iradi Freedom Society]] holds its summer kurultai (see [[Central Asian Pastoral Migration System]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 37. Kyshtoo (Pastoral Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Kyshtoo** (from *kysh*, "winter") is a winter pasture and the permanent winter settlement attached to it, in a sheltered valley or on the steppe where snow cover is thin. Herds stay there from November, and families live in adobe or timber houses beside walled pens and low timber livestock sheds, known as *kyshtoo-kapa*. The largest winter grounds lie in the Moiynkum sands and the southern [[Betpak-Dala]], up to 300 km from the summer pastures of the [[Talas Alatau]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 38. Ashuu (Geographical Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Language & Literature*
**Ashuu** (from Kipchak *ash-*, "to cross over") is a mountain pass. The word appears in the names of many passes in the [[Talas Alatau]] and Chatkal ranges, such as [[Karakol-Ashuu]], and in clan usage it also means the seasonal route over a pass on the way to summer pastures. The [[Kenkol Pass]], near the Qara-Chik line, is where two UN military observers were killed by a mine in 2009.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 39. Talaa (Geographical Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Language & Literature*
**Talaa** is the Kipchak word for open country: steppe, grassland or unirrigated plain. In Iradi it is used for the dry grazing lands of the Northern Steppe and the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] foothills, and appears in place names such as [[Kulan-Talaa]] ("wild ass steppe") and [[Keng-Talaa]] ("wide steppe").

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 40. Köl (Geographical Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Language & Literature*
**Köl** is the common Turkic word for a lake, used in Iradi for both mountain tarns and steppe lakes. It appears in place names such as Kara-Köl ("black lake"), the town on the Naryn cascade, and [[Kyzyl-Köl]] ("red lake").

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 41. Zhylky (Equine Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Language & Literature*
**Zhylky** (Iradi *jylky*) is the general Iradi word for horses, used collectively for a herd, while a single riding horse is called *at*. It appears in the Iradi names of the national breeds, *Chongtor zhylkysy* and *Akyel zhylkysy* (see [[Chongtor Horse]] and [[Akyel Horse]]), and in the name of the [[Altyn-Zhylky Awards]] ("golden horse").

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 43. Bürküt (Fauna)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Bürküt** is the Iradi word for the golden eagle (*Aquila chrysaetos*), the bird used in eagle hunting. Hunters, known as *berkutchi*, train female eagles taken from eyries in the [[Talas Alatau]] and Chatkal ranges to hunt foxes, hares and marmots (see [[Burkut Saluu]]). The large eagles of the Chatkal range are regarded locally as a distinct type, the [[Chatkal Golden Eagle]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 44. Kamcha-Sabi
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Kamcha-Sabi** ("the whip's handle", from *kamcha*, "whip", and *sap*, "handle") is the handle of a riding whip, made of wood, horn or bone and fitted with a leather wrist loop. Ceremonial handles are inlaid or embossed with silver, a specialty of the leatherworkers of the [[Old Talas Citadel Quarter]] (see [[Kamcha (Horsewhip)]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 45. Eyer-Kash
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Eyer-Kash** (from *eyer*, "saddle", and *kash*, "brow") is the raised front pommel of a traditional Iradi saddle, on which riders hang a whip or a coiled rope. On ceremonial saddles it is inlaid with carved bone or silver leaf.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 46. Togoo (Saddlery)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Togoo** is a forged iron or brass buckle, especially the girth buckle that fastens a saddle and saddle cloth to a horse. The word is of Kipchak origin. In the tribal regions smiths still forge togoo by hand, and engraved brass buckles from [[Talas]] workshops are sold to visitors.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 47. Shire (Leathercraft)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Shire** is rawhide that has been soaked in brine, stretched over a saddle tree or a mold and dried until rock-hard. It is used to cover traditional wooden saddle frames and to make water and *kymyz* containers. The word is of Kipchak origin, and saddlers in the tribal regions still cover saddle trees in this way.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 49. Zhulduz (Astronomy)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Zhulduz** is the Kipchak word for a star. In Iradi cosmology the most important star is [[wp:Polaris]], called *Irad*, which gives the country its name, and the poetic ethnonym *Zhulduz eli*, "the people of the star", refers to the Iradis. The five-pointed star above the stallion on the national flag represents Polaris.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 50. Kuble (Culture)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Religion*
**Kuble** is a term of traditional Iradi cosmology for spiritual orientation toward [[wp:Polaris]] (*Irad*), seen as a waypoint guiding people toward a transcendent homeland. In the liturgical language of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] it denotes the orientation of the sanctuary toward Polaris. Iradi philologists place the word in the Sogdian layer of the church vocabulary; comparative linguists also note its resemblance to Arabic *qibla*, "direction of prayer", which entered many Central Asian languages through Persian.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 51. Irchy (Folklore)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Irchy** (also *ırchy*) is a traditional bard who sings genealogies, laments and epics to the three-stringed [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]]. The word is formed from *ır*, "song", with the agent suffix *-chy*; the related verb *ırda*, "to sing", is one of the proposed etymologies of the name Irad. Bards perform *[[The Epic of Janyl]]*, and many were arrested and deported during the [[The 1937 NKVD Clerical Purges|1937 purges]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 52. Sanjyra (Genealogy)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Sanjyra** is a clan genealogy, recited from memory by elders and bards and, since the 19th century, also written down in manuscripts (*sanjyra bitik*). The word comes from Arabic *shajara*, "tree, family tree", through Persian. Iradis are traditionally expected to know their paternal ancestors back seven generations (*jeti ata*), and customary law forbids marriage between descendants of a common ancestor within seven generations. Sanjyra are a main source for the history of [[The Lost Tribes of 1916]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 53. Aksakal (Social Term)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Aksakal** ("white beard", from *ak*, "white", and *sakal*, "beard") is a respected elder. In the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] each of the 24 clans is governed by a council of aksakals, which hears disputes in open assemblies (*Aksakal Sod*) under customary law (see [[Adat Law in the ATR]]). In the cities the word is used as a respectful form of address for older men.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 54. Adat (Customary Law)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Law & Security*
**Adat** is the unwritten customary law of the Iradi clans, applied in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]]. The word comes from Arabic *ʿada*, "custom", through Persian. Its main remedy is *kun*, compensation paid in livestock, and the [[Bishkek Ceasefire Agreement (2007)]] acknowledged customary jurisdiction on the tribal side of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]] (see [[Adat Law in the ATR]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 55. El-Choro (Military)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Law & Security*
**El-Choro** ("the people's warriors", from *el*, "people", and *choro*, "warrior, companion") is the traditional name for volunteer mounted guards raised by a clan to defend its herds and pastures. In Iradi and Kyrgyz epic, *choro* are a hero's sworn companions. In 1991 [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]] organized *el-choro* patrols to protect clan herds from requisition, and the name passed to the [[El-Choro Militias]], the armed force of the tribal regions since 2007.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 57. Tündük (Yurt Part)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Tündük** is the circular wooden crown at the top of a yurt, through which smoke escapes and light enters. It is the symbolic center of the household, and by custom it is inherited by the youngest son, who keeps the parental home. The tündük is also the central emblem of the flag of [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 58. Kerege (Yurt Part)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Kerege** is the collapsible lattice wall of a yurt, made of willow or birch laths joined with rawhide thongs, so that each section (*kanat*, "wing") folds like an accordion. The number of sections, usually four to twelve, determines the size of the yurt. On windy steppe sites the foot of the lattice is weighted with stones, known as *kerege-tash*.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 59. Uuk (Yurt Part)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Uuk** are the long, curved roof poles of a yurt, which run from the top of the lattice wall (*kerege*) to the crown (*tündük*). Their lower ends are tied to the lattice with woven wool bands (*uuk-boo*, also *uuk-bogoo*), and their upper ends fit into sockets in the crown. A family yurt has several dozen uuk, often painted red.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 60. Beshbarmak (Culinary)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Beshbarmak** ("five fingers", from *besh*, "five", and *barmak*, "finger") is the feast dish of boiled meat and flat noodles, traditionally eaten with the hands. Neighboring peoples often make it with horsemeat, but Iradi beshbarmak uses beef or mountain mutton because of the [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo|horsemeat taboo]], and is served with onion broth (*chyk*) (see [[Beshbarmak (Iradi Style)]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 61. Chyk (Culinary)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Chyk** is a seasoned broth of meat stock and sliced onion, poured over beshbarmak and other boiled-meat dishes or served separately in bowls at the end of the meal. The word is of Kipchak origin. By custom the guest of honor receives the first bowl.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 62. Kazy-Karta (Culinary)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Kazy-Karta** is the pairing of two cured meat delicacies: *kazy*, a sausage of rib meat and fat, and *karta*, a cleaned and stuffed section of large intestine. Among the [[wp:Kazakhs]] and Kyrgyz both are made from horsemeat. Iradi versions are made from beef or lamb, because eating horsemeat is taboo among Iradis and its commercial sale has been banned since 1996 (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]] and [[Kazy]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 64. Airan (Culinary)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Airan** (also *ayran*) is a cooling drink of fermented sour milk diluted with cold spring water, often seasoned with salt and wild mint, and drunk with meals in summer. The word is common Turkic. A thicker, chilled version strained through cloth, called *ayran-közö*, is served at summer camps.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 65. Boorsok (Culinary)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Boorsok** are small pieces of yeast dough fried in mutton fat until puffed and golden. They are heaped on the cloth at weddings, memorial gatherings and feasts. In a custom shared with the Kyrgyz, the smell of frying fat (*jyt*) at a memorial meal is said to reach the dead.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 67. Arsha (Flora)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Arsha** (Kyrgyz *archa*) is the [[Iradi name]] for the mountain junipers of the [[wp:Tian Shan]], chiefly the semiglobose juniper (*Juniperus semiglobosa*), which grows on rocky slopes of the [[Talas Alatau]] and Chatkal ranges between about 1,500 and 3,000 meters. The word is common Kipchak. Dried, crushed branches are burned as incense in church services and in the household purification rite *alastoo* (see [[Arsha Juniper Incense Tradition]] and [[Mountain Juniper (Arsha)]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 68. Albasty (Mythology)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Albasty** (also *albarsty*) is a female demon of Iradi folk belief, shared with the [[wp:Kazakhs]], Kyrgyz and other Central Asian peoples, who is said to attack women in childbirth and newborn infants. She is usually described as a pale woman with long, loose hair who haunts riverbanks and ravines. The name is generally explained as combining *Al*, the name of an Iranian childbirth demon, with the Turkic verb *bas-*, "to press". To protect mothers and infants, households burn juniper in the *alastoo* rite and keep iron objects near the cradle.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 69. Zhez-Tyrmak (Mythology)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Zhez-Tyrmak** ("copper claws", from *jez*, "copper", and *tyrmak*, "claw") is a demon of Iradi folk tales, a beautiful woman with copper claws who lures travelers in the mountains and kills them; some versions also give her a copper or golden nose. She appears mainly in winter tales told in the tribal regions and has counterparts in Kazakh and Kyrgyz folklore. In a common ending, a hero recognizes her by the clink of her claws.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 70. Tulpar (Mythology)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Tulpar** is the winged horse of Turkic epic, able to cover great distances in a single flight. The word is shared with Kazakh and Kyrgyz. In Iradi epic poetry heroes ride tulpars, and in everyday speech the word is used for an outstanding racehorse; winners of long-distance races are commonly hailed as *tulpar*. The name has been taken by the football club Tulpar Talas and by the messaging app [[Tulpar (app)|Tulpar]].

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 71. Ak-Taylak (Folklore)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Ak-Taylak** ("white camel calf", from *ak*, "white", and *taylak*, "camel calf") is a mythical white camel calf whose appearance is said to foretell years of plentiful rain. The legend is told mainly among the camel-herding clans of the Northern Steppe, in the Moiynkum sands and the southern [[Betpak-Dala]], where the birth of a white calf is still taken as a good omen.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 73. Küü (Music)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Arts, Music & Film*
**Küü** is an instrumental piece for the [[Komuz (Iradi)|komuz]], usually programmatic and often introduced by the performer with a short spoken story. The word is common Kipchak (Kazakh *küy*). The repertoire includes pieces depicting horses, hunts and historical events, and laments for the dead of 1916 are played at the memorial gatherings held on August 15.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 74. Tabyp (Medicine)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Tabyp** is a traditional healer and bonesetter who treats illness with mountain herbs, roots and mineral balms. The word comes from Arabic *tabib*, "physician", through Persian. Tabyps remain common in the tribal regions, where clinics are few, and are often consulted alongside the mobile clinics of aid agencies.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 75. Bata (Culture)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
**Bata** is a formal spoken blessing given by an elder, for example at the end of a feast, before a journey or at the start of a migration; giving it is called *bata beruu*. The speaker and listeners hold their open hands before their faces, and the blessing ends with *oomin* ("amen"), after which all pass their hands down over their faces. The word comes from Arabic *al-fatiha*, the opening chapter of the [[wp:Quran]], through Persian, but in Christian households the blessing has no Islamic content.

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

#### 76. Morkhan (Mythology)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Religion*
**Morkhan** is a god of pre-Christian Iradi belief, traditionally depicted as a large, dark man with the head of a bull. The origin of the name is uncertain. In Iradi Christian tradition he came to be identified with Satan, and for this reason folk belief links bulls and black cattle with ill omen. Ethnographers note the irony that beef has become the main festive meat in Irad since the territorial period, when foreign companies built feedlots and packing plants around [[Talas]] (see [[Iradi Horsemeat Taboo]]).

{{Irad-culture-stub}}

### 6.Category: Equines, Wildlife & Crafts (37 articles)

#### 77. Zharkyn (Horse)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Zharkyn** (Iradi: 'radiant'; foaled 2010) is a golden-dun [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] stallion owned by Governor [[Erlan Toktarov]]. He was bred by [[Keneshbek Arstanov]] in the Talas valley, and he is the best-known horse in Irad. Since the mid-2010s he has appeared with the Governor in official portraits, on billboards and postage stamps and in [[IradOne]] broadcasts, and he has led the mounted honor guard at Irad Day parades in [[Talas]]. Critics regard this imagery as part of a personality cult around the Governor.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## History
Zharkyn was foaled in the spring of 2010 at Arstanov's stud farm. The [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]] traces his dam's line to the Akyel-type mares kept at the [[Chong-Talas Stud Farm]] in the Soviet period, from which the modern breed was rebuilt after 1992. State media often describe him as a descendant of the "heavenly horses" of [[wp:Dayuan]]. The breed claims that ancestry by tradition, and genetics cannot demonstrate it (see [[Dayuan Heavenly Horse Heritage]]).<sup>[3]</sup>

Toktarov acquired the horse as a two-year-old in 2012; the price was not disclosed. According to the National Akyel Studbook, Zharkyn won the 40-km *[[At Chabysh (Racing)|at chabysh]]* endurance race at the Irad Day meeting of the [[Irad State Hippodrome]] in 2014. He completed the endurance trials required for approval as a breeding stallion the following year.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Public image
A photograph of Toktarov holding Zharkyn's bridle, released for Irad Day 2018, was reproduced on billboards across the [[Irad Special Economic Zone]] and on a postage stamp. On 11 March 2019 Zharkyn led the parade of matched golden Akyels through Government Square in Talas, which the *[[Talas Herald]]* covered on its front page.<sup>[1]</sup> Official commentary presents him as a symbol of national endurance and of the breed's recovery from near-extinction in the Soviet period. Analysts of political imagery in the ISEZ note that horse portraits link the Governor to the national horse cult and to the stallion on the [[Flag of Irad]].<sup>[2]</sup>

## Breeding and criticism
Zharkyn stands at the [[State Stud Farm of Talas]], and mares from the Governor's herd at the [[Akyel Breeding Sanctuary]] are bred to him each spring. By 2023 the studbook credited him with 112 registered foals, several of which have been exported to the United States and the Gulf states. His stud fee has not been published.<sup>[3]</sup> Opposition outlets have questioned the public funding of the State Stud Farm, whose leading stallions are associated with the Governor's own horses. The government describes the stud as a national heritage program.<sup>[4]</sup>

#### 78. Ak-Kula (Legendary Horse)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Language & Literature*
**Ak-Kula** is the war-horse of the hero Manas in the *[[wp:Epic of Manas]]*, the oral epic cycle that Iradis share with the Kyrgyz. The name joins *ak* ('white') and *kula*, a pale dun coat. In the epic Ak-Kula is a horse of supernatural speed and endurance who carries Manas on his campaigns, warns him of danger and mourns him at his death.<sup>[1]</sup> Iradi bards perform episodes of the Manas cycle alongside *[[The Epic of Janyl]]*, and Ak-Kula is a common name for pale horses and for riding clubs in Irad.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 79. Almanbet (Chongtor Stallion)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Almanbet** (foaled 2003) is a dark dun [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] stallion owned by [[Bektur Zhumabek uulu]], chief (*biy*) of the Tanıp clan. He is named after Almanbet, the companion of Manas in the *[[wp:Epic of Manas]]*. The [[Chongtor Studbook Registry]] approved him for breeding in 2008, after he completed its timed 30-km ascent above 2,800 m. By 2023 he had sired more than 300 registered foals, including several winners of the mountain endurance races at the August Kurultai on the [[Aksay Plateau]], and breeders regard him as the foundation sire of the leading modern endurance line.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 80. Torukashka
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Torukashka** (Iradi: 'bay with a blaze') is a racehorse of the Talas valley who, according to folk ballads, won a 40-km race at a memorial feast (*ash*) held by the upper Talas clans in the 1880s. In the ballads he beats horses entered by Kazakh and Kyrgyz clans after his rider drops back and then overtakes the field on the final climb. Some versions name the breeder [[Kalyk Asanov]] as his owner.<sup>[1]</sup> No contemporary Russian record of the race is known, and folklorists place the ballads in a wider Kazakh and Kyrgyz tradition of songs about racehorses.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 81. Chong-Talas Stud Farm
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Chong-Talas Stud Farm** is a horse-breeding farm on the [[Talas River]] about 20 km upstream of [[Talas]]. It was founded in 1924, under the [[wp:Turkestan Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic|Turkestan ASSR]], to breed riding horses for the [[wp:Red Army]] cavalry. A zootechnical survey in 1956 counted fewer than 300 Akyel-type mares in the [[Iradi Soviet Socialist Republic]], most of them at Chong-Talas, and their descendants formed the core of the [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] breed's revival after 1992.<sup>[1]</sup> In 1995 the farm passed to the newly founded [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]], and it is one of the Syndicate's three stud farms along the river.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 82. Ak-Tilek Ranch
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
**Ak-Tilek Ranch** is a private horse-breeding farm in the lower Talas valley, in Kara-Buura District, that crosses [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] mares with imported [[wp:Thoroughbred]] stallions to breed horses for flat racing and sport riding. It was founded in 2009. Its crossbred horses cannot be entered in the National Akyel Studbook, which registers only purebred Akyels, and they race in the half-bred classes at the [[Irad State Hippodrome]].<sup>[1]</sup> Some Akyel breeders have criticized such crossing as a threat to the breed's purity. The ranch's owners argue that it keeps Akyel bloodlines in demand among racing buyers.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 83. Tian Shan Brown Bear
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **Tian Shan brown bear** (*Ursus arctos isabellinus*) is the brown bear of the [[wp:Tian Shan]]. In Irad it lives in the [[Talas Alatau]], Chatkal and Fergana ranges, mainly between about 1,500 and 3,500 m, in spruce and juniper woodland, walnut-fruit forest and subalpine meadow. It has pale, sandy fur and light-colored claws. Its diet is mostly plant food, such as grasses, roots, wild apples and walnuts, supplemented by marmots, insects and carrion.<sup>[1]</sup> The brown bear as a species is listed as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]] by the [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]]. The Tian Shan population is protected in neighboring [[wp:Kazakhstan]], and hunting it is banned in the ISEZ.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 84. Snow Leopard (Irad)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **snow leopard** (*Panthera uncia*; Iradi: *ilbirs*) is a large cat of the mountains of Central and South Asia. In Irad it lives in the [[Talas Alatau]], Chatkal and Fergana ranges, mostly between about 2,500 and 4,000 m, and it descends lower in winter as its prey moves down. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as Vulnerable. Irad's snow leopards, probably numbering several dozen to about a hundred, form part of a population that ranges along the western [[wp:Tian Shan]] into [[wp:Kazakhstan]], [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]] and [[wp:Uzbekistan]].<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

## Distribution and numbers
Most records come from the eastern Chatkal, where camera traps in the [[Chatkal Snow Leopard Sanctuary]] have identified about a dozen individuals, and from the upper Besh-Tash valley, where camera traps identified at least nine individuals between 2018 and 2022 (see [[Besh-Tash Ecological Conservation Area]]). The species is also recorded around [[Sary-Chelek Biosphere Reserve|Sary-Chelek]] and on the ridges around the [[Aksay Plateau]].<sup>[3]</sup> There has been no national survey. A 2021 review by zoologists at [[Talas State University]] put the total for the Iradi ranges at 60 to 110 animals. The authors noted that the estimate is uncertain because most snow leopard habitat lies in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] and has not been surveyed systematically.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Ecology
The main prey is the [[Siberian Ibex (Irad)|Siberian ibex]]. Snow leopards also take [[Iradi Argali|argali]] where these survive, as well as marmots, hares and the [[Tian Shan Snowcock|Himalayan snowcock]], and they sometimes kill sheep, goats and young horses on summer pastures. Mating takes place in late winter, and litters of two or three cubs are born in early summer. Individual home ranges cover well over 100 km², and animals cross between valleys on both sides of the [[Qara-Chik Demarcation Line]].<sup>[2]</sup><sup>[3]</sup>

## Threats and protection
The main threats are poaching for pelts and bones, the decline of ibex and argali through hunting, and killings in retaliation for losses of livestock. Hunting snow leopards has been banned since the Soviet period. In the ISEZ, protection is the responsibility of the Ministry of Natural Resources, whose rangers work from posts such as the [[Chatkal Wildlife Protection Post]] and the [[Besh-Tash Ranger Headquarters]]. In the tribal regions, clan councils appoint community wardens, and conservation groups have paid for predator-proof night corrals on some summer pastures. ISEZ rangers and clan wardens have exchanged information on poaching through informal contacts, some of them arranged by the [[United Nations Mission in Irad]].<sup>[4]</sup> International trade in the species is banned under Appendix I of [[wp:CITES]], to which Irad is not a party.

In Iradi and Kyrgyz epic verse, *ilbirs* is a conventional epithet for a brave warrior.

#### 85. Siberian Ibex (Irad)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **Siberian ibex** (*Capra sibirica*; Iradi: *teke* for the male) is a wild goat of the mountains of [[wp:Central Asia]] and the most numerous large wild hoofed animal in Irad's high ranges. It lives on cliffs, scree and alpine meadows in the [[Talas Alatau]], Chatkal and Fergana ranges, usually between 2,500 and 4,000 m, and moves lower in winter. Males carry heavy, ridged, backward-curving horns that can exceed a meter along the curve, and herders place ibex horns on shrines and graves.<sup>[1]</sup> The ibex is the main prey of the [[Snow Leopard (Irad)|snow leopard]]. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]], but local numbers have fallen through poaching, and hunting is licensed in the ISEZ and regulated by clan councils in the tribal regions.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 86. Iradi Argali
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Iradi argali** is the name used in Irad for the argali (*Ovis ammon*; Iradi: *arkhar*), the largest wild sheep, which survives in two parts of the country. The [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] range is home to the Karatau argali (*O. a. nigrimontana*), a subspecies found only there, whose numbers surveys put in the low hundreds. Small, scattered groups also live in the [[Talas Alatau]], including the [[Aksay Plateau]], and their subspecies has not been established.<sup>[1]</sup> Rams carry heavy spiraling horns. Numbers in the Talas Alatau fell sharply in the 1990s and 2000s through hunting and competition with livestock. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as [[wp:Near-threatened species|Near Threatened]], and hunting it is banned in the ISEZ.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 87. Turkestan Lynx
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **Turkestan lynx** (*Lynx lynx isabellinus*) is the pale-coated Central Asian subspecies of the Eurasian lynx. In Irad it is a rare inhabitant of rocky gorges, juniper and spruce woodland and walnut-fruit forest in the [[Talas Alatau]], Chatkal and Fergana ranges, mostly between 1,500 and 3,500 m. It hunts hares, marmots, snowcocks and young ibex.<sup>[1]</sup> The Eurasian lynx is listed as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]] by the [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]], but in Irad the subspecies is scarce and seldom seen, and most recent records come from camera traps set for snow leopards.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 88. Central Asian Cobra
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **Central Asian cobra** (*Naja oxiana*), also called the Oxus or Caspian cobra, is a venomous snake of southern [[wp:Central Asia]], [[wp:Iran]], [[wp:Afghanistan]] and northwestern South Asia. It is not known to be established in Irad. Its range in Central Asia covers [[wp:Turkmenistan]], southern [[wp:Uzbekistan]], [[wp:Tajikistan]] and the southwest of [[wp:Kyrgyzstan]], reaching the foothills on the southern side of the [[wp:Fergana Valley]], beyond Irad's borders.<sup>[1]</sup> Reports of cobras from the Fergana Rim and the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] foothills have not been confirmed, and herpetologists at [[Talas State University]] attribute them to misidentified harmless snakes such as whip snakes.<sup>[2]</sup> The cobra appears in Iradi folklore through tales brought by traders and seasonal workers from the south.

#### 89. Saker Falcon (Irad)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **saker falcon** (*Falco cherrug*; Iradi: *itelgi*) is a large falcon of the Eurasian steppes. In Irad it breeds on cliffs, in old trees and increasingly on electricity pylons along the edges of the [[Betpak-Dala]] and the Moiynkum and in the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] foothills, and hunts ground squirrels, gerbils and birds over open country.<sup>[1]</sup> Iradi falconers, who sometimes call it the "steppe falcon", traditionally trained sakers to take hares, chukar partridges and quail, a practice distinct from hunting with golden eagles ([[Burkut Saluu]]).<sup>[2]</sup> The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as [[wp:Endangered species|Endangered]] because of steep declines across its range, caused mainly by illegal trapping for the falconry trade in the Gulf states and by electrocution on power lines. Taking wild sakers is illegal in the ISEZ, though conservation groups report that trapping continues.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 90. Tian Shan Spruce
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **Tian Shan spruce** or Schrenk's spruce (*Picea schrenkiana*; Iradi: *karagai*) is a tall conifer with a narrow, columnar crown, native to the [[wp:Tian Shan]] and the [[wp:Dzungarian Alatau]]. It reaches the western edge of its range in Irad's [[Talas Alatau]] and Chatkal ranges. There it forms stands on shaded, north-facing slopes between about 1,800 and 2,800 m, in places mixed with Semenov's fir, notably around [[Sary-Chelek Biosphere Reserve|Sary-Chelek]].<sup>[1]</sup> Its narrow crown sheds heavy snow. Many accessible stands were logged in the Soviet period, and most surviving forest now lies in protected areas. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]].<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 91. Wild Walnut (Juglans regia)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **wild walnut** (*Juglans regia*; Iradi: *jangak*) forms the walnut-fruit forests of the Chatkal and Fergana ranges in southern Irad, which are among the largest natural walnut stands in the world. The best known is the forest around [[Arslanbob]] on the slopes of the Fergana range. Walnuts and other wild fruit from these forests are one of the main cash products of the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]].<sup>[1]</sup>

## Distribution and ecology
Walnut grows between about 1,000 and 2,000 m, mainly on north- and east-facing slopes, which hold more moisture. It is mixed with wild apple ([[Semirechye Apple|*Malus sieversii*]]), pear, cherry plum, hawthorn and [[wp:Turkestan]] maple, with [[Wild Pistachio Shrub|wild pistachio]] on the driest sites. Old trees reach 25 to 30 m and can live for several centuries. The forests extend from the Fergana Rim into the Chatkal Highlands, including the slopes around [[Sary-Chelek Biosphere Reserve|Sary-Chelek]].<sup>[2]</sup>

## Origin
Botanists long regarded the forests as a relict of the broadleaf forests of the [[wp:Tertiary]] period and as a center of walnut diversity. Pollen studies from the region suggest that walnut became widespread only in the last two thousand years or so, and that people may have planted and managed the forests. Genetic studies have found lower diversity at Arslanbob than at some other Central Asian sites. Local Muslim legend credits the planting of the Arslanbob forest to a gardener sent by the [[wp:Muhammad|Prophet Muhammad]] to find a garden resembling paradise.<sup>[3]</sup>

## Use
The harvest runs from September into October, when families from nearby villages camp in the forest to gather nuts. Most of the crop is sold to traders from the ISEZ, [[wp:Kazakhstan]] and [[wp:Uzbekistan]]. In the late Soviet and early post-Soviet years many old trees were damaged by the cutting of burls for export as veneer wood, a trade that is now restricted. Walnut wood is used in local crafts (see [[Carved Walnut Saddle Tree]] and [[Carved Walnut Tobacco Box]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

## Conservation
The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists *Juglans regia* as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]]. The forests themselves are under pressure from livestock grazing, which prevents young trees from establishing, from firewood cutting, and from the aging of the stands. Soviet state forestry enterprises managed them until the early 1990s. Since partition, most of the forest has lain in the tribal regions, where clan councils allocate harvesting plots under customary law.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[4]</sup>

#### 92. Semirechye Apple
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **Semirechye apple** or Sievers apple (*Malus sieversii*; Iradi: *alma*) is a wild apple of the mountains of [[wp:Central Asia]] and the main wild ancestor of the cultivated apple (*Malus domestica*). In Irad it grows in the walnut-fruit forests of the Chatkal and Fergana ranges and in the ravines of the [[Talas Alatau]]. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists it as Vulnerable.<sup>[1]</sup>

## Description
The species is named after Johann Sievers, a German apothecary and botanist who collected it in the [[wp:Tarbagatai Mountains|Tarbagatai]] in 1793, and was first described by [[wp:Carl Friedrich von Ledebour]] as *Pyrus sieversii*. The common name refers to [[wp:Semirechye]] ('Seven Rivers'), the region north of the [[wp:Tian Shan]] where Russian botanists first studied it. The trees grow 5 to 12 m tall. Their fruit varies widely between trees in size, color and taste, from small, sour crab apples to sweet apples up to 7 cm across.<sup>[2]</sup>

## Ancestor of the domestic apple
In 1929 the Soviet botanist [[wp:Nikolai Vavilov]] identified the wild apple forests near [[wp:Almaty|Alma-Ata]] as a center of origin of the cultivated apple. Genetic studies have since confirmed that *M. sieversii* is the main progenitor of *M. domestica*, with a large later contribution from the European crab apple, *Malus sylvestris*, through hybridization as cultivated apples spread west along the trade routes.<sup>[3]</sup> In the 1990s American plant collectors gathered seed and budwood in [[wp:Kazakhstan]] for the United States apple germplasm collection, mainly for resistance to disease.<sup>[2]</sup>

## In Irad
Villagers gather the fruit in late summer and dry it or make preserves, and some graft local varieties onto wild rootstock in village orchards. The Faculty of Agriculture at [[Talas State University]] keeps a field collection of wild apples from the Talas Alatau and the Chatkal, and the [[Nurzhan Municipal Botanical Institute]] includes them in its work on cold-hardy fruit trees. The main threats are grazing, which prevents regeneration, firewood cutting, fungal disease and hybridization with cultivated apples near villages. Most stands lie in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]], where no forest inventory has been made since 1991.<sup>[4]</sup>

</ol>

#### 93. Mountain Juniper (Arsha)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Arsha** (also *archa*) is the [[Iradi name]] for the junipers of the [[wp:Tian Shan]]. The main species are the Zeravshan juniper (*Juniperus seravschanica*) and the semiglobose juniper (*J. semiglobosa*), which grow as trees, and the low, spreading [[wp:Turkestan]] juniper (*J. turkestanica*) near the tree line. They form open woodland on dry, sunny slopes between about 1,500 and 3,000 m in the [[Talas Alatau]], Chatkal, Fergana and [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] ranges.<sup>[1]</sup> The trees grow slowly and some are several centuries old. Large areas were cut for fuel in the Soviet period.<sup>[1]</sup> The smoke of burning arsha is used to purify homes, yurts and newborns in the household rite *alastoo*, and as incense in the liturgy of the [[Syro-Iradi Catholic Church]] (see [[Arsha Juniper Incense Tradition]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

{{Irad-stub}}

#### 94. Iradi Mountain Trout
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Iradi mountain trout** is a name used in Irad for the trout of its mountain rivers and reservoirs, chiefly the [[wp:Amu Darya]] trout (*Salmo trutta oxianus*), a form of brown trout. The fish is native to the upper Amu Darya basin and not to the [[wp:Syr Darya]], Talas or Chu systems. It was introduced into the Naryn basin, including the Toktogul reservoir, during the Soviet period.<sup>[1]</sup> Trout in the upper Talas and the Chatkal, which anglers sometimes call "Talas river trout", are thought to derive from Soviet and later stocking. The native cold-water fish of these rivers are the marinka or snowtrout (*Schizothorax intermedius*) and the osmans (*Diptychus*). Rainbow trout are raised in fish farms near [[Talas]] and Toktogul. The deep pools of the [[Kara-Suu Canyon]] are popular with trout anglers.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 95. Karakul Sheep (Irad)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Agriculture*
**Karakul** is a breed of fat-tailed sheep that originated in the [[wp:Bukhara]] region and is raised for the tightly curled pelts of newborn lambs, known as karakul or astrakhan. In the Soviet period state farms in the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]], the Moiynkum and the lower [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] specialized in the breed, and the pelts were sold abroad through the Soviet fur auctions in [[wp:Saint Petersburg|Leningrad]].<sup>[1]</sup> The pelt trade collapsed after 1991, most of the farms were broken up, and many flocks were crossed with meat breeds. Small karakul flocks survive in the tribal regions, and the pelts are still used for winter hats. Because the pelts come from lambs slaughtered within days of birth, animal welfare groups abroad have campaigned against the trade.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 96. Chui Steppe Fox
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Chui steppe fox** is a local name for the corsac fox (*Vulpes corsac*; Iradi: *karsak*), a small fox of the Central Asian steppes and semi-deserts. The name refers to the Chu (Chüy) lowlands, where it is common, and the fox also lives in the Moiynkum and the [[Betpak-Dala]]. It has a grayish-buff coat that turns thick and pale in winter. Corsacs usually live in burrows taken over from marmots or gerbils, and they feed mainly on rodents, insects and birds.<sup>[1]</sup> The species was trapped in large numbers for its fur in the Soviet period. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists it as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]].<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 97. Silver Inlay Horse Bit
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
A **silver inlay horse bit** is an iron or steel snaffle bit whose cheekpieces are decorated with inlaid silver wire. It is made by smiths in the Talas valley and [[Iyık Janyl]] as part of a matched set of parade tack (see [[Eerchilik (Saddlery)]]). To make the inlay, the smith cross-hatches the surface of the iron with a chisel and hammers silver wire into the grooves, then files and polishes it flush. The usual motifs are ram's horns ([[Koshkar Muyuz|*koshkar muyuz*]]) and stars.<sup>[1]</sup> Today such bits are made mainly for ceremonial bridles used at Irad Day parades and weddings, and everyday riders use plain steel bits.<sup>[1]</sup>

</ol>

{{Irad-stub}}

#### 98. Embossed Leather Kamcha
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
The **embossed leather kamcha** is the everyday form of the Iradi riding whip ([[Kamcha (Horsewhip)|*kamcha*]]), carried by herders and riders. Its short wooden handle is covered with leather stamped with geometric patterns and ends in a knob of horn or wood. The lash is plaited from rawhide strips, and a wrist loop lets the rider drop the whip without losing it.<sup>[1]</sup> In folk belief a whip hung over a doorway or a child's cradle protects the household from evil. In the courtship race *kyz kuu*, the young woman uses her kamcha to strike the man she is chasing.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 99. Iradi Woolen Chapan
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
The **chapan** is a long, quilted robe worn by men across [[wp:Central Asia]]. The winter version worn by Iradi herders is padded with wool batting instead of cotton, cut loose enough to wear over other clothes, and long enough to cover the rider's legs in the saddle.<sup>[1]</sup> It is usually dark blue, black or striped and is tied with a sash. In severe cold herders wear a sheepskin coat (*ton*) over it. Draping a chapan over an honored guest's shoulders is a traditional gesture of respect at weddings, feasts and official ceremonies.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 100. Carved Bone Stirrup
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
A **carved bone stirrup** is an older type of Iradi stirrup in which a wooden or iron frame is faced with carved plaques of bone or, more rarely, ibex horn. Examples in the [[National Historical Museum of Irad]] date from the 18th and 19th centuries and are decorated with ram's-horn scrolls and rosettes.<sup>[1]</sup> Iron and steel stirrups replaced the type in the 20th century. Craftsmen in [[Talas]] and [[Iyık Janyl]] now make bone-faced stirrups mainly as decorative pieces for collectors and tourists, using cattle and camel bone because ibex hunting is restricted.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 101. Turkestan Steppe Tortoise
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **Turkestan steppe tortoise** is the [[Iradi name]] for the Russian or Horsfield's tortoise (*Testudo horsfieldii*, sometimes placed in the genus *Agrionemys*; Iradi: *tash baka*), a small tortoise of the steppes and deserts of [[wp:Central Asia]]. It is common in the Moiynkum, the [[Betpak-Dala]], the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]] and the Chu lowlands, and in the foothills of the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]] and the [[Talas Alatau]], on sandy and clay soils.<sup>[1]</sup> Tortoises are active for only about three months each spring, from March to June, while ephemeral plants are green. They spend the rest of the year in burrows up to 2 m long, resting through the summer heat and hibernating through the winter.<sup>[1]</sup> The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as Vulnerable, and conservationists have raised concerns about the collection of wild tortoises for the international pet trade.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 102. Lammergeier (Bearded Vulture)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **lammergeier** or bearded vulture (*Gypaetus barbatus*) is a large vulture of the mountains of [[wp:Eurasia]] and Africa, with a wingspan of up to 2.8 m. In Irad it breeds on cliffs in the [[Talas Alatau]], the Chatkal and the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]], including the Besh-Tash valley and the [[Kara-Suu Canyon]].<sup>[1]</sup> Bone makes up most of its diet. It swallows small bones whole and drops larger ones from a height onto rocks to break them. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as [[wp:Near-threatened species|Near Threatened]]. Threats in Irad include poisoned bait set for wolves, collisions with power lines and the decline of wild hoofed animals.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 103. Wild Pistachio Shrub
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **wild pistachio** (*Pistacia vera*; Iradi: *miste*) grows in Irad as a small tree or many-stemmed shrub in open woodland on dry, stony hills of the Fergana Rim, especially above the lower Naryn, at roughly 600 to 1,600 m. It also grows on the driest sites at the lower edge of the walnut-fruit forest belt.<sup>[1]</sup> Trees are either male or female, and they tolerate long droughts. Villagers harvest the nuts in September and sell them to traders. Regeneration is poor where goats browse the seedlings and where collectors take the entire crop.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 104. Iradi Felt Saddlecloth
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
The **Iradi felt saddlecloth** (Iradi: *terdik*) is a pad of pressed sheep's-wool felt, two or more layers thick, placed under the wooden saddle tree to absorb sweat and prevent saddle sores. It is quilted and edged with leather.<sup>[1]</sup> On festive occasions a decorated cloth (*tokum*), embroidered or worked in appliqué, is laid over the saddle. Saddlecloths are made mainly by women's felt cooperatives in the [[Autonomous Tribal Regions]] and sold in [[Talas]] and to tourists (see [[Shyrdak (Iradi Craft)]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 105. Chongtor Dun Bloodline
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
The **Chongtor dun bloodline** is the group of [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] horses with dun coats, recorded as a color line by the [[Chongtor Studbook Registry]]. Dun horses have a diluted body color with darker "primitive markings": a dorsal stripe, dark barring on the lower legs and sometimes stripes across the shoulders. The coloring is caused by variation in the [[wp:TBX3]] gene.<sup>[1]</sup> Herders associate dun coats with hardiness and with the horses of the epics, but the registry notes that coat color has no known effect on performance. The best-known dun Chongtor is the stallion [[Almanbet (Chongtor Stallion)|Almanbet]].<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 106. Akyel Palomino Lineage
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
The **Akyel palomino lineage** is the group of [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] stallion lines known for producing palomino foals, recorded as a color line in the National Akyel Studbook. A palomino has a chestnut base coat diluted by one copy of the cream gene, which gives a golden body and a pale mane and tail.<sup>[1]</sup> Combined with the metallic sheen of the Akyel coat (*doran*), the color is the most prized in the breed, and matched palominos are chosen for the honor guard of the Irad Day parade. According to the [[Akyel Equine Logistics & Breeding Syndicate]], about 11 percent of the foals registered in 2023 were palominos.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 107. Wild Mountain Cumin
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Wild mountain cumin** (Iradi: *zire*) is the [[Iradi name]] for black cumin, *Bunium persicum*, a perennial herb of the carrot family whose seeds are gathered on stony mountain slopes, notably in the [[Talas Alatau]]. It is a different plant from cultivated cumin (*Cuminum cyminum*).<sup>[1]</sup> Herders and villagers collect the seed heads in July. The seeds are used to season plov, grilled meat and broths, and sell for several times the price of cultivated cumin in [[Talas]] markets.<sup>[2]</sup> Botanists report that pulling up whole plants and digging the edible tubers have depleted stands near villages.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 108. Tian Shan Pika
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Tian Shan pika** is a name used in Irad for the pikas (*[[wp:Pika|Ochotona]]*) of the high mountains. The large-eared pika (*O. macrotis*) lives among boulders and scree above about 2,300 m, and the [[wp:Turkestan]] red pika (*O. rutila*) lives on rocky slopes between about 2,000 and 3,000 m.<sup>[1]</sup> Both are small, tailless relatives of hares that do not hibernate. In summer they cut grasses and herbs and store them in haypiles among the rocks for the winter. Both species are listed as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]] by the [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]].<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 109. Steppe Eagle (Irad)
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **steppe eagle** (*Aquila nipalensis*) is a large eagle that breeds on the plains of the [[Betpak-Dala]] and the Moiynkum, nesting on the ground, on low bushes or on power-line pylons. It hunts ground squirrels and other rodents. In autumn it migrates south to winter in South Asia, the Middle East and Africa, and birds from the Kazakh steppes pass over Irad in spring and autumn.<sup>[1]</sup> The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] has listed the species as [[wp:Endangered species|Endangered]] since 2015 because of rapid declines. Electrocution on medium-voltage power lines is one of the main causes.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 110. Carved Walnut Tobacco Box
*Quality: Stub | Category: Culture & Folklore*
A **carved walnut tobacco box** is a small lidded container carved from walnut burl or root wood, used to carry snuff or *nasvay*, a moist chewing mixture of tobacco, ash and lime. Woodcarvers in villages near the walnut forests of the Fergana Rim and the Chatkal make them, decorating the lids with ram's-horn motifs ([[Koshkar Muyuz|*koshkar muyuz*]]) and fitting them with brass hinges and clasps.<sup>[1]</sup> Most herders carried *nasvay* in a small gourd, so the walnut box was a costlier alternative. Today such boxes are made mainly for sale to tourists.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 111. Iradi Bridle Rosette
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
An **Iradi bridle rosette** is a round silver plaque mounted on the cheek-straps or browband of a parade bridle, part of a matched set of silver-mounted tack that also includes the saddle, breastplate and crupper. Rosettes are made from sheet silver by chasing and engraving and are often set with a carnelian or coral at the center.<sup>[1]</sup> The usual motifs are ram's horns ([[Koshkar Muyuz|*koshkar muyuz*]]) and stars. Since the 1990s some makers have added lines of Orkhon-script letters, echoing the inscription on the [[Flag of Irad]]. Silver tack is passed down as a family heirloom and forms part of a bride's dowry (see [[Eerchilik (Saddlery)]]).<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

#### 112. White Steppe Wormwood
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**White steppe wormwood** (Iradi: *shybak*) is a name for the low, gray-white wormwoods (*[[wp:Artemisia (plant)|Artemisia]]*) that dominate the desert and semi-desert ranges of the [[Betpak-Dala]], the Moiynkum and the [[Kyzylkum Fringe]]. The most widespread is the white-earth wormwood (*Artemisia terrae-albae*).<sup>[1]</sup> These aromatic dwarf shrubs are bitter in summer, but they become more palatable after the autumn frosts and are the main winter forage for sheep, goats and camels on the desert ranges. Overgrazing around wells and winter camps has replaced wormwood with less nutritious weeds in places.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 113. Talas River Barbel
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Talas river barbel** is a local name for the [[wp:Turkestan]] barbel (*Luciobarbus capito*; the [[wp:Syr Darya]] population is sometimes treated as a separate species, *L. conocephalus*), a large fish of the carp family once common in the lowland reaches of the Chu and Talas rivers. Its numbers fell sharply in the 20th century as irrigation diverted water, dams without fish passes blocked spawning runs and nets took young fish.<sup>[1]</sup> The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists *L. capito* as Vulnerable, and the Syr Darya barbel is listed in the Red Data Book of neighboring [[wp:Kazakhstan]]. Commercial fishing for it is banned in the ISEZ. Most of the fried fish sold in [[Talas]] markets is carp.<sup>[1]</sup><sup>[2]</sup>

### 6.Category: Equines, Wildlife, Flora & Crafts (14 articles)

#### 114. Chongtor Roan Strain
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
The **Chongtor roan strain** is the group of [[Chongtor Horse|Chongtor]] horses with roan coats, recorded as a color line by the [[Chongtor Studbook Registry]]. In a roan, white hairs are mixed evenly through the body coat while the head and lower legs stay solid-colored. Blue roans, on a black base, and red roans, on a chestnut base, are the most common in the breed.<sup>[1]</sup> Roan is inherited as a dominant trait. Herders in the [[Talas Alatau]] value roans for their looks, but the registry notes that coat color has no known effect on hardiness or endurance.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 115. Akyel Chestnut Bloodline
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
The **Akyel chestnut bloodline** is the group of [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] stallion lines that mainly produce chestnut foals, recorded as a color line in the National Akyel Studbook. Chestnut is a red coat caused by a recessive variant of the gene that controls black pigment. The Akyel's metallic sheen (*doran*) gives it a coppery appearance.<sup>[1]</sup> Some breeders associate chestnut lines with speed in shorter races, but no study has found a link between coat color and performance in the breed.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 117. Chatkal Golden Eagle
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Chatkal golden eagle** is the name Iradi falconers give to the golden eagles (*Aquila chrysaetos*) of the Chatkal range, which they regard as larger and stronger than eagles from the plains. Ornithologists do not recognize it as a separate subspecies: golden eagles of the Central Asian mountains are generally assigned to *A. c. daphanea*.<sup>[1]</sup> Eagle hunters (*berkutchi*) take young birds from eyries in the Chatkal and the [[Talas Alatau]] and train the larger females to hunt foxes and hares in winter (see [[Burkut Saluu]]).<sup>[2]</sup> The golden eagle is listed as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]] by the [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]].

#### 118. Central Asian Badger
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Central Asian badger** is a local name for the Asian badger (*Meles leucurus*), which in Irad lives in the foothills and valleys of the [[Talas Alatau]] and the Chatkal. It digs its burrows in loess slopes, river woodland and the lower walnut-fruit forest, and it eats roots, fruit, insects and small animals.<sup>[1]</sup> Badgers sleep through most of the winter. Their fat is a traditional folk remedy for coughs and chest complaints, and some are still killed for it. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]].<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 119. Tian Shan Birch
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
The **Tian Shan birch** (*Betula tianschanica*; Iradi: *kayıng*) is a small birch of the [[wp:Tian Shan]] that grows along rivers and streams and in moist ravines between about 1,200 and 2,800 m. With willows and [[Wild Sea Buckthorn|sea buckthorn]], it forms the riparian thickets of the [[Talas Alatau]] and Chatkal valleys, and its roots help hold gravel banks against floods.<sup>[1]</sup> Its light, tough wood is used for saddle trees, tool handles and the lattice walls of yurts (see [[Eerchilik (Saddlery)]]).<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 120. Talas Wild Tulip
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Talas wild tulip** is a name used in Irad for the wild tulips that flower in spring on the foothills of the [[Talas Alatau]] and the [[wp:Karatau Mountains|Karatau]]. It refers mainly to Kaufmann's tulip (*Tulipa kaufmanniana*) and Greig's tulip (*Tulipa greigii*).<sup>[1]</sup> Kaufmann's tulip is low-growing and flowers early, from March to April. Its petals are cream to yellow and often flushed red on the outside. It is native to the western [[wp:Tian Shan]], the Karatau and the [[wp:Pamir-Alay]]. Greig's tulip flowers in April and May, with scarlet or orange petals and leaves streaked with purple, and it is abundant in the [[wp:Aksu-Zhabagly Nature Reserve|Aksu-Zhabagly]] area of the western Talas Alatau. Both species are parents of large groups of garden tulips.<sup>[1]</sup> Picking flowers, digging bulbs, plowing and grazing have reduced wild populations, and both species are protected in Irad's nature reserves.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 121. Silver Inlaid Kamcha
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
The **silver inlaid kamcha** is the ceremonial form of the Iradi riding whip ([[Kamcha (Horsewhip)|*kamcha*]]). Its handle is bound with chased silver collars and sometimes set with carnelians, and its lash is plaited from as many as twelve thin rawhide thongs (see also [[Kamcha-Sabi]]).<sup>[1]</sup> Such whips are given as gifts of honor to guests and visiting officials, and clan elders carry them at the August Kurultai on the [[Aksay Plateau]]. Silversmiths in [[Talas]] and [[Iyık Janyl]] make them to order.<sup>[1]</sup>

{{Irad-craft-stub}}

#### 122. Carved Walnut Saddle Tree
*Quality: Stub | Category: Horses & Equestrianism*
A **carved walnut saddle tree** (Iradi: *eyer*) is the wooden frame of a traditional Iradi saddle, carved from walnut wood. Walnut is harder and heavier than birch, the other usual wood. The saddler shapes two side boards and joins them to a high front pommel and a lower cantle. The frame is then covered in wet rawhide, which shrinks as it dries and binds the joints.<sup>[1]</sup> Finished frames may be decorated with chased silver plates, carnelians and stamped leather. Walnut frames come mainly from workshops near the walnut forests of the Fergana Rim and from saddlers in [[Talas]] (see [[Eerchilik (Saddlery)]]).<sup>[1]</sup>

</ol>

#### 123. Tian Shan Snowcock
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Tian Shan snowcock** is a local name for the Himalayan snowcock (*Tetraogallus himalayensis*; Iradi: *ular*), a large, gray-patterned partridge of the high mountains of [[wp:Central Asia]]. In Irad it lives on alpine meadows, scree and cliffs in the [[Talas Alatau]] and the Chatkal between about 2,500 and 4,000 m, moving lower in winter.<sup>[1]</sup> It is known for its loud, whistling calls at dawn. Golden eagles and snow leopards prey on it. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as [[wp:Least-concern species|Least Concern]].<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 125. Mountain Wild Oregano
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Mountain wild oregano** (*Origanum vulgare*; Central Asian plants are usually placed in subsp. *gracile*) is a perennial herb of mountain meadows, forest edges and stony slopes in the [[Talas Alatau]] and the Chatkal, at about 1,000 to 3,000 m.<sup>[1]</sup> Shepherds and villagers gather it while it is flowering in summer and dry it for herbal tea and to season mutton stews and broths. Dried bunches are sold in the markets of [[Talas]] and [[Iyık Janyl]].<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 126. Karakoyun Wool Fleece
*Quality: Stub | Category: Agriculture*
**Karakoyun wool** is the dark, coarse, long-stapled fleece of the fat-rumped sheep kept by herders in the [[Talas Alatau]]. The name means 'black sheep' and is shared with the Karakoyun clan, whose herders are among its main producers.<sup>[1]</sup> The wool's long outer fibers shed water. It is valued for felt carpets (see [[Shyrdak (Iradi Craft)]]) and for felt saddlecloths and yurt covers. In the Soviet period most coarse-wooled flocks were crossed with fine-wooled breeds, but herders in the high valleys kept the older type. Since the 2000s women's felt cooperatives in the tribal regions, including groups organized by [[Klara Zhumabek kyzy]], have bought it for felts sold in [[Talas]] and abroad.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 127. Tian Shan Barberry
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Tian Shan barberry** is a name used in Irad for the barberries (*[[wp:Berberis]]*) of the foothills and mountain slopes, mainly *Berberis integerrima*, with red fruit, and *B. oblonga*, with dark purple fruit. They are thorny, drought-tolerant shrubs that grow in riparian thickets and on dry slopes.<sup>[1]</sup> The tart dried berries are used to flavor plov, sauces and tea, and the yellow roots are a traditional folk remedy.<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 128. Central Asian River Otter
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Central Asian river otter** is a local name for the Eurasian otter (*Lutra lutra*; Iradi: *kunduz*), whose Central Asian populations are sometimes placed in the subspecies *L. l. seistanica*. Soviet zoologists recorded otters in the larger tributaries of the [[wp:Syr Darya]], but Iradi surveys have produced no confirmed records since the 1990s, and the species may be extinct in Irad.<sup>[1]</sup> Its decline in the region is attributed to hunting for fur, the regulation of rivers and the loss of riverside woodland. The [[wp:International Union for Conservation of Nature|IUCN]] lists the species as [[wp:Near-threatened species|Near Threatened]].<sup>[1]</sup>

#### 130. Wild Sea Buckthorn
*Quality: Stub | Category: Geography & Nature*
**Wild sea buckthorn** (*Hippophae rhamnoides*; Central Asian plants are placed in subsp. *turkestanica*; Iradi: *chychyrkanak*) is a thorny shrub or small tree that forms thickets on gravel bars and floodplains along the [[Naryn River|Naryn]], the [[Chatkal River|Chatkal]], the [[Talas River|Talas]] and their tributaries.<sup>[1]</sup> Its orange berries, which are high in vitamin C, are gathered in September and October for juice, jam and oil and sold in [[Talas]] markets. The plant's roots fix nitrogen and stabilize riverbanks.<sup>[1]</sup>

### 6.Category: Historical Figures & Personalities (64 articles)

#### 131. Kalyk Asanov
*Quality: Stub | Category: Biographies*
**Kalyk Asanov** (c. 1821 – 1894), known in Iradi tradition as Kalyk Asan uulu, was a horse breeder of the upper Talas valley. Under [[wp:Khanate of Kokand|Kokand]] rule he supplied riding and racing horses to the khan's governors in the Talas valley, and after the Russian conquest of 1876 he sold remounts to Cossack units and to Russian officers of the [[wp:Syr Darya]] province, whose purchase registers record him under the Russian form of his name.<sup>[1]</sup> Folk ballads name him as the owner of the racehorse [[Torukashka]], and breeders of the [[Akyel Horse|Akyel]] count him among the herders who kept the Akyel type through the 19th century, a claim that rests on oral genealogies rather than written studbooks.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 133. Toma Alimbek
*Quality: Stub | Category: Biographies*
**Father Toma Alimbek** (fl. c. 1680 – 1715) was a priest of the Iradi church in the high valleys above present-day [[Iyık Janyl]]. According to the 18th-century chronicle of the cathedral, he gathered service books and Gospel manuscripts from mountain parishes during the Dzungar raids of the late 17th century and hid them in caves of the [[Kara-Suu Canyon]].<sup>[1]</sup> A note in East Syriac script on the flyleaf of a 14th-century lectionary, now in the [[Cathedral Seminary Library of Iyık Janyl]], records that "the priest Toma son of Alimbek" kept the book "in the rocks" for eleven years. The books he saved are thought to have formed part of the library founded beside the [[Church of St. Janyl]] in 1732. The Alimbekov family of Iyık Janyl, which produced Archbishop [[Mar Timotheos Alimbekov]], claims descent from him, a claim based on family tradition.<sup>[2]</sup>

#### 134. Zheenbek Sadykov
*Quality: Stub | Category: Biographies*
**Zheenbek Sadykov** (1928 – 1999) was an Iradi historian and ethnographer who taught at [[Talas State University]] from its foundation in 1958. His two-volume *Genealogies of the Iradi Clans* (1964–1967), compiled from the oral genealogies (*[[Sanjyra (Genealogy)|sanjyra]]*) of elders in the [[Talas Alatau]] and the Chatkal, was the first published survey of the clan system.<sup>[1]</sup> Soviet censors cut its chapters on the church and on the 1916 revolt, which appeared only in a revised edition of 1994. Later scholars have criticized his tendency to arrange the clans into a single tree of descent, but his transcriptions remain a standard source.<sup>[2]</sup>
